*A mark is derived, never stored.* It lasts while the schema lacks what the statement names and is cleared once the schema holds it again -- the collection made again under its name -- where the proposal said "until redefined": a mark kept in memory and lost at the next open would have made the same file broken or not by the process's history, so every open, apply and schema change finds it again from the schema -- a `create index` built beside the database (`Database::maintain`) too, which lands its schema change outside `commit` (`finish_index`, `finish_composite`): left to the next open, its mark outlived the index it lacked on the primary while a replica applying the same record cleared it (`an_index_built_beside_the_database_clears_the_mark_on_the_primary_as_on_a_replica`); a misread text's mark depends on the text and the grammar alone, and lasts. A build without the feature -- the browser module -- reads the record, keeps it, writes it again in its images and applies it from a replica, its text as it came, and reads no tree, checks no dependency and marks nothing: no such build lists or calls by a mark, and the server that opens a file the module changed finds what each statement lacks. The design asked the module to mark by `depends`; done there, the check was 1.7 KB of the module for a mark nothing reads.

*Definitions run where a test lets them.* `Database::allow_definitions` (`#[doc(hidden)]`) lets `create statement` and `drop statement` run on that database; everywhere else they stay `NOT_BUILT`, and every `call`. So nothing a server or an app runs writes kind 13 -- a statement nobody can call, and the first record an older reader of the file would stop at -- and the upgrade's order (slice 4) is not owed yet. Defined, a name held is 409 without `or replace`; a database holds 1 000 (`MAX_STATEMENTS`) and a text 64 KB (`MAX_TEXT`), refused past either; and a replacement keeps the contract or is refused naming the `drop` and `create` it needs (`Definition::breaks_contract`: whether it writes, its claims and their types, the fields it answers -- [the owner's decision 3](#decisions-for-the-owner), its recommended choice taken). It is held to the old tree wherever there is one, a statement marked broken by what the schema lacks included, as `declared::plan_statements` holds it: the mark is the schema's, and a grant names the statement whatever the schema holds, so a broken `transfer` replaced by a read of other claims is refused as a whole one is (`a_broken_statement_is_replaced_only_within_its_contract`). A text that misreads keeps no tree, and is defined again as anything.

*Declared.* `fennec_ql::schema_into` reads a schema text's `create statement`s after the collections each names (refused before them, or declared twice); `schema_text` reads the same and answers the collections. `declared::plan_statements`, beside `plan` rather than around it so the module's apply keeps its one copy of the plan, compares each with the database's by digest: one missing is its text, one changed its `create or replace` where the contract holds and `statement_contract_changed` where not, and under `Mode::Follow` `statement_missing` and `statement_changed`, each refusal's `collection` the statement's name. One the database holds and the code does not declare is left as it is, as a collection is, rather than "reported": the plan has no field for a note, and a refusal would hold up the apply. Applying a planned definition meets `NOT_BUILT` until slice 3, and a tenant's schema declaring one is refused at the router before anything is placed (`admin::schema_description`), as a router from before refused it as a schema that does not read. `integrations/schema-golden.json` gains no case, since the module writes it and reads no definition; fennec-http's `/_schema/plan` plans the collections alone, since what a scoped token is shown of a statement is slice 3's.

*Tests.* `crates/fennec-core/tests/stored.rs`: the record and its count alone and in a block, a block put back, every open and image, a compact read in and mapped, a replica and a reader of the changes, the refusals naming the statement with the replacement first in one block landing, a rebuild, the marks of `internally`, a replica and an open, a definition's own refusals, and the fixture `tests/statements.fennec` (`FENNEC_WRITE_FIXTURE=1`), whose `stale` record holds a digest no tree of its text has; fennec-ql's `a_definition_keeps_its_own_text` and `a_schema_text_declares_statements_after_their_collections`; fennec-abi's `a_schemas_statements_are_planned_by_their_trees`; and the module opening the fixture and keeping every text through its images after a schema change took what one reads (`web/fennec.test.js`). The upgrade's test -- an archiver and a replica built without kind 13 stopping in words -- is slice 4's, beside the order it holds: a replica from before stops at "record kind 13 is not a write" (`apply_records`), an open at "unknown record kind 13" (`load_records`) or "a block holds a record that is no write" (`each_inner`), and an archiver from before counts the record as no write (`writes_in`'s fallthrough), which is why readers upgrade first.

*Size.* The browser module: +4 116 bytes raw, +1 706 gzip and +824 brotli (`make size-report BASE=origin/main`; `fennec-lite` +1 286 brotli, no feature +1 379) -- the record read (`take_statement`, 1.6 KB raw), the table's clone and its image. The first cut was +6 576 brotli: the dependency check and its words in the module, `str` slices that could panic and brought a `char`'s formatting in, and the plan's function taking the mode at run time, which brought `Mode::Follow`'s copy of the plan into the module. `fennec-server`'s code is 57.4 KB more, 3 128.2 KB to 3 185.6, its build 78 KB of 5 404 (`make size-report BIN=fennec-server`, on main and the branch): slice 1's canonical encoder, which nothing reached until a digest was taken, 17.9 KB; the record, its table, the open's read and the commit's check 14.8; the dependency walk 9.4; the replacement's contract 6.4; the plan 2.2. A `Definition` cloned into the table was 5.7 KB more; the tree is read back from the text instead, as an open reads it.

*Time.* A commit asks an empty table one question (`statements.is_empty()`) and a replica's apply none -- a block of 50 000 writes would have had its notes walked for a schema mark; the parse compares each statement's kind once, for a definition's text. The parse's corpus time against main, slice 1's measurement, was not taken: the machine stayed loaded past 11 for the twenty minutes it was given, and a run under someone else's load is no figure; it is owed to a quiet one.

*The shell.* `call` is a write in fennec-cli's `kind`, `drop statement` and a `begin ... end` definition schema changes, `statements` and `describe statement` reads, a definition of one statement a write, since its `where` may come on the next line. An unended `begin` is refused at the text's end, which the shell reads as "go on", with no bracket of `lexer::Unclosed`; `call <name>` at a line's end is a mark in `Parser::cut`, its document on the next line, and so is `describe statement`, the collection `statement`'s description alone and a named statement's with a name after it: the reading says so (`Reading::open`), and the shell holds it for the next line where it would run a read at Enter. Not every mark holds a statement: most are left by a reading tried and dropped -- `get notes count` tries SQL's list first -- and a read the next line may go on with runs at Enter all the same; each form is a line of `a_statement_written_over_lines_reads_as_one`'s corpus, and `tests/shell.rs` types and pipes a definition over lines.

**Slice 3, calls and authority.** Definitions and calls run wherever FennecQL runs -- `/query`, `/batch`, the shell, the native library and a declared schema's apply; `Database::allow_definitions` is gone. What slices 4 to 7 build keeps refusing: MCP's `write` refuses a `call` and a router a tenant's schema declaring one (`NOT_BUILT`, now "named statements are not built here yet: ... run through `/query` and `/batch`"), `Fennec-Wait` holds no call (`waits::holdable`), and `/_call/<name>` is no route -- each but MCP's built by slice 4, below. A sync replica refuses `call`, `create statement` and `drop statement` in both runners in words of their own (`NAMED`, a scenario of `integrations/sync-scenarios.json` first; [native-and-sync.md](native-and-sync.md#sync-core)): the native core reads them (fennec-abi's `sync` feature turns on fennec-ql's `statements`), and FennecSync tells the module's refusal of them in those words on its replica.

*The call* (`engine/call.rs`). `Database::callee` judges one, each answered before the next is asked: the statement there (404, `NotFound`), the claims it declares (403, `Denied`), the arguments (400), `check` (400), a mark (409, `Exists`, with why) -- of what the schema lacks, or of a text that reads here as another statement, which is judged by the tree it reads as (`Stored::misread`) and never run. The first cut answered a text that no longer read before its claims, so a caller lacking them was told the statement was broken; only a text this build reads as nothing at all, or a process that installed no reader, has no claim to judge, and is refused at once. `Database::call` then runs each statement of the body with `run_one` in the block its caller opened -- a lone call's own, a `/batch`'s -- with the values by place, the arguments in the signature's order and then the claims, as the parser numbered them; the answer is the last statement's. `Statement::Call` is a write wherever it is asked, and the read path refuses it ("requires write access"). The arguments come by name (`{...}`, `$n`'s object, SQL's `name =>`) or by place, each read by `Value::coerce` as a document's field of its type is; one missing, extra, given twice, of another type, or `null` where the parameter has no default (the design's "never null unless it has a default", read as written) is refused naming it. `Database::exactly` walks a call's arguments and claims by the signature (`call_exactly`), so a `json` or `geo` one, in the text or a parameter, is read as written.

*The claims.* A call carries the server's token's `as {...}`, or a scoped token's own claims, which `Scope::rewrite` binds into it whole; each claim the statement declares is read by its name (`from "cognito:groups"`), and one missing, `null`, `""`, an empty list, an object, a list holding any of those, or of a type its declaration refuses, is one the caller lacks: 403 naming the claim, nothing run (the policy's `usable`, an empty list refused too). A list of more than `named::MAX_CLAIM_VALUES` (1 000, the one number `access::MAX_CLAIM_VALUES` is) is refused (403) naming the bound, never cut to it. Reading them in the engine, under the lock the body runs under, is what keeps a `drop` and `create` landing between the grant and the lock from running a body with claims nobody bound.

*`--call-max-rows`* (`Database::set_call_max_rows`, 100 000 unless told). The rows left are an atomic of the database, set for the call and given back by a guard (`Bounded`) as the body returns or unwinds: given back only on a return, a hook's panic left the bound set, and every later statement of the database, a plain `get` among them, was refused as a call past its rows until it was opened again. It is charged in `matching_ids_to` -- with no filter the rows taken, an index's exact answer its rows, the candidates and the scan each row tested, the loop stopping at the bound -- by the ordered walks (`walk_order`, `walk_composite`), their rows walked, and wherever else a statement reads rows: `near`'s answer and the rows its filter tested or an index named (`filtered_near`), `match`'s answer or `rerank`'s candidates, both sides of `fuse`, the rows a facet over `match` counts and those a `match` beside `order`, `count` or an aggregate selects (`matched_rows`), each `lookup` level's children -- a bucket, or the run of an index over several fields walked (`kept_children`) -- and the children `required` tests (`retain_with_children_to`). The first cut charged the scan and the indexes alone, and a call read past its bound through `near`, `match` or a `lookup` within their own 10 000, or with none. An insert's unique probe is not counted. Past it the call is a query error naming the bound and its block is put back. Outside a call the bound is `usize::MAX`: a comparison a row in the two loops against a value read once a statement; in a build without the feature, a constant.

*The definition's checks* (`Database::body_checked`, beside `depends`). Each field a `set` writes, and each a `set`, a `del` or a `get` over one collection filters on, is held by its collection -- a select list, an order, a `having` and a join's or `lookup`'s names may be aliases, which only running the statement tells, and are its own checks' at the call. Where the body writes, every `get`, `set` and `del` of it reads its rows through an index -- an equality, an `in` or a range over `id` or a field an ordered index keeps (a hash index's, equalities and `in` alone), an index over several fields led by one, a pick an ordered index walks, `near`, `match` or `fuse`, or a `get` of the first rows with no filter and no order -- or the definition says `scans`. The walk asks the parser's conjunct helpers with a `null` for every parameter and claim, which they take for any value.

*Listing.* `statements` answers the server's token a row a statement -- `name`, `version` (`[history, change]`), `signature`, `writes`, `comment`, `broken` -- and `describe statement` adds `answers`, `text` and `depends`. A scoped token is told what it may call by `/_whoami`'s `calls`, `statements` refused (403) and `describe statement` the 404 a missing one gets.

*Authority* (fennec-http's `access`). A policy line `<statement>[, <statement> ...] call [for <role>]` grants calling (`call_line`): `call` beside another grant or a `where` is refused, `*` is refused with or without a role, and a name no statement may have is refused, each at startup. `Scope::calls` holds what the token's roles are granted. `Scope::rewrite` passes a call it grants -- one it does not is the 404 a missing statement gets, in the same words -- refuses `as` (403) and binds the token's claims in; a definition and a drop stay schema changes, refused. Under the write lock, `access::judge_call` asks `callee` again and holds the body to the policy's `append-only` collections (an update or delete in one, 403), a broken statement's why told as "it runs again once it is defined again"; `access::run_as` runs the call with no scope installed -- the one statement run outside `within()` -- its answer unmasked and its refusals told (`told_call`): a guard's `reason` kept, a duplicate "met a unique value taken", the body's other errors "could not run", `--call-max-rows`'s as it is, while the server's token is told the engine's words. `/query`, keyed or not, and `/batch` go through both, and so does `run_bound`, for a token whose rules read another collection. The order a scoped call is answered in: the tenant and the token (`route_tenant`), `--call-rate` (429), the grant (404), `as` (403), then under the lock the statement (404), the claims (403), the arguments and `check` (400), `append-only` (403) and a broken statement (409). Two places differ from the design's sentence: `as`, the token's own mistake, is refused where the token meets the statement, before its existence is asked, and `append-only`, which needs the definition the lock holds, after `check`, as the table of statuses orders it.

*A scoped `/batch`* holds one call and nothing beside it: two calls, or a call beside any statement, is 400 before a grant is asked; the server's token runs any `/batch`, its calls in its block.

*`--call-rate`* (`calls::admitted`): a token bucket for each caller in each database -- its subject with its issuer, and the `Database` the call runs in, a tenant's own on a node -- refilled at the rate and holding a second's worth, charged once a request holding a call before the grant is asked, answered 429 with `Retry-After` and `retry_after` in the body, the server's token not held. Keyed by the caller alone, tenant A's `sub` "1" spent tenant B's. The callers are held in two generations (`Callers`) that turn each second, the time a bucket takes to refill: a turn forgets those who did not call for a whole window, whose buckets are full again, so forgetting hands back nothing; past 16 384 callers within the last two windows a new one waits for the turn (429), and none still counting is forgotten. The first cut emptied the table past 4 096 callers, as MCP's is, which gave each its second back: a caller holding enough tokens of other subjects reset its own limit by flooding. 100 a second unless told -- the design left the figure open: more than a person's app makes, and a service that calls more says `--call-rate 0`.

*Audited whole.* `audit::statement` logs each schema change of a request by the parser's own boundaries (`fennec_ql::spans`), each shaped as far as `MAX_TEXT`, past the 1 000 bytes a count keeps: split on `;`, a `begin ... end` definition was logged as far as its first. A text the parser refuses is split on `;` as before; a request changing no schema asks the parser nothing.

*The policy's lines naming nothing.* `calls::log_unheld` logs each `call` line naming a statement the database does not hold as the server starts, as each tenant opens (`Tenants::set_calls`, which also hands each tenant `--call-max-rows`) and after a `drop` lands (`after_drop`).

*Tests.* fennec-core's `tests/calls.rs` (the block and its answer, the guards' reasons, the claims, the arguments, `--call-max-rows` over a scan, `near`, `match` and a `lookup`, a panic in a call leaving no bound, a list claim past its bound, a misread statement answered in a call's order, the definition's checks), fennec-http's `calls::tests` (a caller counted by its database, a flood of callers handing back no calls) and `tests/audit.rs` (a definition logged whole) and the fixture's unchanged digest calling while `stale` is 409; fennec-http's `tests/named.rs` -- the review's transfer, eight customers keeping the sum; the same 404; the claims as `null`, `""`, `[]` and other types; the scoped `/batch`; the answer unmasked; the error told; `append-only`; `/_whoami`; `--call-rate` and `--call-max-rows`; a call running one version whole while its definition is replaced, over `/query`, keyed and not, and `/batch` -- and `access.rs`'s `a_call_line_grants_statements_by_name_alone`; fennec-server's `tests/calls.rs` (`* call` and its kin refused at the start, an unheld line logged at the start and after a drop); the sync scenario. Each fails with its part taken out: the claims' check, the `/batch` rule, the run outside `within()`, the grant, the `*` refusal, the log, the row bound, the rate, `told_call`, the refusal of `as`, `append-only`, a call answering `is_read_only`, the plan's check.

*Size and time.* The browser module, built without the feature, is 34 bytes smaller raw and as it was gzip and brotli (`make size-report BASE=origin/main`; `fennec-lite` and no feature 32 smaller raw): a call's bound is a `take` around the rows where a call runs, and folds away where none can. `fennec-server`'s code is 41.4 KB more, 3 185.4 KB to 3 226.8 -- fennec-core 21.2 (the call's judgment and typing, the definition's checks, the listing), fennec-http 14.5 (the policy's `call` lines, the scoped call's path, the rate), `core`'s formatting 2.0 -- its build 57 KB of 5 539 (`make size-report BIN=fennec-server`, on main and the branch). In process, main and the branch in turns -- a scratch example: 160 000 parses of eight ordinary texts, 50 000 `set`s by id, a scan testing 20 000 rows, 50 000 inserts, the median of nine rounds, five pairs, a pair run under another's load discarded -- the parse took 1 277-1 288 ns a text against main's 1 278-1 289, a `set` by id 921-938 ns against 910-931, the scan 39.65-40.00 ns a row against 39.66-40.29. An insert, whose path the slice leaves alone, moved from 1.5% faster than main to 3.3% slower between builds that differ only in the scan's loop, and 2.4% with the row bound compiled out: where the code lies, not what it does. The first cut asked the bound in every statement's loop, a comparison a row, and its scan measured 0.3% slower, 1.7% with every function and block aligned; a call's loop is now a `take` of its own, and every other statement's the loop it was. The review's fixes -- the guard, the charges of `near`, `match` and `lookup`, the list claim's bound, the misread signature, the rate by tenant in two generations, the audit's statement boundaries -- cost the module 8 bytes raw and nothing gzip or brotli tell from noise (`make size-report BASE=origin/named-statements-calls`), and `fennec-server` 7.6 KB of code, 3 227.1 KB to 3 234.7: fennec-core 1.9, fennec-http 5.9 -- the parser's reading inlined into the audit's events (`schema_events`, 5.3 KB, out of line and cold, `statements::record` 3.9 KB shorter on every request's path) and the rate's table (2.0). Each charge is one comparison a statement, or a parent of a `lookup`, where the call bound is `usize::MAX`; the run of an index over several fields and `required`'s probes count the children they test, an increment each, and nothing else a statement outside a call runs changed, so no time was measured for them.

**Slice 4, HTTP, the router and the upgrade.** `POST /_call/<name>` is the `call <name> $1` it stands for (`handle_call`): its body's `params` the one parameter (`api::parse_call`, read exactly where an argument keeps a list of numbers, `exactly_call`), run by `run_query` -- `/query`'s path from `--call-rate` on: the grant, the key, the wait, the lock, the answer -- so a call answers alike whichever way it came, and `a_call_by_its_path_answers_as_query_answers_it` holds ten refusals' statuses and words to `/query`'s. Nothing is parsed: the tree run is the definition's. A body holds `params` alone, an object or `null`, and a statement of no arguments takes none at all; anything beside it is 400, never ignored -- an `as` a caller meant for its claims, run without them, would be a call it did not make. So the server's token calls a statement that reads claims over `/query` alone, `call <name> $1 as $2`: the design left the route's body open, and it is the arguments'. The path takes a POST alone (405, `Allow: POST`), `/_call` and a deeper path are 404, and a name no statement may have (`valid_name`) is the 404 a missing one is, in the same words, and noted as nothing.

*The order.* Every surface but one met the table's order as slice 3 left it: a tenant's binding is `route_tenant`'s, before the tenant is looked up (`/_call/<name>` is a line of `tenants.rs`'s routes), a lapsed lease is `handle_admitted`'s (`a_call_whose_lease_lapses_as_it_runs_is_told_to_come_back`: put back by the fence as its block lands, 503), and CORS and the key are the path's whatever it is -- a preflight answered before any token, the key's hash over the path and the body, so another body under it is 422. The one: a read-only server or a marked read refused a call 403 before the lock, so a bad argument was 403 there and 400 everywhere else. A call is now judged first under the read lock -- `callee`, or `judge_call` for a scoped token -- and refused after (`judged_read_only`; `a_marked_call_is_judged_before_it_is_refused`), at `/batch` as at `/query` and `/_call`, a `/batch`'s refusal saying the line it stopped at (`a_read_only_server_judges_a_call_alike_at_every_way_in`; the review found `/batch` refusing 403 unjudged). A tenant's freeze or lapsed lease runs its request read-only too, but refuses a call unjudged, told to come back (503, `Retry-After`; `Config::come_back`): the review found it judged against this copy's definitions, so that after a failover the old primary answered 404 or 409 for a statement the new primary holds, where only a 403 became the 503 (`a_call_to_a_frozen_or_lapsed_tenant_is_told_to_come_back_unjudged`). A marked read on a frozen tenant passes the freeze and is judged, as a read-only server's.

*`Fennec-Wait`.* `holdable` reads the definition under a short read lock (`callee`): a body of `get`s, `set`s and `del`s, each held as it would be alone, with the arguments and claims bound in; an `insert` is refused, naming the statement -- what it makes, no look can wait for. A scoped caller's call is judged first (`judge_call`), as its run would judge it: asked the body first, a token granted a call whose body inserts and deletes in an `append-only` collection was told the 400 naming the insert before `append-only`'s 403, something of a body it may not read (`a_held_call_is_judged_before_its_body_is_asked`). A call the engine would refuse passes, for the run to answer in a call's order. `plan` plans the body the same way under the write lock it ran under, and a plan with nothing to look for, a call that only reads, leaves none: it is answered as it ran. Whether a call wrote is asked of the database, never of its answer, which is its last statement's: a body whose first `set` wrote and whose last answered no row was held as one that wrote nothing, and every look finding the first `set`'s row ran it again. `waits::Mark` holds the change counter, which a lone statement's block moves as it lands, and the open block's writes (`Database::block_writes`), which a keyed run's and a `/batch`'s grow; a `/batch` holding a call is asked the same, every other `/batch` its answers as before. `a_call_is_held_as_its_body_would_be` holds both paths and the mark: taken out, its bump was held the whole 10 s.

*Counted and traced by name.* `statements::call` notes `call <name> $1` wherever a call is read -- `/_call`, a document of literals, a parameter, SQL's `CALL` -- one shape (`a_call_is_one_shape_whichever_way_it_came`). Its test found a held request counted wrong, a call or not: each run noted its text again, so a claim held for two runs was counted as `set ...; set ...`, a shape of its own; each run now forgets what the one before noted (`statements::clear` in `respond`). The slow line's `statement` is the shape and its `version` the `[history, change]` that ran (`metrics::called`, `audit::Field::Pair`), never an argument; the server span's `db.operation.name` is `call`, `db.query.text` the shape and `db.stored_procedure.name` the name, and `http.route` is `/_call/{name}` whatever word the name is (`trace::route` keeps the words it knows under a path of its own, and a statement named `stats` would have been one).

*The router.* It counts `/t/<tenant>/_call/<name>` under `route="call"` (`tenant_route`, read off the path's segments with nothing allocated), never its name, and the alerts and dashboards that read `route="tenant"` read `tenant|call`. `admin::schema_description` refuses a schema declaring a statement no more: the router reads it with the grammar it was built with (`fennec_abi::read`), each statement after the collections it names, before anything is placed, and the node checks it against the schema as it applies it; a router from before refuses it as a schema that does not read, 400, nothing placed. `Server::with_tenants` installs the statement reader as `Server::new` does: a node built as a library -- the router's tests, the benches -- read every tenant's statements as text that did not read, each broken.

*The upgrade.* `make upgrade-check` (`tools/upgrade-order.sh`) builds `fennec-server` and `fennec` at `12d6f8db^`, the last main before kind 13, runs this tree's server as a primary with the old replica following it and the old archiver writing it, and defines a statement. The replica refuses the record -- "replication: could not apply a write: corrupt: record kind 13 is not a write; connecting again" -- and connects again for ever, never taking a write past it. The archiver does not stop: it counts the record one write, as it should, and keeps the stream's bytes, but every image it takes of its own fails ("no image taken: corrupt: unknown record kind 13"), and the old `fennec verify` refuses the archive, which this tree's reads whole. So nothing is misread and nothing an older archiver held is lost, but a restore needs the new binary: the order stands, written into RELEASING.md and `deploy/RUNBOOK.md`. The review found RELEASING.md claiming more than the script drove, so it now builds `fennec-shard` at the base too and drives the rest: the old `fennec restore` refuses the archive ("fennec restore: corrupt: unknown record kind 13") and the old server the file the new one restored ("could not open ...: corrupt: unknown record kind 13"); the old router answers a schema declaring a statement 400 ("the schema does not read: ... a schema is `create collection` and `create index` statements") and `GET /_shard/tenants/<t>` 404 after; and this tree's router, a tenant placed with its statement on this tree's node, fails a move onto an old node at the install (400, "node `n2`: ... the image does not load: corrupt: unknown record kind 13"), the tenant still on its source answering its row. The review also found the three texts in two orders: RELEASING.md and the runbook said archivers, routers, nodes, this file archivers, nodes, routers. They say the runbook's now, since what the order holds is the last step alone and the router's place cannot lose anything (above).

*Tests.* fennec-http's `tests/named.rs` (the parity, the form of the path and the body, the marked call's order, the key, CORS, the replacement test's fourth site), `tests/waits.rs` (`a_call_is_held_as_its_body_would_be`), `tests/lease_answers.rs`, `tests/tenants.rs`'s route list, `admin`'s and `trace`'s unit tests; fennec-server's `tests/statements.rs` (the shape, a held call counted once), `tests/logs.rs` (the slow line) and `tests/tracing.rs` (the span); fennec-shard's `tests/metrics.rs` (a tenant created with a statement, its calls counted under `call`).

*Size and time.* The browser module is the same to the byte raw, the full build, `fennec-lite` and no feature alike (gzip and brotli within their noise, `make size-report BASE=origin/main`): nothing of the slice is the module's but `Database::block_writes`, which it never calls. `fennec-server`'s code is 9.4 KB more, 3 234.7 KB to 3 244.1 (`make size-report BIN=fennec-server` on main and the branch) -- fennec-http 9.1: the route and its body, the wait over a body and its mark, the shape, the slow line's version and the span's name -- and `fennec-shard`'s 2.3 KB, 2 924.8 to 2 927.1: the route counted and the schema read with the grammar. `make shard-bench ARGS=latency`, main and the branch in turns, ten pairs at a 1-minute load of 1.8 to 2.4 (one run past 2.5 discarded), the medians: a GET straight to the node 21.6 us p50 on main and 21.5 on the branch, through the router 42.5 and 42.3, the router's addition 20.9 and 21.0, each inside the runs' spread (20.3-26.9 on main, 20.5-25.4 on the branch); with `--entry-token`, 42.2 and 42.4 without the header, 42.6 and 43.0 with it. A request that is no call pays, at the node, a scan of its path for `_call`, a match on its statement as `/query` notes it, a write's two loads for the mark and a thread-local taken as it is counted, and at the router its route read off its segments twice.

**Slice 5, the read-only executor.** A call whose statement reads is a read. `Statement::is_read_only` still answers `false` for a call everywhere; a call is asked again under the lock it then runs under, never apart from it: `Database::call_reads` -- `Some(true)` where its statement reads, `Some(false)` where it writes, `None` where none here can run (none of that name, broken, or a text that reads here as another statement), which the call's judgment answers wherever it is asked -- and `Database::reads` for any statement. `Database::read_call` is the executor: over `&Database`, a call judged by `callee`, each statement of its body run as `query` runs one, the answer the last statement's; a body that writes is refused (`Error::ReadOnly`, "statement `x` writes, and only a statement that reads runs here"), never run. `Database::query` runs a call through it, so every read path takes one: the server's, the native library's and a pin's.

*Where it is asked.* The server's lone call (`read_call`, before read-only mode's refusal): under one read lock it asks `call_reads`, answers a key an earlier call kept (`idempotent::answered`; a read keeps none), judges the call as the token (`access::judged`, which `judge_call` now asks and returns nothing of) and reads -- its last statement, where a plain `get`, written out from the stored documents as `/query` writes one (`read_call_json`) -- pinned (`pin_call`, then `Pinned::read_call_json` with no lock held) where `pin` takes the body, and again under the lock, asked and judged there again, where a statement of it reached for an index the pin left out. A statement that writes goes on to the write path, which judges it under the write lock, or to read-only mode's refusal after its judgment, as slice 4 left it. A `/batch` of reads and calls is a batch of reads where every call reads under its read lock (`read_batch`, `all_read`), pinned as one (`pin` judges a call and pins its body), each call judged as the token and told as a lone one (`batch_call`); one holding a call that writes goes on as a write. A tenant frozen or past its lease (`Config::come_back`) answers a call whose statement this copy holds and reads, as it answers a `get`, and tells any other to come back unjudged. A query across tenants (`admin_query`'s `writes`) passes a call whose statement reads on that tenant and refuses one that writes, naming it. The native library asks `fennec_abi::reads` under the read lock it then reads under, for a text holding a call. In the engine, `execute_with` reads a call that reads with no block, no replica's or lease's refusal and no counter moved, `execute_block` starts by `reads`, and `Database::call` reads a reading body in its caller's block. The shell's `--read-only` takes a call that reads. MCP's tools refuse every call still, until each statement has its own (slice 7).

*The answer.* A call that reads names the change it read at -- the read lock's, or the pin's, of the history the database is on -- in `Fennec-Seq` and `Fennec-History` whether it was marked or not, as a read-only `/batch` does, where a lone `get` names it only when marked: every call named it while every call was a write, and an SDK reading it for its position finds one still; a marked read's moves nothing of the client's, as ever. The design left that open.

*The row bound is the thread's.* Slice 3 kept `--call-max-rows`'s rows left in the database, an atomic only the writer running the call touched. A call that reads runs beside other readers -- a plain `get`, another call -- which a bound held there would charge, or refuse past it, and two calls would spend each other's rows. The rows left are a thread-local now (`call::LEFT`), set as a body starts and given back as it ends, on a panic too (`Bound` for a read; `Database::bounded` for a write, whose body takes the database whole, by `catch_unwind`), and the database counts the calls running over it (`calls`): a statement while none runs asks that one atomic, as it asked the rows left before, and reads no thread-local. A pin is a database of its own, with its own count. `a_reading_calls_rows_are_its_threads_alone` runs twelve threads over one database, eight calls each reaching its whole bound and four plain scans, none refused; with the rows held in one cell (swapped in for the test) it fails.

*Found on the way.* `fennec_open_memory` installed no statement reader: an app that never opened a file had each statement it defined marked broken ("its text did not read") and every call of it refused. It installs it now, as a file's open does.

*The SDKs.* A call text sent with the read mark every SDK has -- JavaScript's `run(text, params, { read: true })` and its kin -- goes near and is answered there; no SDK changed. `call`'s own `{ read: true }` comes with the method in slice 6: the plan put it in this slice, and with no `call` method yet there was nothing to put it on.

*Tests.* fennec-core's `tests/read_calls.rs` (answered over a shared database and judged as every call, `execute` reading it with no block and no counter; the executor refusing a body that writes; a replica answering alone and in a block; the thread's bound; a long call pinned as its body, one an index answers declined or refused on the pin); fennec-http's `tests/named.rs` (`a_call_that_reads_is_answered_where_reads_are`: a read-only server, a marked read, a key, a `/batch` of reads and calls, the counter and `_idempotency` untouched; `a_call_that_reads_takes_no_write_lock`, answered at every way in while another reader holds the database; `a_replica_answers_a_call_that_reads`; `a_call_read_on_a_pin_answers_as_under_the_lock`; `a_call_runs_as_the_statement_it_finds_under_its_lock`, a statement made again between reading and writing while it is called at every site -- each read answered as a read and each write landed once, a read-only server never writing; asked and read under two locks, it is answered 403, "`flip` writes"), `tests/lease_answers.rs` (a frozen or lapsed tenant's call that reads), `tests/mcp.rs` (`a_call_is_no_tools_to_run_yet`); fennec-shard's `tests/read_replicas.rs` (a marked call answered by the read replica from its own rows, and one that writes refused there) and `tests/fanout.rs` (`a_call_across_tenants_reads_and_one_that_writes_is_refused`); fennec-ffi's `a_call_that_reads_is_read_beside_the_readers`; fennec-cli's `tests/lock.rs` (`--read-only` and a call); fennec-server's `tests/statements.rs` (counted by its shape, its rows those written out) and `tests/logs.rs` (a read's slow line, naming its version); fennec-ql's `they_are_writes_and_their_listing_reads` (the read path answering a call that reads and refusing one that writes).

*Size and time.* The browser module, built without the feature, is the same to the byte raw, `fennec-lite` 4 bytes more and no feature 2 (`Database::reads`, which a block's start asks), gzip and brotli within their noise (`make size-report BASE=origin/main`). `fennec-server`'s code is 14.8 KB more, 3 244.6 KB to 3 259.5 (`make size-report BIN=fennec-server` on main and the branch): fennec-core 5.3 (the executor, its JSON path, the question, a call pinned as its body, the thread's bound), fennec-http 8.6 (the lone call's read path, a batch's calls, the question a query across tenants asks), `core`'s formatting 0.9 -- a call that reads answered on every route that answers a read, where a replica, a read-only server and a marked read refused it. In process through `fennec_http::handle`, main and the branch in turns -- a scratch example over 20 000 rows: each operation's median over blocks of 50 requests, the median of nine rounds, five pairs at a 1-minute load of 1.8 to 2.4, none discarded -- a call that reads is answered without the write lock: by id 1 422 ns on main and 1 054 on the branch by its path (1 377 and 1 046 over `/query`), through a hash index 2 105 and 1 585. Its read runs as the `get` it runs: the executor's JSON path 341 ns against the `get`'s `query_json` 367, the pin's look 26 ns for either, the question under the lock 3; a call over 20 000 rows is pinned as the `get` is, 482.8 us against the `get`'s 481.3 (475.4 on main, under the write lock with no pin, the writers waiting it out). The call's request costs 225 ns more than the `get`'s by id (1 054 against 829): its judgment (`callee`, 125 ns with its arguments' object), the shape and the version noted, the change it names -- each its write path's too, and on main. Nothing a statement that is no call runs changed in time but by where its code lies: a `get` by id 826 and 829 ns, through a hash index 1 361 and 1 372, a scan of 20 000 rows 482.5 and 481.3 us, a `put` 1 312 and 1 311, a `set` by id 1 381 and 1 366 -- the read lock every lone write took to ask the data ceiling now taken only where a ceiling is set and the write may grow the data (`grows`). A call that writes, 1 896 and 1 926 ns, takes that read lock to ask its statement where it took it for the ceiling, as many locks as before; the 30 ns are its question and the row bound's thread-local.

**Slice 6, the SDKs, the golden files and types.** Every SDK has `call`, and the builder renders it, held to `integrations/builder-golden.json`. No Rust runs a call differently: a call is the `call <name> $1` slice 3 judged, sent over `/query` as any text is.

*The text.* `call(name, args, { claims })` makes a call, and its text method `toCall` the pair `[text, params]`, as `toInsert` does: `call <name>` for no arguments (absent or null), `call <name> $1` for an object (an empty one is still one argument), and ` as $n` after them for claims, numbered after the arguments (`call mine $1 as $2`, `call stamp as $1`). The arguments and the claims are parameters, normalised as a document is; the one thing written into the text is the name, checked by the engine's own rule (`named::valid_name`: ASCII letters, digits and `_`, a letter first, 59 bytes) before anything is sent, so it is the call's injection boundary, as `ident` is a query's. The refusals are three messages every SDK repeats to the word: ``invalid statement name: "<name>"`` (quoted as `invalid collection name` quotes), `a call's arguments are an object of values by name` and `a call's claims are an object of values by name`. The golden file holds 25 cases (`web/golden.mjs`), a chain of `call` and `toCall`: each parameter type, no arguments and none given as null, an empty object, claims with and without arguments and `null` claims, a date, a float32 array, a text that is never in the text, the name at 59 and 60 bytes, with a space, with a `;`, a leading digit, past ASCII and empty, arguments as a list or a text, claims as a text, and three `docs:` cases the site's tabs show.

*The client.* The method sends the text over `/query` through the SDK's own statement path, so the transport, the errors, the key and `wait` are what that path has. `claims` is the `as` (the server's token alone may say it: a scoped token's are its own and a call that says them is 403), `read` the mark every SDK has for a text it knows only reads, which on a client reading near goes to the read replica after the client's writes -- slice 5 left the option for this slice -- and a statement that writes, marked, is 403 there and never landed. Without `read` a call goes home. The spellings:

| SDK | builder, text | method |
|---|---|---|
| JavaScript | `call(name, args, { claims })`, `toCall()` | `db.call(name, args, { claims, idempotencyKey, wait, signal, read })` |
| Python | `call(name, args=None, claims=None)`, `to_call()` | `db.call(name, args=None, *, claims, idempotency_key, wait, read)`, `AsyncClient` alike |
| Go | `NewCall(name, args, opts...)`, `ToCall()` | `db.Call(ctx, name, args, Claims(...))`; `db.IdempotencyKey(k)`, `db.Wait(d)`, `db.Read()` make the copy that calls |
| .NET | `Query.Call(name, args, claims)`, `ToCall()` | `db.CallAsync(name, args, claims:, idempotencyKey:, wait:, read:, cancellationToken)` |
| Swift | `Call.call(name, args, claims:)`, `toCall()` | `FennecRemote.call(name, args, claims:, idempotencyKey:, wait:, read:)`, `Fennec.call(name, args, claims:)` |
| Kotlin | `call(name, args, claims)`, `toCall()` | `FennecRemote.call(name, args, claims, idempotencyKey, wait, read)`, `Fennec.call(name, args, claims)` |
| Dart | `Call(name, args:, claims:)`, `toCall()` | `FennecRemote.callStatement(name, args:, claims:, idempotencyKey:, wait:, read:)`, `Fennec.callStatement(name, args:, claims:)` |

The answer is the text method's: JavaScript's `run`, Python's `query`, Go's `Result` (its `Rows` for a body that reads or says `returning`, and, found on the way, its `Facets` for a body ending in a faceted `get`: a call's last statement can be any `get`), .NET's `ExecResult` (not `QueryAsync`'s rows, which lose `Seq` and `Replayed`), the native SDKs' `Answer`. Go bounds a call by its `ctx` and .NET by its `CancellationToken`, not the HTTP client's timeout (their tests hold a silent server to it); Dart offers no cancellation and no timeout, having none for its text. A refusal is each SDK's error with the status and, for a `require ... else`, the `reason` -- a lone call's 412 carries no `at`. In JavaScript `batch` takes a `Call` beside a query; Python's `batch` takes `(text, params)` pairs alone, so `call(...).to_call()` goes there; .NET's takes `Statement`s.

*The embedded SDKs.* Swift's, Kotlin's and Dart's local database has `call` too, the same text through `fennec_query`: no C ABI, no JNI entry, no `fennec.h` line. `claims` there is the embedder's `as` (a signed-in user's, held to the declared types as a token's are); `wait` and a key do not exist on a local database, so there is nothing to refuse. A database made by `sync` refuses `call` in the engine's words (`NAMED`), surfaced as each SDK's error before anything runs or is queued; the app calls the server through the HTTP class (`sync.remote.call` in JavaScript), and what the call wrote comes back through the shapes. The page: `Fennec.call` sends the same text and the module refuses it in its own words ("named statements are a server's; this module is built without them"), `wait` in `LOCAL_WAIT`'s, and `FennecSync.local.call` in `NAMED`'s.

*Types.* `fennec types` writes the named statements of a database file or of a `.fennecql` (`fennec_ql::schema_statements`, which `schema_text` leaves to read as before), in all seven languages, beside the collections' rows -- whose output is byte for byte what it was. For each statement an arguments type from the signature (a parameter with a default is optional, a claim is never an argument) and the row of its answer where the answer is typed: the last statement a `get` or a `returning` over one collection, each item a plain field (its type and nullability as the collection's row has it, `id` an int) or a count; an item worked out, renamed or reached by a path is the language's json type, and a body that answers only the count of rows it wrote has no row (`unknown`). TypeScript: `FennecStatements = { transfer: { args: {...}; row: {...} } }` and, in `fennec.d.ts`, `TypedCall<FennecStatements>`, which `db.call.bind(db)` takes (`const call: TypedCall<FennecStatements> = db.call.bind(db)`: a name completes, an argument missing or of another type is a compile error, `make types-check`); the other languages one `<Name>Args` and one `<Name>Row` each -- TypedDicts (the defaults in a `total=False` subclass, 3.10 having no `NotRequired`), Go structs (a default a `*T` with `omitempty`, so a Go caller can hand the struct to `Call`, which marshals it), C# records, Codable structs with a public initialiser, Kotlin data classes with `toMap()`, Dart classes with `toJson` and `fromJson` -- a parameter named as a keyword escaped (`class` is `class_` in Kotlin). No function wrapper is written: the call is the SDK's `call` with the arguments type. `integrations/types-golden/schema.fennecql` gained eight statements covering each parameter type, none at all, claims, plain fields required and not, `returning`, a count, items not typed, a write answering a count, and parameters named as the keywords are; `every_generator_writes_what_types_golden_holds` makes each file again, Python's, Swift's, Dart's and Go's checks of the file as before, Kotlin's and Dart's tests compile it. Left: `_score` is untyped, and `Definition::answers` lists an alias twice (`title as headline` is `headline` and `headline`), which the writers keep once.

*The site.* `{{builder}}` tabs for a call -- `site/builder_tabs.py` writes `db.call(...)` in each language from a `call` case, ending at `toCall` as a chain of a query ends at an endpoint -- on the FennecQL page's named statements, with the SDKs' own docs tests (`make docs-builder`; the Swift, Kotlin and Dart ones needed a `call` on their recorder), and a section each on *Your language* and *Mobile*; the JavaScript page, the TypeScript example (type-checked, `docs/schema.fennecql` holding a statement), the sync and read-replica pages, `web/README.md`, README and the seven SDK READMEs.

*Tests.* JavaScript: `web/fennec.test.js` (the text, its options and headers, `read` on a client reading near and home, a refusal's status and `reason`, the page's refusal and `wait`), `fennec.replicas.test.js` (a router and a read replica: a call marked `read` is answered by the replica, shown by an answer while home is down, unmarked it goes home, a writing call marked is 403 and lands nothing), `fennec.sync.test.js` (`remote.call` runs, a key replays, a `require`'s reason, and `local.call` is refused), the golden file's 25 cases, `fennec.client.test.js`'s export list, `make types-check` (a `TypedCall` that takes and refuses what it should). Each other SDK runs the 25 golden cases and its own: Python 525 (`tests/test_call.py`: argument types, 412's reason, 404, 400, 403, a key replaying, claims, a read near and a write refused 403), Go (`vet` and the suite, `-race` on the call tests; a call sent exactly as `ToCall` makes it, the context, the timeout), .NET 616 (`CallTests.cs`, a silent handler for the cancellation), Swift 157 (and again on one thread), Kotlin 637, Dart 621 -- each against a real `fennec-server`, and a router and a read replica where the SDK already tests one (Kotlin, Swift and Dart; Python's marked calls go to a plain server, which runs a marked request read-only on every route, and Go's and .NET's reads tests script the server). `cargo test -p fennec-cli` 43, three new (a database file writes what its schema text does, a broken statement is named and not typed, the answer typed as far as its items are fields).

*Size and time.* The browser module is the same module: no Rust of the slice is in it but a function `fennec-ql` builds with `statements` alone (`fennec.wasm` is 828 261 bytes before and after, its hash moved by the line numbers its panics carry). The JavaScript gained `Call`, `call`, `FennecHttp.call` and `Fennec.call`: `builder.js`, `http.js` and `client.js` together 94 839 bytes to 98 684 and 26 029 to 26 952 brotli, 0.9 KB, unminified and mostly the comments; `fennec.js` 12 lines (the glue's line count the docs quote, 605 to 617). `fennec`, the shell, is 2 055.8 KB of code to 2 096.9 (`make size-report BIN=fennec`, main and the branch): `fennec` 172.2 to 204.5 -- the generators, a statement's two types in seven languages -- `core` 279.9 to 286.6 and `alloc` 62.4 to 64.5; every other crate to the byte, and `fennec-server` and `fennec-shard` do not link it. Generating types runs once on a developer's machine, so no time was measured; no path a request takes changed.

**Slice 7, the studio, MCP and the shell.** Every surface the design names now calls a statement, and none refuses one as not built: `named::NOT_BUILT` is gone.

*MCP's tools* (`fennec_http::mcp`). `tools/list` holds, after the fixed tools, a `call_<name>` tool for each statement the token's list holds (`callable_stored`), by name: one that can run (not broken, its text read here); granted by a `call` line to a scoped token (`Scope::may_call`) holding every claim it declares, judged as its call judges them (`engine::claims_carried`: one held as `null`, `""` or a value of another type is missing), so a token that may never call it is told nothing of its arguments, and to the server's token where it declares no claim -- the server's token calls one that does with `as {...}`, which no model is handed, so its tool would refuse every call; and one that writes only under `--mcp-write`. That list is asked under a read lock let go before the call, and a `drop` and a `create` landing between make a reading tool's statement one that writes: the bound the call runs under carries `--mcp-write` (`mcp::Bound`), and the lock it runs under asks it again (`mcp::writes_refused`) -- `read_call`'s read lock, `mcp::open`'s write lock, the keyed path's -- refusing (403) a statement that writes as it now is where the session takes none. Its `description` is the `comment` and what the bounds do to it, its `inputSchema` the signature (each argument typed, `bytes`, `sparse<N>` and `json` any value, a default's `default` and left out of `required`, `additionalProperties: false`), its `outputSchema` the answer typed by `Definition::answer` -- `fennec types`' typing moved into fennec-core so both read one -- every column but `id` and a count nullable and an untyped one any value, since the official client checks a result against it and must never refuse what the server writes, and `{"affected": n}` for a write with no `returning`; `readOnlyHint` where it reads, `destructiveHint` where it sets or deletes (a body of `insert`s only adds). A call is `call <name> $1` with the tool's arguments as `$1`, through the session's write path -- `/query`'s own on a server (`handle_query`: the grant, the claims, `--call-rate`, the key, the lock), and a local session's (`write_local`), which now asks `call_reads` under its lock and judges and runs a scoped call as `/query` does (`judge_call`, `run_as`) where it ran every write `within()`. *The bound before the block lands*: the tool sets its rows and bytes in a thread-local around the call (`mcp::bounded_call`), and the path that answers asks it -- a reading call as `read_call` answers it, a writing one in a block `mcp::open` opens before it runs and `mcp::land` lands only once the answer, rendered as the tool would give it, is within them, put back and refused (413) past them -- so a model is never told half an answer nor leaves a write it was not told of; the keyed path asks it too, though no tool sends a key. A request that is no call tool's pays one read of the thread-local. A statement not in the token's list, or none of that name, is the unknown tool a missing statement is (`-32602`); a `call` sent to `query` or `write` is refused naming its tool. Every statement's tool counts as `tool="call"` in `fennec_mcp_tool_calls_total`, no label naming a statement, and a call of one that writes is an `mcp` audit line by its tool's name. The list stays `listChanged: false` and its `ttlMs` five minutes: a definition or a drop changes it, rarely, and a client lists again.

*The studio.* The schema view lists the database's statements below the collection's fields (or alone, with no collection picked): to the server's token `statements`' rows -- the signature, whether it writes, the comment, why one is broken -- and each one's definition from `describe statement`, its answers and what it reads, which opens in the query editor as `create or replace statement` (`replacing`); to a scoped token the names `/_whoami`'s `calls` grants, since `statements` stays the server token's (slice 3), its call form taking the arguments as a JSON object; to the database in the page none, the module being built without them. The call form reads the signature (`signatureParams`) into an input an argument, each read as its type holds it (`values.js`' `readInput`, the row editor's), shows the statement it sends -- `call <name> $1`, the arguments the one parameter, the name held to the engine's rule (`statementName`) -- and runs it in the query editor under an `Idempotency-Key` (the editor's `load` takes a key for that run), its answer in the grid. `/_schema/plan` plans collections alone, so a replacement is checked as it runs, by the engine. `splitStatements` reads a definition's body as one statement: after `as begin` in a piece that begins `create [or replace] statement`, a `;` cuts nothing until the `end` that is no `case`'s; `begin` elsewhere, a field, opens nothing. The highlighter's words gain `end`, `call`, `statement`, `statements`, `claims`, `public`, `scans` and `replace` (`make studio-highlight` writes the studio's and the Grafana data source's copies; `site/test_highlight.py` holds them).

*The shell.* `.help` gives a definition's forms, a call's with `as` and SQL's `CALL`, and `statements`, `describe statement` and `drop statement`. `fennec_ql::reading` knew `begin ... end` (slice 1); its corpus gains a body whose `case` ends inside it and whose `;` comes before its `end`, read over lines as one, and the left-open test names its prefix cut after the `case`'s `end` as the unnamed item it is.

*Tests.* fennec-http's `tests/mcp.rs`: the tools typed by their signatures, listed and called in both eras, refused as `/query` refuses, the unknown tool, `query` and `write` sending a call on (`a_statement_is_a_tool_of_its_own_typed_by_its_signature`); a writing call past the rows and past the bytes refused and put back, a reading one refused, a client's call through `/query` held to no tool's bound (`a_call_tool_past_its_bound_is_refused_and_its_writes_put_back`, failing with `mcp::land` or `read_call`'s check taken out); a scoped agent's tools its `call` lines, its claims bound, and a statement whose claims the token lacks -- `null`, `""`, another type or none -- no tool (`a_scoped_agent_calls_what_its_policy_grants`); a reading statement made again as one that writes while a session with no `--mcp-write` calls its tool, refused and never written (`a_tool_listed_as_a_read_never_writes_once_its_statement_does`, failing with `mcp::writes_refused` taken out). fennec-cli's `tests/mcp.rs`: a session over a file listing and calling its statements, a write past `--max-rows` put back. `integrations/mcp`: every era over both transports lists and calls a reading and a writing statement through `@modelcontextprotocol/client`, which checks each answer against its `outputSchema`, every tool name held to Claude's and OpenAI's patterns. The studio: the splitter over a definition, one statement sent whole, and a call's text, name and signature (`statements.test.mjs`); a call's answer on the server held to the page's answer to its body, and the page's refusal of `statements`, `call` and `create statement` in its words (`transport.test.mjs`, last, the definitions leaving the server's counter ahead); the list, a definition and a call from its form into the grid under a key (`e2e.test.mjs`).

*Size and time.* The browser module is the base's byte for byte (`make size-report BASE=origin/main`). The studio's first load is 71 289 bytes gzipped to 71 315, the 70 KB the docs quote; the schema view with what it shares is 20 124 to 23 337 (the bound `statements.test.mjs` holds is 24 000), the query editor's 20 820 to 22 336, `statements-views.js` and the schema view's statements their share. `fennec-server`'s code is 3 259.8 KB to 3 276.8 (`make size-report BIN=fennec-server`, main and the branch): `fennec_http` 905.3 to 919.2, the tools' definitions, schemas and bounds, and `fennec_core` 1 193.5 to 1 195.8, the answer's typing moved in from the shell. A request that is no call tool's reads one thread-local more on the write path and a call's read path; a tool call renders a writing call's answer once more, under the lock, to measure it.

## The plan in slices

Each slice is a pull request of its own, green and measured, and carries its own docs -- the reference pages, README, the SDK lists and the site's search vectors for what it adds ([CLAUDE.md](../../CLAUDE.md)'s conventions) -- never a docs slice at the end. A slice that adds a statement form or a status says so where a user looks in the same pull request.

**0. `require <n> else '<reason>'`, alone and first** (built: [writes.md](writes.md#require)). The parser's clause, which owes no `Parser::cut` mark; `Error::Unmet`'s reason; `render_batch_stop` and fennec-abi's error (`"at"` and `"reason"`, the module's and the native library's); every builder's `require` option and the golden file (`make builder-golden`); each SDK's error (`FennecError.reason` and its six kin); the sync's refusal of a write put back carrying it, a scenario first, both runners. Useful without anything below, and the smallest change to measure the parser and the module by.

**1-3. The parser, storage and replication, calls and authority, landed together** -- or, where one lands first, execution refuses `call` and `create statement` as not built: a statement defined with nothing to check it, or a call with nothing to scope it, must never exist on main. All three are built, and the upgrade's order with slice 4 (RELEASING.md, `make upgrade-check`): a reader from before stops at kind 13.

- *1. The parser and the tree* (fennec-ql, fennec-core's `query`) -- **built** (#423, [what is built](#what-is-built)), execution refusing: `create [or replace] statement`, the signature with `claims` and the markers, `$name` and `$jwt.<claim>` numbered inside a definition and refused outside, `check`, `comment`, `begin ... end`, `drop statement`, `statements`, `describe statement`, `call` in its three forms with `as`, SQL's `CALL` and the three refusals, the body's refusals at definition; the boxed variants (a `Statement` is as large as its largest variant); the canonical tree's digest and the grammar's epoch. Owes: the corpus of every FennecQL text the repository holds parsed on main and the branch, no tree changed ([fennecql.md](fennecql.md#sql-spellings)); fennec-cli's `kind`, `Parser::cut`'s marks and the shell corpus' lines; the refusals not consuming their word; `TAKEN`; the stack of 511 parentheses measured.
- *2. Storage and replication* (fennec-core) -- **built** ([what is built](#what-is-built)), definitions refused but where a test lets them: the table in its own `Arc` cell, record kind 13 alone and in a block, `writes_in` counting it, `Undo::Statement`, the image, the open's digest check marking broken, `apply` on a replica, `changes_in` passing over it, the dependency checks at commit and the internal writers' marks, the version as `(history, change)`, `declared::plan` and `schema_text`, the `statements` feature outside `full` with the record kept in every build.
- *3. Calls and authority* (fennec-core's call, fennec-http's `access`) -- **built** ([what is built](#what-is-built)): `Database::call` under the write lock as a write at every site, the arguments' and claims' typing, `check`, the last statement's answer, `--call-max-rows`, `--call-rate`; `scoped()` taking a `Call` in the refusal order -- the grant, the claims, `as`, `append-only` -- the run outside `within()`, a scoped `/batch` of one call alone, `told` for a call's errors, the policy's `call` lines, `* call` refused and names no statement holds logged, `/_whoami`; the sync's refusals in both runners.

**4. HTTP, the router and the upgrade** -- **built** ([what is built](#what-is-built)). `POST /_call/<name>`, its statuses in their order, `answered_under_lease`, CORS, the `Idempotency-Key`, `Fennec-Wait` (`holdable` and `waits::plan` over a body), `/_metrics`' route, the statement shape, `db.stored_procedure.name`, the slow line; fennec-shard's schema parse taking the grammar; RELEASING.md's and the runbook's order. Owes `make shard-bench ARGS=latency` inside its spread.

**5. The read-only executor** -- **built** ([what is built](#what-is-built)). A call over `&Database` reading its definition again under the read lock, refusing a body that writes; pins for long ones; replicas, read-only servers and marked reads answering it; every site of the table above held by its test; the SDKs' `{ read: true }` on a text they send already, `call`'s own with the method in slice 6.

**6. The SDKs, the golden files and types** -- **built** ([what is built](#what-is-built)). `call` in six SDKs and Dart's `callStatement`, the builder's `call` and `toCall` held to `builder-golden.json`, `fennec.d.ts` (`make types-check`), `fennec types`' wrappers in `types-golden/` as far as the answer is typed.

**7. The studio, MCP and the shell** -- **built** ([what is built](#what-is-built)). The schema view's statements and the call form (`transport.test.mjs` holding a call's answers to a server's), `splitStatements` and the highlighter taught `begin ... end` (`make studio-highlight`), the `call_<name>` tools (`integrations/mcp` against the official client in both eras, the bound checked before the block lands), the shell's `.help`. Owes `make studio-test` and `make mcp-test`.

**The tests the review asked for**, each failing with its part taken out, beside each slice's own:

- a claim the token carries as `null`, as `""`, as `[]` and as another type: 403, nothing run;
- `put` naming an id from an argument, `put <c> $n` and `insert <c> $n`, `else set`: each refused at definition;
- dropping a unique index a statement's `if absent` relies on: refused; with the statement replaced first, in one block: lands;
- the definition replaced between the question and the lock at every `is_read_only()` site of the table: the call runs one version whole, under the write lock;
- `rebuild` (`fennec_abi::schema`'s add, `set`, drop and rename) under a statement: lands where the statement still checks at the commit, refused naming it where not; a follower's re-copy of a collection under a statement: marked broken, never a stopped follower;
- the studio's splitter over a definition: one statement, sent whole;
- stored statements across a grammar epoch: the fixture opens, a changed digest marks broken, an unchanged one calls;
- `call_<name>` tools listed by the official MCP client and their names held to Claude's and OpenAI's patterns;
- a scoped `/batch` of two calls, and of a call beside a `get`: 400;
- a body naming `_consumers`, `_follow`, `_idempotency`: refused at definition;
- the upgrade's order: an archiver and a replica built without kind 13 stop with words at the first definition;
- a scoped token's transfer, granted only `transfer call`: moves money it could not move with `/batch` (403 there), eight clients keeping the sum as `tests/require.rs` does;
- an ungranted name and a missing one: the same 404; `* call` refused at startup; a line naming no statement logged;
- a call past `--call-max-rows` put back; a subject past `--call-rate` answered 429 while another subject is not;
- field lists not masking a call's answer; a scoped caller's error told; `as` from a scoped token 403; an `append-only` delete 403.

**What CLAUDE.md would gain**, a section *Named statements* after *Writes*, each rule linking here:

> **[A named statement is a record of its database, checked against its schema and marked broken rather than failing an open.](docs/design/named-statements.md#definitions)** `create statement` writes record kind 13, a write `writes_in` counts, undone in a block (`Undo::Statement`), kept by every image with its grammar epoch and its tree's digest, and applied by a replica; its version is `(history, change)`. A body holds `get`, `insert`, `set ... where` and `del ... where` alone -- no `put`, no documents from a parameter, no `else set`, no schema change, no `call`, no `_` collection -- its last statement names its fields, and a writing scan says `scans`. A schema change breaking one is refused at its commit; an internal writer, a changed digest or a build without the feature marks it broken. A replacement keeps whether it writes, its claims and its answer's fields.
>
> **[A call runs its statement's text with the statement's own authority.](docs/design/named-statements.md#authority)** It is judged tenant, token, grant, existence, claims, arguments, `check`. `scoped()` passes a `call` only where a policy line names it (404 otherwise, as a statement no database holds; `* call` is refused), binds its declared claims from the token (a claim missing, null, empty or of another type is 403) and refuses `as` and an `append-only` update or delete; it is the one statement run outside `within()`, alone in a scoped token's `/batch`, and a scoped caller is told a refusal's kind, `at` and `reason` alone.
>
> **[A call is one block, a write at every site, its arguments values.](docs/design/named-statements.md#calls)** `Statement::Call` is never read-only before the lock; only the read-only executor, over `&Database`, reads a definition again and answers a read. Arguments are checked against the signature (400) and bound by place, never spliced; the answer is the last statement's; a call examines at most `--call-max-rows` rows and a subject makes at most `--call-rate` a second. A sync replica refuses `call` and `create statement`.
>
> **[Every surface names a call alike.](docs/design/named-statements.md#surfaces)** `POST /_call/<name>`, FennecQL's `call`, SQL's `CALL`, the SDKs' `call` (Dart's `callStatement`) held to the golden file; MCP's `call_<name>` tools, a writing one under `--mcp-write` and an answer past the bounds put back; counted as the shape `call <name> $1`, traced with `db.stored_procedure.name`, and no metric label names one. Readers upgrade before a primary defines one.

And *Declared schemas*' "plans additions alone" becomes "plans additions, and a statement's replacement where its contract holds".

## What it would measure

No figure above is this feature's own. What each slice owes, in turns against main: a call against the `/batch` of its statements under the server's token (the parse skipped: a row by id parses in 0.8-0.9 us and a filtered `get` in 2.4-2.5, [http.md](http.md#parse-cache), [fennecql.md](fennecql.md#sql-joins-subqueries-and-schema)) and under a scoped token (the scope's rewrite and `Check` skipped: a scoped `set` costs 1.6-1.9 us more than the server token's in process, [access.md](access.md#scoped-tokens)) -- `make policy-bench` and `make requests-bench`; eight clients' transfers a second as a call and as a scoped `/batch` (`make counters-bench`'s shape); a held claim as a call against `make queue-bench`'s; an open of 1 000 statements, each parsed and its digest checked (`make opens-bench`); a schema change's commit over a collection 1 000 statements depend on; the row count's cost on a call's scan; the module's bytes with and without `statements` (`make wasm-sizes`), and the server's, the router's and the shell's. Every path that holds no call -- a `get`, a `put`, a `/batch` -- must measure as it did: a statement form costs the parser a word and the engine a match arm, the policy a line kind it skips, and a schema change a look at a table that is empty.

## Decisions for the owner

1. **Where a definition lives.** *Recommended: in the database file*, a record of kind 13, replicated, archived, moved and restored with the data and checked against its schema; a tenant's statements are its own. The alternative, the server's policy file, is one API for every tenant of a node and goes unchecked when a schema changes.
2. **Whose authority a call runs under.** *Recommended: the statement's text, with typed declared claims* -- granted by name, no default grant, claims missing when null, empty or mistyped, the one statement outside `within()`. The alternatives -- the caller's rules (invoker), their intersection with the statement's, a role per statement -- each leave a transfer unable to do what it is for or put a second layer between the reader and what a statement may do.
3. **A replacement that changes the contract** -- whether it writes, the claims it reads, the fields it answers. *Recommended: refused, written as `drop` and `create`*, so no grant starts meaning more without a line of the policy changing, and the `drop` leaves every grant naming it logged. The alternative: allowed, logged as an audit event of its own, which keeps a statement's name and grants through such a change and asks operators to read the log.
4. **Sync replicas in the first version.** *Recommended: `call` and `create statement` refused in both runners*, from the slice that brings the syntax; the app calls its server over HTTP and the streams bring back what the call wrote. The alternative, forwarding from the replica, needs settling by the collections a body writes, a barrier in the queue and an asynchronous native path ([above](#forwarding-calls-from-a-sync-replica)), each a scenario of its own.
5. **The browser module.** *Recommended: the record in every build; defining and calling in a `statements` feature outside `full`, refused in the shipped module unless measured within a budget the owner sets* -- 3 KB brotli is suggested, against the review's 6-9 KB estimate, which would keep them out of the module for now. The alternatives: core in every build, which pays 6-9 KB for every page and gives a Durable Object calls; or never in the module.

## Write-bound statements (F2)

**Status: built, slices 1 to 6 -- F2 complete ([below](#what-f2-has-built)).** The form reads into its tree; a binding's record, op 3, is read, kept and applied by every build; the server's token defines one at every way in and its writes run it, in their block, once a row; a scoped token's write or call wakes one only where a policy's `wake` line grants it, the binding's writes its text's authority, its runs charged to `--call-rate`; what they did is named in every error the database's own authority meets, counted, logged, traced and audited, listed with the schema to the server's token and held by both sync runners' scenarios (slice 5); every SDK's error names the binding that refused a write, the studio lists a collection's bindings and opens each as its replacement, and the shell's `.help` gives the form (slice 6). A named statement bound to the writes of one collection, run in the block of the write that woke it, once a row, with that row as `new.` and `old.`: a ledger line, a counter, a denormalized field, kept with the write that made them in the record that holds it. This section decides what [F2's paragraph](#what-it-does-not-do) left open. It changes no rule of CLAUDE.md now; the rule comes with the slices that build it ([below](#what-claudemd-would-gain-from-f2)).

```fennecql
create statement paid_ledger
  after set orders where new.status = 'paid' and old.status = 'open'
  comment 'A ledger line and the debit for each order as it is paid'
as begin
  get accounts select id where id = new.account limit 1 require 1 else 'no such account';
  insert ledger {order: new.id, account: new.account, amount: new.total, at: now()};
  set accounts {balance: balance - new.total} where id = new.account
end
```

A customer's token granted `update(status)` on its own orders and `paid_ledger wake`, and nothing on `ledger` or `accounts`, pays an order with a `set`; the ledger line and the debit land in that write's block, or nothing does.

### The form of a binding

**A clause of `create statement`, not an object of its own.** `create [or replace] statement <name> after <event> [or <event> ...] <collection> [where <condition>] [comment '<text>'] [scans] as <body>`, an event being `insert`, `set` or `del` -- SQL's `update` and `delete` read as `set` and `del` there, as they do as statements ([fennecql.md](fennecql.md#sql-spellings)). The binding is its statement's: its name is the statement's, its record the statement's record, its digest covers it, and `drop statement` takes both away. Rejected:

- *`create binding <b> on orders after set call ledger(new.id, new.total)`*, a binding naming a callable statement: two records with a dependency between them, the condition that wakes a body and the body in two texts read apart -- where F1's one rule is that the text is the authority -- and the statement it names callable, so a token granted `ledger call` would write the ledger line with no order paid. Refusing a bound statement's calls would leave the second object nothing to add.
- *SQL's `CREATE TRIGGER`*, refused naming `create statement ... after` without consuming its word, as `CREATE FUNCTION` is: PostgreSQL's trigger runs a function as whoever fired it unless that function is `SECURITY DEFINER`, before, after or instead of the write, for each row or each statement, and fires the triggers its own writes wake. Read as ours, each of those would mean something else, which the SQL-spellings rule forbids.
- *`before`*, a binding changing the document as it is written (PostgreSQL's `BEFORE ROW` trigger returning a new row). A document is encoded before the write lock where no hook rewrites it ([engine.md](engine.md#prepared-blocks), `Hook::rewrites`) and judged as written by a scoped token's rules (`before_overwrite`); a field worked out from its own row is a generated field, a proposal of its own.

**Events are what happened to a row, never the statement that did it.** A counter of orders asks whether an order was made, not whether its client said `put` or `insert`, and the write path knows the effect already (`WriteOp`):

| The write | `insert`: a row made | `set`: a row written over | `del`: a row taken out |
|---|---|---|---|
| `insert`, `insert ... if absent` | each row it makes, over an expired row too | -- | -- |
| `insert ... if absent else set` | each row it makes, over an expired row too | each live row it sets | -- |
| `put` | a document naming no id, an id not held, or an id whose row is past its `@ttl` time | a document naming an id whose row is alive | -- |
| `set`, picked or not | -- | each row it sets | -- |
| `del`, picked or not | -- | -- | each row it deletes |
| REST's routes, MCP's `write`, a held write, a call's body | as the statement each is | | |
| `@ttl`'s sweep; an expired row a write clears for its `@unique` value | -- | -- | none: every read left the row out at its time |

`new.` is the row as the write leaves it -- a `put`'s document, a `set`'s row as set -- and `old.` the row as it stood, the document `before_overwrite` is handed. An `insert` binding reads `new.` alone, a `del` binding `old.` alone, a `set` binding both. `new.` is the spelling an upsert's `else set` and a policy's transitions already give the row as written ([writes.md](writes.md#upserts), [access.md](access.md#scoped-tokens)); `old.` is new, since a body's bare names are the rows of the collections it writes. A statement may bind several events of one collection (`after insert or set orders`) and reads only what every one of them has: `old.` in a binding of `insert or set` is refused at definition, as `new.` in one of `set or del`, never read as `null` for the event lacking it, whose comparison would be unknown and wake nothing without a word. One collection a statement: `new.` names one schema's fields, and two collections are two statements.

**An expiry is no event, and a binding promising to see one is refused.** A row past its `@ttl` time is gone to every read at that time ([indexes.md](indexes.md#ttl)) and taken out later by the sweep, which wakes nothing (below). The write path marks a write over such a row's id an update -- `put` reads the expired row as `old` and writes over it (`gone`, `WriteOp::Update`) -- but a binding is told what every read saw: a row made, an `insert` with no `old.`. Taken from the raw `WriteOp`, a `set` binding would read an `old.` no read could, and an `insert` counter would miss the row. So no binding ever sees a row expire: not by the sweep, not by a write clearing an expired `@unique` holder, not as a write over one. What that leaves unkept is a `del` binding's promise to see every row its collection loses -- a count kept by `after insert` and `after del` would count every expired row for ever -- so `after del` on a `@ttl` collection is refused at definition, and `create index ... @ttl` on a collection an `after del` binding is bound to is refused at its commit, naming the binding, as any schema change breaking a statement is; an internal writer making the collection `@ttl` marks the binding broken, which refuses its writes (below). `insert` and `set` bindings hold on a `@ttl` collection: every row made is an `insert`, and a `set` never finds an expired row, every filter having `alive` ANDed in.
