An `after expire` event is [the owner's](#f2s-decisions-for-the-owner) and left out.

**The condition is a filter over the pair.** `where` reads `new.<field>`, `old.<field>` and `now()` alone -- a bare name has no row to be read from, and is refused -- and is bound to the collection as a read's filter is ([indexes.md](indexes.md#bound-filters)): each field compares in its collation and its case fold as a `get` compares it (`Field::text_order`), so `new.status = 'PAID'` over a `nocase` field wakes where `get orders where status = 'PAID'` finds the row. No inner `get`, aggregate or `expired()` in it: a read of another collection is the body's guard (`get ... require`), whose refusal can say why.

**The body is F1's, with three rules more.** It holds what a callable statement's holds -- `get`, `insert`, `set ... where` and `del ... where`, never `put`, a parameter's documents, `else set`, a schema change, `call` or a `_` collection ([definitions](#definitions)), reading through an index or saying `scans` -- its `new.<field>`s and `old.<field>`s numbered as places in the order they first appear, so the body is the same `Expr::Param`s every statement holds and the engine's paths take it unchanged. A bound statement has no parameters, no `claims`, no `check`, no `public` and no parentheses: its values are the row's, its condition the `where`, and its writes are on behalf of whoever wrote the row, the server's own token included, which carries no claim. Then:

- *Its rows go nowhere*, so `returning` is refused, and so is a `get` without `require`: a guard is the one thing a read does there.
- *It writes no collection it is bound to.* A write into the rows its writer just wrote would change what the writer was answered (`returning`) and what its `require` counted, and a `set` over the collection its writer is setting would move rows the writer is still walking -- the Halloween problem. A field worked out from its own row is a generated field.
- *It writes no collection another statement is bound to.* A binding's writes wake nothing ([depth one](#a-bindings-authority)), so a binding inserting into `ledger` while another is bound to `ledger`'s inserts would leave the second asleep for every line the first wrote. Refused at definition in both orders -- a binding writing a bound collection, and a binding of a collection one writes -- naming the other, as a policy's rules reading each other's collections are refused at startup (`ring`, [access.md](access.md#rules-reading-another-collection)). PostgreSQL's triggers cascade, and SQLite's too (its `recursive_triggers`, off by default, stops only a trigger waking itself), which makes what a write does a graph to walk; a refusal can be lifted later, a cascade never taken back.

A collection is bound by at most 16 statements, refused past it, never cut ([crates.md](crates.md#limits)), and a collection the database calls its own (`declared::own`, a `_` name) by none.

### When a binding runs

**At the end of the statement that woke it, inside its block, before the block lands.** `run_one` runs a statement -- each row through the hooks (`before_write`, `before_overwrite`, `after_write`, `answering`) and the `@unique` check, its `require` counted and its answer made, as today -- then the bindings its rows woke, then spills where the block is due, and the block lands as one record: a crash, a replica and a subscriber see the write and what it woke together or neither ([engine.md](engine.md#blocks)). After the statement and never inside its loop over rows: `require` counts the statement's own rows, `returning` answers them as the statement wrote them, and no binding moves a row the statement has yet to walk. A `/batch`'s next statement reads what the bindings wrote, as it reads what the statement before it wrote; the writer's `Fennec-Seq` is the block's, the derived writes among it; an `Idempotency-Key`'s row lands in the same block, so a retry is answered what the first try was and wakes nothing again. A binding never changes the document its writer wrote, so a block prepared beside the readers lands the bytes it encoded, and the bindings are read under the write lock, from the table as it stands there, as a call's definition is. Nothing a binding does is past a block's undo -- its writes are rows the block's marks take back, and op 3 (below) is a definition `Undo::Statements` puts back as op 1's -- so `Statement::fits_block` is as it was.

**Once a row, in the order the statement wrote them, and on each row its bindings in name order.** Each row is asked each binding's condition as it is written, beside `after_write` -- the condition reads `new.`, `old.` and `now()` alone, nothing the statement is still changing -- and only a row passing one is kept, with its pair and the bindings it passed (`Database::woken`, asked only where the collection's mask holds the event, [below](#what-a-binding-costs-a-write)): a `set` over a million rows of which twelve are paid keeps twelve pairs, never a million `old` rows cloned for a check at the end. At the statement's end each kept row runs the bindings it passed, each body as a call's body runs, each statement through `run_one` with the row's values by place: rows first, then names, as PostgreSQL fires the `AFTER ROW` triggers a statement queued row by row and same-event ones by name ([trigger behavior](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/trigger-definition.html)). A run sees what the rows before it, and the bindings before it on its row, wrote. Once a statement -- every row handed to one run, PostgreSQL's transition tables (`REFERENCING NEW TABLE`) -- was weighed: a `put` of 1 000 likes would bump a post's counter once rather than write it 1 000 times into one record. FennecQL has no relation to hand a body, and an `in` over the `new.id`s is a list where a row's fields are the point; it is a follow-up of its own form, once a bulk write's record shows the cost. SQLite runs row triggers alone ([CREATE TRIGGER](https://www.sqlite.org/lang_createtrigger.html)).

**Bounded, refused past it, never cut.** A statement wakes at most `named::MAX_WOKEN` rows (10 000, the number `near`, `match` and a facet hold to), a row counted once whatever number of bindings it passed: the 10 001st row passing a condition refuses the write as it is written, naming the bound, and its block is put back -- never landed with the first rows' derived writes, and never more than 10 000 pairs held. The runs of one statement examine at most `--call-max-rows` rows together, its rows the thread's (`call::LEFT`): a fresh budget for a statement a client sent, set as the first run starts and given back as the last ends, on a panic too; inside a call's body, what the call has left, never a fresh budget a body could multiply by its statements. So the write lock a write holds is bounded by its rows and that budget. `now()` is the waking statement's, read once as its first row is asked a condition, so the condition and every run of one write read one time. A mass `del` of a bound collection is split by its writer, or the binding dropped first; a load goes in unbound (below).

**What wakes one, and what never does.** `run_one` is the one place a binding wakes, and every statement a client, an app or a call sends passes there: `/query`, `/batch`, REST's routes, MCP's `write`, a held write's look, a call's body, the native library, the shell. The writers holding no client's write never reach it, or run unbound:

- *Never reaching it.* A replica's `apply` and an archive's restore take records, not statements, and the record holds what the binding wrote: running it again would write it twice. An open, an image adopted, a move's install and a promotion load a file. A compact and the graph keeper write no document. `@ttl`'s sweep (`Database::sweep`, which deletes through `delete`, beneath every statement) and a write clearing an expired row for its `@unique` value (`erase`) take out rows every read left out at their time: a binding over expiry would fire when the sweeper got there, not when the row expired, and [F3](#scheduled-statements-f3)'s scheduled statements are the form for acting on time.
- *Unbound* (`Database::unbound(|db| ...)`, as `internally` runs a writer that cannot refuse a schema change, and implied by it). `--follow`'s mirror, whose collections copy another database's rows -- their derived rows are their source's, and a copy again would wake every row once more. `fennec import`, a load, whose derived rows are a backfill the operator runs as a statement. A declared schema's migrations (`declared::migration_texts`, a `rebuild`'s `set` among them), which write what their text says and not what a binding declared beside them would make of it in the plan's order. The sync's apply of a stream's rows, and its remake.

The CVEs' danger -- one authority's code run by another's action -- is closed here at definition, which the server's token alone makes, so no internal writer runs anyone else's text. What the two lists keep an internal writer from is writing derived rows twice, or at a time no write chose.

### A binding's authority

**Its definer's -- the text -- never the writer's.** A bound statement's body runs as `run_as` runs a call, its writes checked by no token's scope, so a customer's `set` writes a ledger line no write rule of the customer's grants -- once a `wake` line grants the binding (below). Its authority is its text, defined by the server's token alone as every definition is. None calls one: `call` of a bound statement is the 404 a missing one is to a scoped token and a 400 naming why to the server's, a policy's `call` line naming one is logged as the server starts and grants nothing, and MCP lists no tool for it.

**A scoped token wakes only the bindings its policy grants it, by name.** Without a grant, any token that may write `orders` would write whatever `orders`' bindings write with no line of the policy naming them, and a statement bound later would widen what a grant made for something else lets a token cause: what F1's grants by name, and its refusal of a replacement widening a statement ([decision 3](#decisions-for-the-owner)), keep out. So a policy line `<statement>[, <statement> ...] wake [for <role>]` grants waking, as a `call` line grants calling:

```text
# statements     access   role
paid_ledger      wake     for customer
count_likes      wake     for member
```

`wake` stands alone on its line, `* wake` is refused with or without a role, and a line naming a statement no database holds, or a callable one, is logged and grants nothing, as `call`'s lines are (`calls::log_unheld`). A scoped token's write that wakes a binding its roles are not granted is refused (403) before the binding runs, its block put back: a binding never skips, and woken it runs or its write is refused. It is asked as a binding wakes -- a row of the statement passing its condition -- not of every write of its event: a courier moving an order's `location` wakes no `paid_ledger` and needs no grant, and one able to set `status` to `paid` is refused unless granted. **But where the condition reads a field the token is not shown, it is asked of every write of the event, a row passing or not.** Asked of passing rows alone, a condition reading `old.cap` that a customer's `read(holder, note)` leaves out answered a 403 where the card was over the cap and a 200 where it was not: a bit of a withheld value a write, at the token's choosing. So the engine hands the hooks each binding a row reached by its event, its rows 0 where none passed (`Woken::reached`), and the `Check` hook judges one with none where its condition reads a field some read rule over the collection leaves out of its list, or any but `id` of a collection no read rule reaches (`Scope::reads_unshown`): fail closed, the grant and `append-only` asked as if it woke, nothing charged, since nothing runs. A condition reading only what the token is shown keeps being asked as a row passes, as the courier's `location` above needs. Row filters are not weighed: a write's rows are found through the token's own rules, and a field list is what withholds a field of a row it writes. The 403 names no binding to the token, as an ungranted statement is no name to it (F1's 404); the audit's `unwoken` line names it (`audit::unwoken`), for the operator writing the grant. `/_whoami` lists a token's `wakes` beside its `calls`. A scoped token's call is held the same, before its body runs: `judge_call` refuses (403) one whose body may wake, by the collections and events it writes, a binding the caller is not granted -- a call's writes are fixed by its text, so what they may wake is known before they run, where a write's rows are not. The server's token wakes every binding, as it calls every statement; the native library and the shell hold no policy.


**The writer's own write is judged first, by its own rules.** The writer's statement runs within its scope as ever -- `scoped()`, the field lists (`fields::hold`, `fields::write`), the `Check` hook over each row with its transitions, `append-only` -- so a row its rules refuse is a 403 before any binding is asked, and a write that wrote no row wakes nothing. Then the engine runs the bindings its rows woke:

- *`Hook::waking`*, asked of each binding a row of the statement reached by its event once the statement's rows are written and before the first binding runs, handed its definition -- its name, its condition and its body's statements, whose `set`s and `del`s name the collections it updates or deletes in -- and the rows that woke it, 0 where none passed the condition (above), with the writer's scope in place: where the policy meets a binding. The `Check` hook refuses (403) a binding the token is not granted (`wake`, above) and one updating or deleting in an `append-only` collection, since `append-only` binds every scoped token ([access.md](access.md#scoped-tokens)), and charges its runs to the subject's `--call-rate` (below). `judge_call` asks the same of each binding a call's body may wake.
- *`Hook::writer_only`*, asked once, as a hook is registered: a hook judging whoever wrote rather than what was written -- the `Check` hook -- says so, and the engine passes over it for a binding's writes. The other hooks run on them: a plugin generating embeddings writes them into a binding's insert as into anyone's, and fennec-server's guard of a mirrored collection refuses a binding writing into one, putting the writer's block back -- its `ReadOnly` names the collection, so a scoped writer is told it as `told_bound` tells every kind but a quota's and the disk's: its status and no words of the engine's. The registry keeps beside its hooks the list of those not judging their writer (`Registry::swap_beside`), and the engine swaps it into their place around a binding's runs and back after them, with the clock and `unbound`, inside the runs' `catch_unwind`, a panic included: a write nothing binds asks nothing of it, where a flag would be a look every write's hooks paid, and nothing is taken away that a panic could leave taken -- as F1's slice 3 found its row bound left set.

**A scoped writer's runs are charged to its `--call-rate`.** A binding's run is a body run under the write lock on a token's behalf, as a call's is, so each run a scoped token's write wakes is charged to its subject's bucket in that database (`calls::admitted`), the server's token unheld: past it the write is answered 429 with `Retry-After` and `retry_after`, its block put back. A write waking more runs than a full bucket holds -- a second's worth -- would wait for ever, so it is refused (400, naming `--call-rate` and the runs), never told to come back, as a quota where waiting makes no room is a refusal ([quotas.md](quotas.md#quotas-in-the-sync)). A call's runs are its caller's, charged beside the call. The 429 hands back the runs the request was charged before it, since none landed: a scoped request's writes are one block -- `/query`, REST and MCP's `write` one statement, a `/batch` one block, since a scoped token compacts nothing (`handle_batch` asserts it in a debug build), a held write's every try charged afresh -- and a path landing a scoped request's statements as blocks of their own would owe a refund of the block put back alone.

So access's "a scoped write run outside `within()` goes unchecked, a named statement's call alone" gains a second case, a binding's writes, held by the same kind of test: each part taken out, the transfer's sum broken or a write refused.

**`new.` is the writer's document.** Whatever a binding does with `new.<field>`, a token that may write that field chooses: a binding debiting `new.account` debits the account a customer's order names, so the policy over the bound collection is the binding's guard -- `orders` insertable only `where account in $jwt.accounts` -- with the binding's own condition and guards, as a callable statement's `where`s are a call's. Every such input must be held, as a `SECURITY DEFINER` function's arguments must: the first example the access page gave let a customer insert any `total`, and an order of a negative total, paid, credited the account its `set accounts {balance: balance - new.total}` debited. The page's policy now inserts an order `open` with a `total` above zero and moves it from `open` to `paid` alone, `total` and `account` fixed by the field list. The writer's identity reaches a binding the same way: a policy holding `new.by = $jwt.sub` over the row the writer writes (a transition) makes `new.by` the writer, which the binding copies into its audit line. A binding reading the writer's claims (`$jwt.sub`) was weighed: the server's token, an app's native library and an internal writer carry none, so a binding reading one would refuse the server's own writes (F1's missing claim, 403) or read `null` into a filter, the hole a missing claim must never leave -- both worse than a row the policy holds already.

**What it reads goes nowhere.** A binding answers nothing: its last statement's rows are dropped and `returning` refused, so no writer is answered a row through it. What it writes is its definer's to show: a binding copying `old.email` into a collection a token reads shows it to that token, as a callable statement's answer is the statement's ([authority](#authority)). A guard's refusal tells its writer one bit and the `reason` the text wrote (below).

**Depth one.** A binding's writes wake nothing: its statements run through `run_one` with waking off, keeping no row; with a chain refused at definition, depth one never leaves a binding asleep without a word.

### When a binding refuses

**The block is put back whole, and the writer told a binding refused.** A binding's error -- a guard's `require` unmet (412), a `@unique` value taken (409), `MAX_WOKEN` or `--call-max-rows` (400), a broken binding (409), and for a scoped writer a binding not granted or reaching an `append-only` collection (403) and `--call-rate` (429, or 400 past a second's worth) -- is its writer's statement's: `run_one` returns it, and the block -- the statement, the bindings run before, a `/batch`'s statements before it -- is put back as any block whose statement fails ([engine.md](engine.md#blocks)). A tenant's quota is asked of the record as it lands, the derived rows' bytes and rows in it and charged to the writer's thread (`writing_here`), and its 507 names the bound alone, as ever ([quotas.md](quotas.md#tenant-quotas)). The status is the binding's error's, and `at` the writer's statement's place in its `/batch`, since the binding ran as part of it. The engine notes the binding that refused (`Database::woken`), and each writer is told:

| Writer | Told |
|---|---|
| the server's token, the native library, the shell | the engine's words after "bound statement `paid_ledger`, after `set` on `orders`, refused this write:", and `binding: "paid_ledger"` beside `at` and `reason` |
| a scoped token | the status, `at`, and the `reason` its guard's `else` wrote -- the definer's words, written to be told -- in "a write bound to this one refused it" (`told_bound`, as `told_call` tells a call's), never the binding's name or the engine's words |

A scoped writer is told no name because F1 tells a token no name it was not granted (a missing statement and an ungranted one are one 404): a name says what the schema does -- `fraud_check` -- and a unique clash's words name values the token may not read. The name and the words are the trace's and, past `--slow-ms`, the slow line's, under the request id. Each SDK's error gains `binding`, `null` but for the server's token.

**A binding that cannot run refuses the writes it binds.** A broken binding -- a text reading here as another statement, a dependency an internal writer took away, one a process with no reader installed holds unread -- is neither run nor passed over: a write of its event on its collection is refused (409, "the write would wake `paid_ledger`, which is broken: ... define it again or drop it"), its condition unasked, since a broken binding's condition is no more to be trusted than its body. F1's broken statement is refused to its callers; a binding's callers are its writers. Letting the write land without its rows leaves a ledger silently short. An ordinary writer cannot break one -- its schema change is refused at its commit, as for a callable statement -- and an internal writer that marks one broken writes unbound.

### A binding's record, replicas and upgrades

**Kind 13, op 3: a bound definition, which a binary from before refuses.** `[13][0][length][3][history][change][epoch][digest][name][depends][collection][events][text]`: op 1's fields, then the bound collection's name and its events (a byte: 1 `insert`, 2 `set`, 4 `del`) outside the text, so a build that cannot parse the text knows what it binds. Its drop is op 2, as any statement's. F1's reader refuses an op it does not read as corrupt ([slice 2](#what-is-built)), so an F1 binary -- a replica, an archiver, a node a tenant moves to, a standby a failover would promote -- stops at the first binding in words. Read as op 1, the record would be a callable statement whose text no longer reads, marked broken while the writes it binds land without their rows on the first F1 node a failover promoted: a misread the file format forbids ([storage.md](storage.md#file-format)). A new kind was weighed: kind 13 carries the version, the epoch, the digest, the table and `Undo::Statements` already, and an op is a number that binary refuses all the same. `writes_in` counts op 3 as one write as it counts op 1, so a replica's counter stays its primary's; an image writes a bound definition with op 3, a compact keeps it and `changes_in` passes over it. The canonical tree gains the binding's fields, each written only where it holds something, so no stored digest moves and `GRAMMAR_EPOCH` stays where it is (2), and `make fennecql-corpus` holds every text the repository holds to main's tree.

**A replica never runs a binding: the record holds what it wrote.** A follower applies the block -- the order paid, the ledger line, the debit -- as written, and op 3 into its table, so a promoted copy runs the bindings from its first write. `/_changes` tells the derived rows as the writes they are, in the change range of the write that made them; an archive holds them, and a restore and a move carry them. A query across tenants reads, and wakes nothing.

**Its dependencies are F1's, the bound collection among them.** `depends` names the bound collection and each field `new.` and `old.` read, beside what the body names: dropping `orders`, or its `status`, is refused naming the binding while it stands, and a `rebuild` lands where the binding still checks at its block's commit. A replacement keeps the kind -- a callable statement replaced by a bound one, or the other way, is refused naming the `drop` and `create` it needs, since a `call` or `wake` line naming it would name something else -- and a binding's contract, so a `wake` line grants what it granted: the collection and events it is bound to, and what it writes, each collection its body inserts into, sets or deletes in, by the kind of write (`Definition::breaks_contract`). Within it -- a condition, a value, a guard, a field a `set` writes -- a replacement needs no new grant, as a callable statement's within its contract needs none; one bound to another collection or event, or writing a collection or a kind of write it did not, is refused naming the `drop` and `create` it needs, whose `drop` leaves each `wake` line naming it logged, as F1's decision 3 has it. A condition widened is within it: the grant is to cause what the binding writes, and the grantee's own rules over the bound collection hold which rows it writes.

**Upgrades go one way, every reader before the first binding.** The order is F1's ([upgrades](#replicas-sync-the-router-and-upgrades)): archivers, routers, every node, and then a primary may define one. `make upgrade-check` gains a base, the last main before op 3, whose replica must stop at op 3 in words and connect again, whose archiver keeps the stream's bytes and takes no image of its own, whose `restore` and server refuse the archive and the file, whose router refuses a schema declaring a binding (400, nothing placed), and onto whose node a move fails at the install, the tenant left on its source. RELEASING.md's notes name `after` as the clause that moves the format.

### A binding beside sync replicas and the module

**A sync replica sends the write and receives what its bindings wrote through its streams: no new code refuses it.** A sync replica holds its shapes, never its server's statements -- the streams pass over kind 13 -- so it cannot know which of its writes wake a binding, and runs none. Its optimistic write is that write alone, queued and sent as any synced write, which the server runs in a block with the bindings it wakes. The derived rows come back through the shapes following their collections, in the record numbered as the write's own, so a shape past the write's `Fennec-Seq` holds them. A binding's refusal (412, 409) puts the optimistic write back as any refusal of it does, with its `reason`, and a 507 naming a `quota` is a quota's ([quotas.md](quotas.md#quotas-in-the-sync)). `NAMED` refuses a binding's definition on a synced database as it refuses any `create statement`. Rejected:

- *Refusing a synced write that would wake a binding*, by a code beside `NAMED`: the replica would need its server's bindings in the shapes' handshake to know them, and an app taking orders offline -- what a sync replica is for -- could not write a bound collection at all.
- *Running the binding optimistically on the replica*: its rows are a filtered part of its server's, so the debit would find no account its shapes do not hold and a guard would count the replica's rows -- why it refuses `GUARDED`, `PICKED` and `UPSERT_KEY` writes -- and its derived rows would differ from the server's until the streams replaced them.

`integrations/sync-scenarios.json` gains the cases before either runner changes ([native-and-sync.md](native-and-sync.md#sync-scenarios)): a synced `set` waking a binding, the derived row absent from the optimistic view and arriving through a shape following its collection; a binding's guard refusing, the write put back with its `reason`; a queued write sent again under its `Idempotency-Key` after its answer was lost, answered the first try's answer from `_idempotency` with its binding run once -- one ledger line in the stream, never two; a synced database refusing a binding's definition (`NAMED`). Both runners are expected to pass them as they are, the native core's apply of a stream's rows running unbound, where a local database could hold no binding anyway.

**The browser module runs none, and refuses the writes a binding binds.** Bindings are F1's `statements` feature, outside `full` and out of the module ([the browser module](#the-browser-module-and-embedded)), which refuses defining and calling already. But a page opens a server's file (`openFile`) and writes it, and a write there landing without its derived rows would go back to the server a ledger short. So the record's binding is core, as op 1's record is: every build reads op 3's collection and events into the collection's mask, and a build that cannot run bindings refuses a write of a bound event on a bound collection before it changes anything, the condition unread ("`orders` has statements bound to its writes, which this module does not run: write it through a server"), as `collate::refuse` refuses a statement reaching a chunk the page lacks. A Durable Object running the module writes no bound collection, and a FennecSync replica holds no binding (above). Measured in its slice (`make size-report BASE=origin/main`), against the 824 bytes brotli op 1's record cost.

### Held writes wake on derived rows

**A derived write wakes the groups reading what it wrote, through what is there already.** `Waits::wrote` is called by the database's watcher with the collections the landed block noted (`changed_collections_since`), and a binding's writes are notes of that block: a claim held on `ledger` looks when a `set orders` lands a ledger line, the row going to the group ranking it first (`waits::settle`), with nothing new in fennec-http. A held `set` or `del` on a bound collection is held as ever and wakes its bindings when it writes, in its look's block; whether it wrote is asked of the database (`waits::Mark`), which counts the derived writes too and is right to, a write writing no row of its own waking nothing. `--max-memory`'s ceiling and `grows` ask what a write may grow by its bindings as by its own statement: a `del` of a collection whose binding inserts is held as the insert it makes (`over_ceiling`, as it asks `call_body` of a call). A test holds the wake: a claim held on `ledger` answered by a `set orders`, failing with the bindings' notes taken out.

### Watching bindings

- *The slow line* names the bindings a request woke, each with its version and rows (`bound: [["paid_ledger", [h, c], 12]]`); its time is the request's, which holds theirs. A scoped writer's refused write is logged with the binding's name and words, which its writer was not told.
- *Traces*: a child span a binding a statement -- never a row -- its `db.operation.name` `bound`, `db.stored_procedure.name` the name, `fennec.rows_woken` its rows. The engine times a binding's runs together, two clock reads a binding a statement and none where nothing woke, and hands them over on the request's thread (`Database::take_runs`, as built: [slice 5](#what-f2-has-built)), since the trace's thread-local is fennec-http's.
- *`/_stats/statements`* counts each binding as a shape of its own, `after paid_ledger`, its count the statements that woke it, its rows theirs, its time its runs', beside the writer's shape, whose time holds it: an operator tuning a slow `set orders` sees what of it was the ledger. Read as every shape is ([http.md](http.md#statement-counts)).
- *`/_metrics`*: `fennec_bound_runs_total` and `fennec_bound_refused_total`, no label naming a binding or a collection ([http.md](http.md#metrics)).
- *The audit*: `create statement ... after`, its replacement and its drop are schema changes logged by their shapes (`audit::statement`), the line naming the bound collection and events. A run is no event, as a write is none; a scoped writer refused through `Hook::waking` is a 403 logged as any.
- *Listings*: `statements` gains `after` (the collection and events, or `null`) and `describe statement` the condition; `GET /_schema` lists a collection's bindings beside it to the server's token, `?as=fennecql` writing them after the collections. A scoped token sees none but the names its `wake` lines grant, in `/_whoami`'s `wakes`.

### What a binding costs a write

**A collection with no binding pays one byte's look a writing statement, never a row's.** Each collection keeps a byte, `Collection::bound`, the events its bindings wake, set wherever the table or the collections change (`Database::rebind`: a definition, a drop, `Undo::Statements`, an open, a replica's apply, an image adopted), and the write path reads it once a statement, where it asks `unique` once a statement today. Where the byte lacks the event, no pair is kept, no `old` cloned and no condition asked, `woken` stays empty and `run_one` looks at one more byte as the statement ends (`Database::bound_due`), nothing as it starts and nothing a row. Where it holds it, each row is asked the conditions as it is written, and only a row passing one keeps its pair -- a `set` the `old` it reads for the hooks already, a `del` the document `after_write` is handed -- so what a write holds is bounded by `MAX_WOKEN`, never by the rows it walks. Held by `a_write_to_an_unbound_collection_keeps_no_row` -- a count of the pairs kept, under `#[cfg(test)]`, staying 0 over every write form on one collection while another is bound -- and measured in turns against main, as F1's slices 3 and 5 were: in process and through `fennec_http::handle`, a `put`, an `insert`, a `set` and a `del` by id, a `put` of 1 000 documents and a `set` over a scan, each the median of nine rounds over five pairs, a pair run under someone else's load (`uptime`) discarded; `make requests-bench`; `make size-report` for the module and `fennec-server`. A path holding no binding measures as it did, or the slice is not done.

**What a binding costs is held to what it saves.** A `set` waking one binding that inserts a row, against the `/batch` of that `set` and that `insert` under the server's token -- the parse and a round trip spared, the condition and the pair paid -- and eight clients paying orders with the ledger bound, against the same as a scoped `/batch` (`make counters-bench`'s shape), which a scoped token could not send without a grant on `ledger`.

### Bindings against other systems

| System | When it runs | As whom | fennecdb takes | fennecdb leaves |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| PostgreSQL triggers | before, after or instead of a write, for each row or each statement, in its transaction; same-event triggers by name ([trigger behavior](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/trigger-definition.html)) | the function's: whoever fired it unless it is `SECURITY DEFINER` -- the CVEs above | the write's transaction, row by row, by name; `NEW` and `OLD`; a condition (`WHEN`) | functions, `BEFORE`, `INSTEAD OF`, cascades, statement triggers (a follow-up) |
| SQLite triggers | before or after, for each row alone, in the statement's transaction ([CREATE TRIGGER](https://www.sqlite.org/lang_createtrigger.html)) | the connection's: SQLite has no users | the row trigger, its body a list of statements | `BEFORE`, `RAISE`, cascades |
| Firestore triggers | a Cloud Function after the write commits, outside it, delivered at least once ([Firestore triggers](https://firebase.google.com/docs/functions/firestore-events)) | the server's | an event named by what happened to a document | derived writes that land after their write, perhaps twice |
| PocketBase hooks | `onRecordAfterUpdateSuccess` and its kin, JavaScript in goja ([event hooks](https://pocketbase.io/docs/js-event-hooks/)) | the app's | events on a collection's records | a JavaScript engine |

### F2 in slices

Each a pull request of its own, green and measured, carrying its own docs, as F1's were. Slices 1 to 3 land apart only with execution refusing what the next has not built: a binding defined with nothing to run it is a write landing without its rows, the one thing F2 must never do.

1. **The parser and the tree** (fennec-ql, `fennec_core::named`). `after <event> [or <event> ...] <collection> [where ...]` in a definition, SQL's `update` and `delete` there; `new.` and `old.` numbered as places; the refusals the text alone answers -- parameters, parentheses, `claims`, `check`, `public`, `returning`, a `get` without `require`, `old.` in an `insert` binding and `new.` in a `del` one, a bare name in the condition -- and `CREATE TRIGGER` and `before` refused naming the form without consuming their words; the binding's fields in the canonical tree, written only where they hold something. Execution refuses a binding's definition in words of its own ("write-bound statements are not built yet"), the module in its own. Owes: `make fennecql-corpus BASE=origin/main`, no tree changed and no stored digest moved; fennec-cli's `kind`, a mark in `Parser::cut` where a `where` may follow the collection at the text's end, and the shell corpus' lines; the parse time of texts that are no definition, as F1's slice 1 measured it.
2. **The record and the builds that run none** (fennec-core) -- **built** ([below](#what-f2-has-built)), the definition refused but where a test lets it. Op 3 and its fields, an op-1 text reading as a binding still marked broken (slice 1); `Statements` indexed by collection and `Collection::bound` kept by every change (`Database::rebind`); the image, a replica's apply, `changes_in`; `depends` with the bound collection and the pair's fields; the kind and the contract kept across a replacement (`breaks_contract`: the bound collection and events, each collection written by kind of write); `after del` on a `@ttl` collection refused at definition and `@ttl` under an `after del` binding at its commit, an internal writer's marked broken; a write a binding binds refused by a build without the feature and by a server holding the binding broken or unread; `tests/statements.fennec` gaining a binding (`FENNEC_WRITE_FIXTURE=1`), which the module opens, keeps through its images and refuses the writes of. Definitions run where a test lets them, as F1's slice 2 had them. Owes: the module's bytes (`make size-report BASE=origin/main`, all three builds).
3. **Running them** (fennec-core) -- **built** ([below](#what-f2-has-built)). The condition asked as each row is written and only a passing row's pair kept, a write over an expired row an `insert`, the wake in `run_one`, rows then names, `MAX_WOKEN` refusing at the 10 001st woken row, `--call-max-rows` fresh for a statement and the call's own inside a call, `now()` read once a waking statement, depth one, the refusals of a chain and of a binding's own collection; `Database::unbound` and every writer under it -- `--follow`'s mirror, `fennec import`, a declared schema's migrations, the sync's apply -- and the sweep and an expired holder waking nothing; the binding that refused noted; a broken one refusing its writes; `over_ceiling` and `grows`. Definitions run for the server's token (`/query`, `/batch`, the shell, the native library, a declared schema), and a scoped token's write that would wake a binding is refused until slice 4 -- fail closed, never a binding run as its writer. The FennecQL reference's section, for the server's token; `make upgrade-check`'s base before op 3, and RELEASING.md's and the runbook's notes, since a primary can write op 3 from here; CLAUDE.md's rule (below). Owes: [the measurements above](#what-a-binding-costs-a-write), every path holding no binding as on main.
4. **Authority** (fennec-http's `access`, `plugin::Hook`) -- **built** ([below](#what-f2-has-built)). The policy's `wake` lines (`wake_line`: alone on its line, `* wake` refused, one naming no binding logged), `/_whoami`'s `wakes`; `Hook::writer_only` and `Hook::waking`; the `Check` hook passed over for a binding's writes, refusing one not granted (403, the audit naming it) or reaching `append-only`, and charging its runs to `--call-rate` (429, or 400 past a second's worth); `judge_call` holding the bindings a body may wake to the grant and `append-only`; `told_bound`, and `binding` for the server's token; a bound statement never called and a `call` line naming one logged. CLAUDE.md's access rule gains the second case. The auth page's section. Owes: `make policy-bench`, a scoped write waking nothing as on main, and a granted one's runs against the server token's.
5. **The surfaces** (fennec-http, fennec-abi, fennec-shard, the sync) -- **built** ([below](#what-f2-has-built)). `binding` in every error (`render_batch_stop`, fennec-abi's error, so the native library's); the slow line, the spans, `/_stats/statements`' shape, `/_metrics`' two counters, the audit line, the listings and `/_schema`; the claim held on a derived collection; the sync's cases, the keyed retry among them, both runners; the router reading a tenant's schema declaring a binding with the grammar (`admin::schema_description`). README's feature table, the home page and docs index where a visitor picks a database for keeping derived rows with their write, the sync and observability pages, and the search cache. Owes: `make sync-scenarios-check`; `make shard-bench ARGS=latency` inside its spread where the router's path changed.
6. **The SDKs, the studio and the shell** -- **built** ([below](#what-f2-has-built)). Each SDK's error with `binding` (JavaScript's `FennecError.binding` and its six kin) and the SDK lists; the studio's schema view listing a collection's bindings, each definition opened as `create or replace statement`, no call form, the highlighter's `after` and `old` (`make studio-highlight`); the shell's `.help`. Owes: `make studio-test` and each SDK's suite.

**The tests the design asks for**, each failing with its part taken out, beside each slice's own:

- a customer granted `update(status)` on its own orders and `paid_ledger wake`, and nothing on `ledger` or `accounts`, pays an order: the line and the debit land with it, and eight customers paying at once keep the ledger's sum the accounts' (`tests/require.rs`'s shape);
- a write its own rules refuse wakes nothing; a binding deleting in an `append-only` collection, woken by a scoped token: 403, nothing landed, and the server's token's same write lands;
- a scoped token's write waking a binding no `wake` line grants it: 403, nothing landed, no name told, the audit line naming it; the same token's write passing no condition lands with no grant; a call whose body may wake an ungranted binding: 403 before the body runs; `* wake` refused at startup and a line naming no binding logged;
- a replacement within the contract keeping its grant, and one binding another event or writing another collection refused naming the `drop` and `create`;
- a scoped writer's runs charged to `--call-rate`: 429 past the bucket, 400 past a full one, the server's token unheld;
- a `put` over an expired row waking an `insert` binding with no `old.` and no `set` binding; `after del` on a `@ttl` collection, and `@ttl` under an `after del` binding, refused in both orders;
- a `set` over 100 000 rows of which a few pass the condition keeping those few pairs (the `#[cfg(test)]` count); the 10 001st woken row refusing the write; one `now()` across a write's runs; a call's bindings spending the call's `--call-max-rows`, not a fresh one;
- a guard refusing: 412, the block put back whole, a scoped writer told its `reason` and no name, the server's token the `binding`;
- every event of the table above, with `new.` and `old.` as each reads; an upsert inserting and setting in one statement; a picked `set` waking its rows in its order; two bindings on one row in name order; a `nocase` field's condition waking as a `get` finds;
- `MAX_WOKEN` and `--call-max-rows` refusing, never landing a part;
- a binding's writes waking nothing, and a chain and a binding's own collection refused at definition, in both orders;
- each internal writer over a collection bound to write a row -- a replica's apply, an archive's restore, a move, an open, a compact, the sweep, an expired `@unique` holder cleared, `--follow`'s mirror and its copy again, `fennec import`, a declared migration and a `rebuild`, the sync's apply -- writing no derived row, and a replica's ledger its primary's;
- each way in waking it: `/query`, `/batch`, REST's routes, MCP's `write`, a held write, a call's body, the native library, the shell;
- a broken binding refusing its writes (409), the module and a process with no reader refusing them, an F1 binary stopping at op 3 in words (`make upgrade-check`);
- `call` of a bound statement the 404 to a scoped token, no MCP tool, a `call` line naming one logged;
- a claim held on a derived collection woken by the write that made its row;
- the sync's cases, both runners, the keyed retry writing one ledger line;
- `a_write_to_an_unbound_collection_keeps_no_row`.

### What F2 has built

**Slice 1, the parser and the tree.** `create [or replace] statement <name> after <event> [or <event> ...] <collection> [where <condition>] [comment '<text>'] [scans] as <body>` reads into `fennec_core::named`'s tree: `Definition::after`, a `Binding` boxed so a callable definition carries a pointer for it -- the collection, the events as the record's bits (`Binding::INSERT`, `SET`, `DEL`: 1, 2, 4), the condition, and `places`, what each `Expr::Param(k)` of the body reads (`Place`: `new.` or `old.` and the field, a path into a `json` one its own place). An event is `insert`, `set` or `del`, SQL's `update` and `delete` reading as `set` and `del`, each once, in any order. The condition keeps `new.status` and `old.status` as the `Expr::Field`s they are written as, for slice 3 to bind to the collection as a read's filter is, its collation and case fold with it; the body's are numbered as places in the order they first appear (`Parser::defined_field`), so a body is the statements every path of the engine takes. In a binding `new` and `old` are words, read in any case as every word of the language is (`row_field`): PostgreSQL's `NEW.total` is `new.total`, the same places, the condition's field written `new.` -- one tree, one digest -- and `Old.a` in an `after insert` is refused as `old.a` is; the field after the dot keeps its case. The review found the first cut matching the lower case alone, so `Old.a` in an `after insert`'s body read silently as a path into a field `Old`, and in the condition was refused as a bare name. Refusing every case but the lower was weighed and not taken: it refuses the spelling SQL writes, which reads as FennecQL everywhere else ([fennecql.md](fennecql.md#sql-spellings)). Out of a binding, `new.` and `old.` read as on main, in any case: a path into a field of that name, and an upsert's document.

*The refusals the text alone answers*, each at the word it is about: `old.` where an event is `insert` and `new.` where one is `del`, in the condition and the body, never read as `null`; a bare name in the condition; a `$` of any kind (`$1`, `$a`, `$jwt.sub`), parentheses, `claims`, `check` and `public` (`BOUND_VALUES`); in the condition an inner `get` (at its `get`, before its own names would be refused as bare ones), an aggregate or `expired()` (`named::condition_refused`); in the body F1's refusals, and a `get` without `require` and any `returning` (`Definition::bound_refused`), and `new.` or `old.` where a name stands, in any case or quoting (`Parser::pair_named`): every key a write writes -- a document's, braced, listed or quoted, a `set`'s braced or SQL's, an upsert's `else set` (`Parser::document`, `Parser::path`) -- where the review found the braced keys of the first cut passing as paths. A constant object's member is data and no name written, read as written; out of a binding each key is the path into a `json` field it is on main, so `new.a` is one rule everywhere: no field's name holds a dot ([fennecql.md](fennecql.md#json-fields-and-paths)), and only in a binding's body does `new.` name the row, where a key is no value. An item of a list is an expression already, refused as one answering under no name; a collection whose name starts with `_`, at the parse rather than at the table, since the text alone answers it; an event named twice. SQL's words, unconsumed and naming the form: `create trigger` (`TRIGGER`), `drop trigger`, `before`, `when` for `where`, and `on` before the collection (`after update on orders`). A binding's own collection and a chain are slice 3's, as the plan has them: they ask what other statements are bound, which only the table holds.

*Nothing runs one.* `Database::define_statement` refuses a definition holding `after` before it asks anything else (`named::BOUND_NOT_BUILT`, "write-bound statements are not built yet", a query error, 400): `/query`, `/batch` -- whose block is put back, a write before it landing nothing -- the shell, the native library and a declared schema's apply all define through it. A scoped token meets the schema change it is (403), as before. The module refuses it as it refuses every definition, `UNBUILT`, before anything of the text runs. A definition's record (op 1) whose text reads as a binding, its digest and all, is none this build writes: `read_statements` marks it broken (`its text reads as a bound statement`), never loading it as callable, which the review found it would have (`a_binding_s_text_in_a_callable_record_is_marked_broken`); op 3 is the one record a binding is read from, and slice 2 keeps the mark.

*The canonical tree and the epoch.* The binding is tag 9 of the definition, after the body, a node of kind 4 -- the collection (1), the events (2), the condition (3), the places (4), each a node of kind 5, `old.` a flag (1) and the field (2) -- written only where a definition holds one, and `Debug` writes `after` only there too. So every callable definition's bytes are as they were (`the_digest_holds_the_tree_not_the_text`'s pin holds), no text that parsed reads otherwise, and `GRAMMAR_EPOCH` stays 2; the ledger binding's digest is pinned in `a_binding_is_in_the_canonical_tree`. `make fennecql-corpus BASE=origin/main`: 10 033 texts, 20 066 parses, no tree changed and none that parsed refused; 22 parse that were refused -- this branch's bindings and the design's ledger -- and 37 are refused in other words, this branch's refusals and `create trigger` and `drop trigger` in the parser's and this file's text.

*The shell.* No arm of fennec-cli's `kind` is new: a bound definition is `CreateStatement`, a write where its body is one statement and a schema change where it is `begin ... end`, as a callable one. No mark in `Parser::cut` is needed either: the form never parses before `as` and its body, so its events, its collection and its condition at the text's end are refusals there, which the shell reads as "go on", and its body's own marks are F1's. Four lines join `a_statement_written_over_lines_reads_as_one`'s corpus, and `a_binding_over_lines_reads_as_one_statement` pipes two through the shell. No bracket is new to `lexer::Unclosed`.

*Parse time.* What every text pays is one look a field (`primary`, before `Expr::Field`), one a name `path` reads and one a key a written document reads (`Parser::pairs`, since the review: one copy for a document and an object, the module 11 bytes raw more, gzip and brotli within their noise, `make size-report` against the branch before it), whether a definition is being read, each path past it `#[cold]` and out of line; `create` asks for `trigger` once a `create`. The parse of the repository's 9 727 texts that are no definition, `parse` alone, in turns with main, each pair redone where the load reached 2.5: 200 rounds a run, 11 pairs, main 1 184 ns a text (1 181-1 190) and the branch 1 190 (1 187-1 260), the median pair +0.6%; 40 rounds a run, 9 pairs, +1.6%, and the quiet pairs of a first run +0.3%. The look is not what moved it: the branch with `primary`'s look taken out measured +1.4% beside main and the branch, 11 pairs again (main 1 180, the branch 1 187, without the look 1 197) -- taking a branch out of the hottest arm made the parse slower, so what moves it by that much either way is the layout of the code around it, not the work a text does.

**Slice 2, the record and the builds that run none.** *What it lets a user do: nothing yet, by design.* The plan's words -- slices 1 to 3 land apart only with execution refusing what the next has not built -- leave two ways to land the record before anything runs it: define and store a binding while the writes it binds are refused, or keep the definition refused and hold the record with a test. The second was taken, as F1's slice 2 took it: `Database::define_statement` still answers `named::BOUND_NOT_BUILT` on every way in (`/query`, `/batch`, the shell, the native library, a declared schema's apply), and `Database::allow_bindings` (`#[doc(hidden)]`) lets a test define one. Defined anywhere, a binding would stop its collection's writes until dropped -- loud, but a statement a user could make with nothing it does -- and no server writes op 3 before the engine runs it, so the upgrade's order (below) is held by a fixture rather than owed by a primary. What a build meets is a file or a stream holding op 3, which every build now reads, keeps and refuses the writes of.

*The record.* Op 3 is op 1's fields, then the bound collection and the events byte, then the text: `[13][0][length][3][history][change][epoch][digest][name][depends][collection][events][text]` (`engine/stored.rs`, `OP_BOUND`). `Stored::after` holds the two fields from the record, never from the text, so a build that cannot read the text -- the module, a process with no reader installed -- knows what it binds. The decoder refuses an op past 3, an events byte of 0 or with a bit past the three (`INSERT`, `SET`, `DEL`: 1, 2, 4), and a byte past the text, as a record this version does not read: what main's binary does with op 3 (`an_op_or_an_event_this_version_does_not_read_is_refused`, and `make upgrade-check`, below). `writes_in` counts it as one write whatever its op, an image and a compact write it with `Stored::encode` as op 1's, a replica's apply and an open take it through `take_statement`, and `changes_in` passes over it as the write of no collection it is (`an_open_an_image_a_replica_a_compact_and_a_restore_keep_a_binding`: an open, an image and an image of it, a replica write for write and its file, a restore -- an archive's image, the records after it, opened, forked and checkpointed as `fennec restore` writes its file -- and a compact of a file read into memory and of a mapped one).

*Read back.* An op-1 record whose text reads as a binding stays marked broken (`its text reads as a bound statement`, slice 1's mark); an op-3 record whose text reads as a callable statement (`reads as a callable statement`), or as a binding of another collection or other events than its record names (`binds other writes than its record`), is marked broken as a misread, never loaded: the mask is the record's, and a tree binding other writes would be run for writes the mask does not name (`a_bound_record_reading_otherwise_is_marked_broken`).

*The table and the mask.* `Statements` keeps beside its statements by name an index of the bound ones, their places in the table ordered by collection and, within one, by name, so a collection's bindings are a run in name order -- the order slice 3 runs them in on a row -- read by `Statements::bound_to` and folded into its events by `events_of`. Places rather than names: the first cut kept each `(collection, name)` twice, sorted by `partition_point`, taken out by `retain` and cloned with every table, those generics' copies 0.7 KB of the module and its code 1.1 KB more than now (+3 128 bytes against main); the index is made again whole where a bound statement is defined or dropped, or one beside a bound one moves its place (`reindex`, nothing for a table binding none), and walked rather than halved, a database binding a handful (`a_collection_s_bindings_are_read_in_name_order`). A build without the `statements` feature -- the browser module -- keeps no index: it reads no binding's tree and asks only a collection's events, at a schema change, so `events_of` walks the table there, and the index, `bound_to`, `run` and `reindex` are none of its bytes (the review: 794 bytes raw, see *Size*). `Collection::bound` is that byte, every binding's, broken and unread ones among them, set by `Database::rebind` from the table wherever the table or the collections change: a definition and a drop (`statement_record`), `take_statement` (an open's and a replica's), the end of an open (a collection made after the binding it is bound by, dropped and made again), a collection made by a statement or a replica's apply, an image adopted, a block put back where it held a collection dropped or the table changed (`rewind`, after its loop: a dropped collection comes back with the byte it had, under a table the block may have changed after), and a compact of the module, whose fresh collection takes the live one's byte at its end (a definition meanwhile notes no collection's tail). A pinned read's copy carries it. `the_mask_follows_a_drop_a_rename_and_an_alter_of_the_bound_collection` holds each: a drop and a rename refused naming the binding, an alter it does not reach keeping the byte, an internal writer's drop leaving the binding broken and the collection made again bound again, a rename by one refusing the writes as a broken binding's, the same on a replica and after an open, and a block dropping the binding and the collection put back with its byte.

*The writes refused.* The write path reads the byte where it looks the collection up already -- `put_with` for `put`, `insert` and an upsert, `update_rows` for `set` (which looked it up twice and now once), `delete` for `del` -- and refuses, before anything is taken or written and the condition unread, a statement that may cause a bound event: `insert` a row made (`INSERT`), `put` and `if absent else set` a row made or written over (`INSERT | SET`), `set` a row written over, `del` a row taken out. A statement, not a row: a `set` of a bound collection matching nothing is refused all the same, since only slice 3 asks the condition. The sweep passes `delete` `waking: false` and is refused nothing: no binding sees a row expire, and a broken `after del` on a collection an internal writer made `@ttl` must not stop the rows every read left out from going. The refusal (`Database::bound_refused`, `#[cold]`) names the first broken or unread binding in name order the statement would wake, else the first it would wake, so the status is the same whichever name sorts first -- the first cut named the first by name, and a whole `audit` beside a broken `credit` answered 400 where a whole `zeta` answered 409 (`a_write_waking_several_is_answered_whatever_their_names_order`): a broken one with the 409 slice 3 keeps ("the write would wake `credit`, which is broken: ...: define it again or drop it"), one this process holds unread with the same 409, and a whole one with a query error, "... bound after `insert` on `entries`, and write-bound statements are not run yet: drop it to write `entries`"; a build without the `statements` feature -- the module -- says "`entries` has statements bound to its writes, which this module does not run: write it through a server". `each_event_refuses_the_writes_that_may_make_it` holds the events against each write form; `web/fennec.test.js` holds the module's words over the fixture.

*Definitions, where a test lets them.* Beside F1's checks a binding's definition holds its collection there (404), at most `named::MAX_BOUND` (16) statements bound to it, never cut, every field `new.` and `old.` read -- the condition's and the places' -- held by it (`Binding::unheld`, a path into a `json` field by its root), and `after del` refused on a collection whose rows expire. Its `depends` names the bound collection first and each field the pair reads (`Binding::fields`), so dropping `entries` or its `amount` is refused naming it while it stands, and an internal writer's marks it broken. A body's places stand where a callable one's parameters do in `body_checked`'s holes, so `where id = new.dst` narrows by `id` as `where id = $1` does. A `call` of a bound statement is refused by the engine before it is judged (`callee`, "statement `credit` is bound after `insert` on `entries`: the writes it binds run it, and no call does"), which a binding read from a later build's file would otherwise run with no row to read; slice 4 makes it a scoped token's 404.

*The contract.* A replacement keeps its kind and, bound, its collection and events, asked from the record (`named::kind_kept`) before the tree: a text this build reads no tree for -- a misread, a later grammar -- still names what it binds, so a callable `later` replacing a bound one is refused where its text does not read. Then `Definition::breaks_contract`, bound against bound: each `(collection, kind of write)` the body writes, counted as the write path counts what a statement may do to a row (`put_with`) -- `insert` and `put ... if absent` a row made, `put` and `if absent else set` a row made or set, `set` a row set, `del` a row taken out -- must be one the old body wrote. A body's text refuses `put` and an upsert today, and the first cut counted a `put` as an `insert` alone, a misreading that refusal hid: lifted, an `insert` replaced by a `put` would have started setting rows no grant said it would. `a_replacement_widening_by_a_put_or_an_upsert_is_refused` holds the count on trees made by hand, never leaning on the parser's refusal. Writing less, another condition, value or guard is within it; a collection or a kind of write it did not is refused naming the `drop` and `create` it needs (`a_replacement_keeps_the_kind_the_binding_and_what_it_writes`). `declared::plan_statements` holds a declared schema's replacement to the same, through `breaks_contract`.

*`after del` and `@ttl`.* At definition, `after del` on a collection with a `@ttl` field is refused, naming both ways out. The other order is held where the schema changes: `statements_hold` asks each whole binding `expiring` beside `unmet` at a block's commit, so `alter ... @ttl` and an inline `create index ... @ttl` are refused naming the binding; `create index` built beside the database asks `expiry_held` in `check_index` and again in `finish_index` under the write lock, since it lands outside `commit` and a definition meanwhile notes no collection's tail. An internal writer's `@ttl` lands, and `read_statements` marks the binding broken ("it is bound after `del` on `visits`, whose rows `@ttl` takes out with no write to wake it"), which refuses its `del`s while the sweep takes the expired rows out (`after_del_and_ttl_refuse_each_other_whichever_comes_second`).

*The fixture and the module.* `tests/statements.fennec` gains `credit`, bound after `insert` on `entries`, after F1's records, which stay as an earlier epoch wrote them: `FENNEC_WRITE_FIXTURE=1` now writes the F1 part afresh only where there is no file, and appends the binding once (`with_binding`). Every build reads it (`the_fixture_s_binding_is_read_and_refuses_its_writes`); the module opens it, keeps its text through two images, and refuses an `insert` and a `put` of `entries` in its own words while `set`, `del` and every other collection write as ever.

*The upgrade's order, for op 3.* `make upgrade-check` gains a second base, `OP3_BASE` (`b73ce4ca`, the last main before the op), and a section of `tools/upgrade-order.sh`: main's server refuses to open the fixture ("a statement record this version does not read"), main's replica of this tree's server serving it refuses the image it is sent, in those words, holds nothing and connects again, and main's `fennec types` refuses the file too; this tree's server answers its reads and refuses the write its binding binds. Readers still upgrade before a primary defines one, which slice 3's RELEASING.md notes owe.

*Size.* The browser module against main (`make size-report BASE=origin/main`): with every feature +1 458 bytes raw, +586 gzip, +495 brotli, its code +1 349 -- the op's reading, `rebind` and the mask at each place, the three looks and the refusal's words, which every build carries since the record is core. The first cut was +2 252, +930, +691, its code +2 030 (with `language` alone +2 660, +1 207, +1 058; with no feature +2 235, +918, +939): the index, its walk and its upkeep were in the module, which asks only the events (*The table and the mask*), and the review left them to the builds with the `statements` feature. Brotli moves by some 300 bytes either way with where the code lies, as the trims showed (the walk in place of `partition_point` took the code 206 bytes down and brotli 286 up, the one comparison a statement 42 and 242 down). `fennec-server`'s code is 8.9 KB more, 4 101.4 KB to 4 110.3, fennec-core's all of it -- the definition's checks, the contract's, `expiring` and `expiry_held`, the refusal's arms -- its build 18 KB of 7 165 (`make size-report BIN=fennec-server`, on main and the branch).

*Time.* What a collection no statement is bound to pays is the byte read where its collection is looked up already, an `and` and a branch a statement: `update_rows` now looks the collection up once where it looked it up twice. Measured in process, main and the branch in turns, each run waiting for the 1-minute load to fall under 2.5 and made again where it rose past it, five pairs of the median of nine rounds: 50 000 `insert`s, `put`s over live rows, `set`s and `del`s by id, a `put` of 1 000 documents twenty times, and a `set` scanning 50 000 rows twenty times. Three runs of eleven, one run discarded and made again each: an insert +0.1 to +0.3% (main 519-522 ns), a `put` over a live row -0.5 to +0.4% (800-814), the `put` of 1 000 -1.1 to -1.5% (306 ns a document), the scan 0.0% (29.3 ns a row); a `set` by id +1.1 to +1.6% (851-856) and a `del` by id +1.3 to +2.6% (555-568). Those two are not the looks': the branch with the three looks taken out measured beside it +1.4% and +1.9% over main, the branch against it -0.5% and +0.6%; and main with nothing but the two fields added -- `Collection::bound` and the database's `bindings` flag, read on no path -- +2.2% on the insert and +1.2% on the `set`, -0.3% on the `del`. As F1's slices found, where the code and the fields lie moves a write by id by some two per cent either way, and what this slice puts on the path -- a byte loaded where its collection is, an `and` and a branch never taken -- is below what that lets a measurement tell. The review held that to code placement: main and the branch built with `RUSTFLAGS="-C llvm-args=-align-all-functions=6"`, every function on a 64-byte boundary, and run in turns, seven pairs, each run the median of nine rounds of 50 000 writes by id, waiting for the 1-minute load under 1.5 and made again where it was not under 1.5 after (five runs discarded): an insert +0.1% (main 345.4 ns), a `set` by id -0.2% (609.1), a `del` by id +0.1% (360.6), the medians of the seven. Two runs a side ran under someone else's burst a one-second run's load average does not show, alike on both sides; without them, +0.4%, -0.4% and +1.0%, the pairs' `del` from -1.0 to +2.1%. Aligned, the branch is main's time.

**Slice 3, running them.** *What it lets a user do: keep derived rows with the write that makes them, from the server's token.* `Database::define_statement` no longer refuses `after` (`named::BOUND_NOT_BUILT` and `Database::allow_bindings` are gone): `/query`, `/batch`, the shell, the native library and a declared schema's apply define a binding, and every write those ways send -- REST's routes, MCP's `write`, a held write and a call's body among them -- wakes it. A scoped token's write or call that would wake one is refused (403) until slice 4's `wake` lines grant it: fail closed, never a binding run as its writer.

*Asking a row.* A writing statement whose collection's byte holds what it may do to a row (`put_with`, `update_rows`, `delete`) asks `Database::bind` once, out of line, where the slice-2 refusal stood: a writer under `Database::unbound` asks nothing; a broken or unread binding of those events refuses the write before it changes anything (`bound_refused`, now its 409s alone and the module's words); otherwise the bindings it may wake are held in `Database::woken` (`engine/bound.rs`'s `Woken`), with the collection's schema and, for each, which side of the pair its body reads. Each row is then asked the conditions of its event as it is written, beside `after_write` (`Woken::keep`): a `put`'s row made, or written over where its row was alive -- `put_with` reads the expiry wherever a write may wake, so a `put` naming an expired row's id is an `insert` with no `old.`, whatever the hooks are handed -- `set_row`'s row set, an upsert's too, and a `del`'s row in `erase`, where it is read as it is taken out. The condition is `eval`'s over a pair (`Pair`: `new.` and `old.` stripped, each field compared by its `Field::text_order`), so a `nocase` field's condition wakes where a `get` finds. A row passing none is not kept; one passing keeps a copy of only the sides its bindings' bodies read -- the review's trim: a `set` binding reading `new.` alone keeps no `old` row -- and a bit a binding it passed. The 10 001st row passing refuses the write as it is written (`named::MAX_WOKEN`, 400, "nothing it wrote landed"). `now()` is read once, as the first row is asked, as the writer's statement reads it -- the database's clock, else the registry's `now`, never the system's behind a replaced one (`every_run_of_a_write_reads_one_now` replaces `now` with a clock ticking at each reading).

*Running them.* `run_one` looks at one byte after `execute_inner` -- the statement's rows, hooks, `require` and answer behind it -- `Database::bound_due`, which a statement asking a binding's conditions or a binding's refusal sets, and where it is set `Database::ended` lets the last refusal's note go and takes the rows: the statement failed, they are let go of with the block its caller puts back; it succeeded, `wake` asks each hook `Hook::waking(name, rows)` for each binding a row passed, then runs, inside `catch_unwind`, the kept rows in the order written and, on each, the bindings it passed in name order, each body's statements through `run_one` with the row's values by place (`new.id` its id, a path into a `json` field its value). The runs read the waking statement's `now()` (`Database::clock` set and given back) and run unbound (depth one: a body's writes keep no row and meet no broken binding's refusal); with no call running they are `Database::bounded` -- a fresh `--call-max-rows`, `call::LEFT`, given back on a panic -- and inside a call's body they spend what the call has left. A run's error is the writer's statement's, the binding named in `Database::woken()` (the slow line, the trace and `binding` are slice 5's). The note is let go of as the next statement ends, and as a block or a reading call starts (`settle_woken`), which no statement's end settles. Rows a panic left kept are let go of with the block the panic unwound out of, which is put back (*A panic mid-write*, [engine.md](engine.md#blocks)) -- they land with the rows that woke them or not at all -- and a block starting lets go of any other.

*Definitions.* `binding_checked` holds depth one at definition, by collection whatever the events (`chained`): a binding writing the collection it is bound to, one writing a collection another statement is bound to, and one bound to a collection another binding writes, each refused naming the other, from `Definition::written` (now public) of the trees the table holds -- a binding read from no tree here writes nothing a definition can know of, and refuses its own writes. A callable statement's writes wake bindings as any writer's do. A binding bound to or writing a collection whose name starts with `_` (`declared::own`) never reaches it: the text alone refuses both (slice 1, `Definition::refused` and the `after` clause's parse), which matters beyond tidiness -- the database's own are written beside a client's write, a scoped token's `Idempotency-Key` row outside `within()`, where the `Check` hook finds no scope to refuse a wake by, so a binding on `_idempotency` would run on a scoped token's behalf with no grant. The review asked for the refusal; fennec-http's `no_statement_is_bound_to_the_database_s_own_collections` now holds it at `/query`, beside fennec-ql's.

*The writers that wake nothing.* `Database::unbound(|db| ...)` -- given back on a panic, implied by `internally` -- wraps `--follow`'s mirror (`Mirror::flush`'s statements), `fennec import`'s load (`load::run_with_progress`, a follower's copy among its callers), a declared schema's migrations (`fennec_abi::schema`), and the sync's apply of a stream's seed and changes, its undo of a write put back and its drop of rows past a shape (`fennec_abi::sync`); its remake ran under `internally` already. The sweep passes `delete` `waking: false` as in slice 2, and an expired `@unique` holder is `erase`d by no statement. Each is held by a test with a binding written by hand into the file -- op 3, a later build's -- so the writer would be refused, or would wake it, without its wrap: `a_mirror_wakes_no_statement_bound_to_its_collection`, `a_load_wakes_no_statement_bound_to_its_collection`, `a_stream_s_rows_wake_no_statement_bound_to_them`, `a_binding_wakes_at_the_native_way_in_and_a_migration_writes_unbound`; each fails with the wrap taken out.

*fennec-http.* The `Check` hook's `waking` refuses every scoped token's wake (403, "the write would wake a statement bound to it, which no scoped token's write wakes"), naming no binding: asked as a binding wakes, so a scoped write passing no condition, or of an event nothing binds, lands as ever. `judge_call` refuses a scoped call whose body writes a collection and kind a binding is bound to (`unwoken`), before the body runs outside `within()`. `over_ceiling` holds a `del` of a collection whose `after del` binding inserts or sets as the insert it makes (`grows_bound`), in a call's body too. `/query` asks a `del` under the write lock it takes anyway, never a read lock of its own, and `grows_bound` reads the collection's byte before the table: the first cut took a read lock for every `del` under `--max-memory`, bound or not, which the review took out -- a `del` of a collection no statement is bound to takes the locks it took on main. The engine's note of the binding that refused (`Database::woken`) is read by `waits::wrote_nothing`: a binding's unmet `require` under `Fennec-Wait` is answered at once with its `reason`, never held as the writer's own, since its guard reads a collection no write to the writer's wakes a look for ([waits.md](waits.md#fennec-wait), `a_binding_s_unmet_require_is_answered_at_once`); the audit line naming it is slice 5's.

*Tests.* `tests/bound.rs` (fennec-core): the ledger line and the debit landing with the write that paid, in one record, a guard's 412 with its `reason` putting the block back whole and noting the binding; every event's `new.` and `old.`, an upsert making and setting in one statement; rows then names, and a picked `set` waking in its pick's order; a `put` over an expired row an `insert`; a `nocase` condition; `MAX_WOKEN`; one row budget a statement and a call's own; one `now()`; depth one at definition and in a file another build wrote; `unbound`, `internally` and a replica's apply waking nothing, a panic giving `unbound` back; a hook refusing a wake; `a_write_to_an_unbound_collection_keeps_no_row` -- every write form on one collection while two others are bound, no row asked or kept, through `Database::bound_counts` (`#[doc(hidden)]`, a thread's counts: a `#[cfg(test)]` count is no integration test's to read) -- and a `set` over 20 000 rows asking each and keeping the five that pass. `tests/bound.rs` (fennec-http): `/query`, `/batch` and a call's body waking it under the server's token, a later failing statement putting back what it wrote; a scoped write and call refused, and its writes waking none landing; the ceiling. Slice 2's tests that held the refusal now hold the run (`tests/stored.rs`, fennec-ql's, fennec-cli's shell).

*The upgrade's order.* `make upgrade-check`'s op-3 section now drives a live definition: this tree's primary defines a binding by `/query` and writes what it binds, and `OP3_BASE`'s replica stops at the record in words and takes no write past it, its archiver keeps the stream and takes no image of its own, its `fennec restore` refuses the archive this tree's restores, its server refuses to open that file, and a move onto its node fails at the install, the tenant left on its source with its binding running; the fixture's cases stay. RELEASING.md and `deploy/RUNBOOK.md` name `after` as the clause moving the format, since a primary can write op 3 from here.

*Size.* The browser module against main (`make size-report BASE=origin/main`), every feature: +57 bytes raw, gzip and brotli within their noise (+37, +157), its code +55 -- the three looks at `unbound` beside the byte's, which a migration the module applies may run under; `erase` is 52 bytes smaller, handing no row back. Before the timing fix (*Time*) it was +194 raw, +120 gzip, +129 brotli, its code +192, `erase` handing its row back to `delete`. The module runs no binding: `bind`, `Woken` and the runs are the `statements` feature's. The first measure was +645 raw, +344 gzip, +319 brotli: `Database::unbound` caught a panic to give the flag back, 0.4 KB of catch and resume in a module that aborts on one, which now sets and restores it alone there. `fennec-server`'s code is 13.3 KB more, 4 119.5 KB to 4 132.8 (`make size-report BIN=fennec-server` on main and the branch), fennec-core 8.7 KB of it -- `bind`, the condition's pair, the runs, the chain's refusal -- and fennec-http 0.9; 4 133.1 after the timing fix, the note's clearing moved into `ended`. After the instruction count's fixes (*Instructions*) the module is 2 bytes smaller (`make size-report BASE=` the commit before them), `erase` letting its row go at its end there, before the append natively, and `fennec-server`'s code 4 132.9 KB.

*Time.* Measured as slice 2's were: main and the branch in turns, seven pairs, each run the median of nine rounds over a fresh database, made only once the 1-minute load is under 1.5 and discarded where it rose past 1.8 while it ran -- in process, 50 000 `insert`s, `put`s over live rows, `set`s and `del`s by id, twenty `put`s of 1 000 documents and twenty `set`s over a 50 000-row scan, and through `fennec_http::handle` 20 000 `put`s and `set`s by id, to collections no statement is bound to; and on the branch, what it costs against what it saves -- a `set` by id waking one binding that inserts, against the block of that `set` and that `insert`, in process and as one `/query` against a `/batch`; a `put` of 1 000 waking one insert a row against the block of it and a `put` of the 1 000 rows; and a `put` of 1 000 asked a condition none passes against one with no binding. The figures come with the quiet machine's run (timing pending).

*Instructions.* The first quiet run had the branch's insert +2.4%, its `set` by id +2.4% and, built with every function aligned, +9.5%, where a path holding no binding must measure as it did. Counted rather than timed -- `/usr/bin/time -l`'s instructions retired on this Mac, a million inserts into a collection no statement is bound to, then a million `set`s or `del`s by id, five runs a side, a mode's count less the one it builds on -- the slice added 41 instructions an insert (+0.6% of 6 442), 16 a `set` (+0.1% of 11 441) and 15 a `del` (+0.2% of 6 827); its cycles moved with where the code lay (the bench's own `main` rebuilt alone moved main's `set` 3%, and 4 KB of heap allocated ahead of the database 2%).
