Each statement single-stepped under lldb, its instructions by function against main's, named the added work: `run_one` handed its answer, 160 bytes, through `match self.bound_due` and back on every statement, bound or not (8 to 12 instructions; `ended` now takes it `&mut`); `put` carried `gone` and `beside` through its loop, which moved its registers (12; a bound row now goes through `woke_put`, out of line, which reads whether the row was past its time again from the row as it stood); and `erase` held the row it read across the append and the log (3; a bound row is now kept beside the hooks, before anything is taken out, and let go of where it was read). What is left is the byte's look and a flag a row, and the registers those move: single-stepped, 13 instructions an insert of main's 3 459 (0.4%), 14 a `set` of 5 827 (0.2%) and 8 a `del` of 3 866 (0.2%), where the slice had added 33, 22 and 20 (`malloc`'s left out, whose paths differ from one call to the next); counted over a million, an insert 14 of 6 442 (+0.2%), and a `set` and a `del` within the count's spread from one run to the next, some 10 instructions an op. Timed after these fixes, as above: plain, an insert 369.9 to 371.7 ns (+0.1%), a `set` by id 649.9 to 715.6 (+10.0%) and a `del` by id 388.9 to 386.0 (-1.2%); every function aligned, +0.2%, +1.1% and +0.0%. The `set`'s gap is where its code lies, not work it does: its instructions are main's within the count's spread, and it moves with the alignment -- +2.4% plain and +9.5% aligned in the run before, +10.0% plain and +1.1% aligned here.

**Slice 4, authority.** *What it lets a user do: let a scoped token's writes keep derived rows it may not write itself.* A policy's `paid_ledger wake for customer` lets a customer, granted `update(status)` on its own orders and nothing on `ledger` or `accounts`, pay an order: the binding's line and debit land in the write's block, its writes judged by no rule of the customer's.

*The policy.* `wake_line` reads `<statement>[, ...] wake [for <role>]` as `call_line` reads a `call` line, one reader for both (`grant_line`): `wake` alone on its line (`paid read, wake` and `paid wake, call` refused), no filter, and `*` refused among its names with or without a role. A scope keeps the names its roles are granted (`Scope::wakes`, `may_wake`), an empty list costing a token nothing, and `/_whoami` lists them as `wakes` beside `calls`. `calls::log_unheld`, as the server starts, as a tenant opens and after a `drop`, logs a `wake` line naming a statement the database does not hold or holds callable, and a `call` line naming a bound one, each granting nothing.

*The judgment.* `Hook::waking` is handed the binding's name, its rows and its body's statements, and `Check::waking` asks, with the writer's scope installed, `Scope::held_from`: a `wake` line granting it, and no `set` or `del` of its body in an `append-only` collection (`Scope::reshapes`, which a call's body is asked too). Either refusal is a 403 naming no binding -- "the write would wake a statement bound to it that no `wake` line grants this token" -- its block put back by the engine as slice 3 has it, and the audit's `unwoken` line names it with the reason, the method, the path and the status (`audit::unwoken`): held in a thread-local under the write lock and written by `audit::http` once the answer is, so no line is written under the lock, whatever the status -- MCP answers its refusal inside a 200. The token checked out: the line waits nothing and clears nothing, `audit::verified` asked where the token was (`tests/audit.rs` holds the count). `judge_call` holds a scoped call's body before it runs, for each collection and kind of write it makes, to every binding of that collection and event (`wakes_held`), the same two refusals; a bound statement named by a `call` line is the 404 a missing one is to a scoped token (`judged`, before the engine's 400), and MCP lists no tool for one (`callable_stored`).

*The runs.* `Check::writer_only` is true; `Registry::register_hook` keeps the hooks that are not in `beside`, and `wake` swaps that list into the hooks' place around the runs and back after them (`Registry::swap_beside`). A flag every hook site asked was weighed and not taken: the four sites that read the hooks are on every write's path, where two `Vec`s swapped once a woken write cost nothing.

*The rate.* A scoped request's scope, made for that request alone, holds its database and `--call-rate` and what the request was charged so far (`calls::Runs`), set by `authenticate` where the request runs in a database -- `handle` and MCP's endpoint -- and nothing for the server's token or `--call-rate 0`; `calls::admitted` marks a request it charged a call there. `Check::waking` charges each binding's runs to the writer's scope (`calls::charge_runs`, `access::charged_to`), a call's body's to its caller's, which `run_as` notes for the body alone (`CALLING`), the body running with no scope installed. A scope bound for a statement (`Scope::bound`) carries the request's charge on. The first cut held the database, the scope and the rate in a thread-local `calls::charging` set and put back around every scoped request, which every scoped write paid for whether it woke anything or not (*Instructions*, below). The request's runs and its call beyond the rate are refused (400, "too many runs at once ... would never be let through"); past the bucket, 429 with `Retry-After` and `retry_after` (`api::status_of` reads the words `RUNS_WAIT`, `error_response` and `batch_stop` write the wait), and the runs the request was charged before are handed back with its block, the wait told being for every run it woke: kept, a write waking two bindings of 30 rows with 40 runs left would spend 30 on the first, be refused the second, and spend the first's again at every retry, never landing. Counted per request, a `/batch`'s writes and a call's body share the second's worth.

*What a writer is told.* `within` reads the flag `Check::waking` sets once it let a binding run, kept in the slot the writer's scope is installed in (`Current::woke`) so that a write waking nothing touches no thread-local more than before bindings, and an error after it is the binding's: `told_bound` keeps its status and `reason` -- "no ledger line (`this write` was put back: a write bound to it refused it)", which `Error::reason` reads back as ever -- and drops the engine's words and the binding's name; a quota's, a lease's and the disk's refusals, which come as the block lands, are told as ever. A scoped call's runs are told as `told_call` tells its body's, the rate's words passed on whole. The server's token's REST writes, and its `/query` and `/_call` writes sent with no `Idempotency-Key`, carry `binding` and the engine's words after "bound statement `voided`, after `set` on `orders`, refused this write:" (`bound_by`, `told_response`), read under the write lock; a keyed `/query`, `/batch`, the native library and the shell gain them with slice 5's `binding` in every error.

*Tests.* `tests/bound.rs` (fennec-http): a customer granted `wake` pays an order through `/query`, `/batch`, `/_call`, MCP's `write`, an `Idempotency-Key` write (sent again, answered and waking nothing) and a held write (`Fennec-Wait`), each landing its line, its own write to `ledger` still 403; without a grant each is 403, nothing landed, no name told and no key kept; a binding deleting in an `append-only` collection refused a granted token's write and call, the server's token's landing; a call judged by what its body may wake, `call paid` the scoped token's 404 and the server's 400, no `call_paid` tool; a guard's refusal told with its `reason` and no name, in `/query` and at a `/batch`'s `at`, the server's token told `binding`; the rate's 429 with `Retry-After`, the 400 past a full bucket, a write waking none uncharged and the server's token unheld; `/_whoami`'s `wakes`. `tests/bound.rs` (fennec-core): a `writer_only` hook passed over by a binding's writes and met by the writer's own, before and after the runs, and every other hook meeting both. `access.rs`: the `wake` line's refusals and grants. `tests/audit.rs`: the `unwoken` line, the count left where it was.

*Size.* The browser module against main (`make size-report BASE=origin/main`): every feature 0 bytes raw, gzip +5 and brotli -271, both noise; `fennec-lite` and no feature within their noise too. The module runs no binding: `beside` and its swap are the `statements` feature's, and `Hook`'s two methods have no implementation there. `fennec-server`'s code is 15.9 KB more, 4 141.7 KB to 4 157.6 (`make size-report BIN=fennec-server` on main and the branch), fennec-http 14.6 of it: `Check::waking` with the charge inlined (1.8), `wait_of` (1.3), `told_response`, `bound_by` and `batch_stop`'s 429 (2.4), `held_from`, `told_bound`, `judged` and `log_unheld` (2.0), the audit line (0.7); `grant_line` is `Access::with`'s reading moved, and `Callers::take` `charge`'s. The first head was 4 154.9, the review's fixes 4 156.9, and the cut of every scoped request's work (*Instructions*, below) 0.7 more: `Access::scope` 1.4 smaller and `charging` and its drop gone (0.9), against `/_whoami`'s `granted_into` (0.7), `Scope::granted` (0.3), `Calling` (0.5) and its guard where `run_as` is inlined (some 0.3 a site, of seven). A guard holding what it put back was 0.5 more, and the note inlined at each site 2.5.

*Time.* Measured as slice 3's were, main and the branch in turns, the medians of runs made under a 1-minute load below 1.5 and discarded where it reached 1.8 while they ran: `make policy-bench`, and `make wake-bench` (`examples/wakes.rs`), through `fennec_http::handle` in process, a scoped `set` and `insert` into `plain`, a collection no statement is bound to, while `orders` beside it is bound and the policy grants the customer `paid wake`, beside the server token's `set`, and on the branch a granted scoped write's runs against the server token's. The first head, 7 pairs of 9 rounds of 20 000 writes: the scoped `set` 2 785 to 2 859 ns (+2.7%, every pair +2.2 to +3.8%) and the scoped `insert` 2 022 to 2 119 (+4.8%, every pair +3.3 to +5.2%), where a write waking nothing must measure as on main; the server token's `set` and its wake +0.5%; `make policy-bench`'s scoped reads and writes, under a policy with no `wake` line, equal or faster.

*Instructions.* Counted as slice 3's were -- `/usr/bin/time -l`'s instructions retired, a million of each write through `handle` less a run of none, five runs a side -- the first head added 1 128 instructions to the scoped `set`'s 45 014 and the `insert`'s 32 909, the same for both, so the request's and not the write's: 918 of them went with the policy's `wake` line (the head with no `wake` line in the policy: +210). `Access::scope`, made for every request, collected the names the token's `call` and `wake` lines grant into a `Vec<String>` each, a `Vec` and a `String` made and freed a request for the one name granted; the lines are now one `Arc` every scope shares with the `append-only` collections (`Lines`, the reference count `append_only` was already paying), a line's role asked of the token as a grant is (`Scope::granted`): a call's admission, a binding's wake, `/_whoami`. `calls::charging`'s thread-local, set and put back around every scoped request with the scope's `Arc` cloned into it, was some 50 more: the charge now rides in the scope (*The rate*, above), read only as a binding wakes. `within`'s `WOKE` was two thread-local lookups a scoped write: the flag is in `CURRENT`'s slot, which `within` installs and puts back as it did. And `bound_by`, asked of every write under the lock and its pair of `String`s dropped at its end, cost the server token's `set` 46: it is asked of a failed write alone, cold, its answer boxed. The fixed head, counted beside main again: the scoped `set` 44 951 against 45 012 and `insert` 32 855 against 32 907, the scoped `get` 34 479 against 34 556, the server token's `get` 12 052 against 12 061 and its `set` 18 665 against 18 633 -- a run lands in one of two modes some 85 instructions apart, the hash tables' seeds, so the scoped writes are main's, a scope now cheaper to make than main's. The server token's `set`'s 32 stayed with `bound_by` and the charge taken out altogether, and with a `Registry` without `beside`: where the code lies, not work it does. Re-timed on the fixed head against main, 7 pairs of `make wake-bench` and 5 of `make policy-bench`: every write within 1.5% (the scoped `set` +0.9%, its `insert` +1.0%, the server token's `set` +0.5%, its wake +0.1%), a page of 20 read in process 0.4 to 0.9% faster and a row by id over HTTP 0 to 0.8% faster -- but a page of 20 as a `POST /query` over HTTP +3.1% under the server's token, +2.8% `plain` and +4.4% `reach`, every pair on its side, and -1.0% `fields`, where the first head had measured -0.6 to +0.3%. Counted through `handle` in process, 200 000 such reads less a run of none, five runs a side, that read is main's: under the server's token 164 566 instructions on main, 164 598 on the first head and 164 617 on the fixed one, under `reach` 273 069, 273 115 and 273 016, and its cycles main's too (29 836 against 29 856 a read, 7 runs in turns); the whole bench under the server's token -- its server's threads and its client's, writes and reads, in process and over loopback -- 15 075.6 M instructions on main and 15 070.6 M on the branch. A read does no more work than on main: the charge the fixed head sets in a scoped token's scope is three words written as the token checks out, the wake and the grants are never asked by a read, and the server token's read makes no scope at all, yet moved as much. It moved with where the stack lies: `run_query`'s frame grew 32 bytes, which left `codec::text_at`'s 16-byte answer in `query_json` across two cache lines, a store a load right after cannot take from the store queue -- 278 thousand instructions a request over HTTP on both, 78 thousand cycles against main's 73 ([http.md](http.md#json-from-stored-rows)). `query_json`'s frame is now aligned to a line, its slots placed whatever the frames above it hold: main's cycles again, and with `run_query` padded 16 bytes. That row re-timed alone, the four policies, 7 pairs: timing pending (`/tmp/claude-scratch-f2s4http/timing/results.txt` on the dev machine).

**Slice 5, the surfaces.** *What it lets a user do: see what a binding did and why it refused, wherever a write is answered, counted, logged or traced, and keep a synced app writing a bound collection.* Nothing a binding does changes here; what changes is who is told it.

*`binding` in every error.* Slice 4 named the binding to the server's token on REST's routes and an unkeyed `/query` and `/_call`. The words and the name now come from the engine, `Database::refused_by` -- the binding `Database::woken` notes and "bound statement `voided`, after `set` on `orders`, refused this write: " -- in every build, `None` without the `statements` feature, so the module carries an always-`None` look and no words. It is asked with the error it is told beside, which must be the one the binding refused with: the note stands until the next statement ends, and slice 4's read named a binding beside any later error a write met before it ran -- a follower's, a fence's, a storage failure's -- which ends no statement. A keyed `/query` (and so a keyed `/_call`) answers through `told_response` as the unkeyed one does, its answer kept with the key as told; `/batch`'s `Stop` gains the name (`api::bound_stop`), read before the block is put back, and `render_batch_stop` writes `binding` beside `at`, `completed` and `reason`; `fennec_abi::Refused::Error` carries the pair, read in `run_text` before `rollback`, and `answer` writes the engine's words after the binding's with `binding` beside `reason` and `at` (`refused_bound`, cold), so the native library's every SDK gets it with slice 6's field; the shell prints the words before the error. A scoped token's answers are what slice 4 made them: `bound_by` asks nothing for it, and `told_bound` and `told_call` drop the name.

*The engine's note, on the request's thread.* The design had the runs handed over in `Database::woken`, read under the write lock after each statement. Built so, every way a write comes in -- `/query` keyed or not, `/batch`'s loop, REST's `rest_run`, a call's body, whose statements' notes the next statement lets go of, a held write's runs -- would have owed its own read, and a way in forgetting one would count nothing. What the bindings did is noted instead in a thread-local of `engine/bound.rs` (`Runs`), on the bound path alone, and the server takes it once a request where it counts and logs the request (`Database::take_runs`, in `serve_connection` before `metrics::record`, where `Database::runs_noted` -- a thread-local `bool` every note sets -- says there is something, out of line in `bound_noted`): a write runs on the thread serving its request, under the write lock, whichever way it came, so no way in can forget it. Each entry is a binding a statement (`Ran`): its name, its version from `Stored::version` (`Woken::versions`, filled in `bind`), its rows, when its first run started and the time its runs took together -- `runs` reads the clock as the runs start, as they end, and where the next run is another binding's, so twice a binding a statement where the rows wake one binding, as the design asked; the rows-then-names order is kept, so interleaved bindings read it at each switch. `refused` notes the count and the last refusal's binding and engine words; `define_statement` and `drop_statement` note a bound statement's collection and events (`Runs::changed`), which a drop's text does not say. A thread no server takes it from -- the shell's, an app's -- keeps at most `RUNS_KEPT` (256) entries, a binding's runs past it added to its last; `statements::clear` lets it go with the request's text where a held run is run again and where `fennec mcp` keeps no counts. A request with no binding pays the `bool`'s look as it is counted (*Time*, below).

*Watching.* `/_metrics`' `fennec_bound_runs_total` (a run a row a binding ran for) and `fennec_bound_refused_total`, per-thread shards as every counter, no label; `monitoring/datadog/conf.yaml` keeps both, which `tests/datadog.rs` holds to a scrape. `/_stats/statements` counts each binding as the shape `after <name>` (`statements::record_bound`): calls the statements that woke it, rows its rows, time its runs' with their least and most, an error where it refused the write; the writer's shape is counted as before, its time holding the binding's, and `record`'s path for a request with no binding is the code it was but for one look at `bound`. The slow line's `bound` is `[["paid_ledger",[3,41],12]]`, a binding and version's rows over the request (`audit::Field::Json`). A span `bound <name>` a `Ran`, recorded once the request has run (`trace::bound_runs`) as a child of the request's span, its start the engine's `Instant` taken back from the clock now, the binding that refused marked failed with the status the error answers (`api::status_of`) and the reason its `else` wrote (`Error::reason`), `error.type` the status: never the engine's words, which may hold a row's value -- a unique value its body met is named in them, "document 4 holds \"a@b\" already" -- and a span names a shape. `Runs::refusal` holds the error, the audit's `bound` line writing its words, the span reading its status and reason (`a_durable_write_is_traced_through_the_lock_the_disk_and_the_replicas` holds a clash's value out of every post). The audit: a `schema` line naming a bound statement's definition, replacement or drop carries `bound_collection` and `bound_events`, from the note; a scoped writer's write a binding refused writes a `bound` line -- the binding, the engine's words, the method, the path and the status -- flagged where `within` tells a refusal as `told_bound` and where a scoped call's body was refused (`audit::told_bound`), written once the answer is (`audit::bound`), outside the lock, waiting and clearing nothing, as `unwoken` does.

*Listings and `/_schema`.* `statements` gains `after`, `["orders", "set or del"]` from the record, so a binding this process holds no tree for is listed too, and `describe statement` `condition`, the `where` as its text writes it: no build writes an expression back as text, and the tree's `Binding` holding the text beside the condition would change its `Debug` form, which `make fennecql-corpus` compares; `named::condition_text` reads it from the stored text, stepping over strings, comments and brackets (`a_condition_is_read_from_its_text_as_written`). Both are the server's token's, refused a scoped one as before. `GET /_schema` writes, to the server's token alone, each collection's bindings beside it (`"bindings": [{"name", "events", "text"}]`, `declared::describe_with`, generic so the module's `describe` is the code it was), and `?as=fennecql` each binding's definition after the collections; `declared::parse` reads a collection's `bindings` into `Declared::bound`, and `fennec_ql::bound_into` (called by `fennec_abi::read`, natively) reads each text as the definition of the statement it is listed by, bound to the collection it is listed beside, so either form goes back through `/_schema/plan` as a plan of nothing. A binding's `events` are its text's: left out, they are read from it; listed, they are read back (`Binding::events_read`, any order and case, SQL's `update` and `delete`) and held to the text's, a description claiming others refused naming both -- never taken, as nothing else would ever check them. A scoped token is described what it may read, no binding beside it.

*The claim on a derived collection.* Held writes needed nothing new (*Held writes wake on derived rows*, above): `a_claim_held_on_a_derived_collection_is_answered_by_the_write_making_its_row` holds a `set ledger` with `Fennec-Wait` until a `set orders` lands the line its binding inserts.

*The sync.* `integrations/sync-scenarios.json` gained four scenarios before either runner was looked at, and both passed them unchanged, as the design expected: a synced `set` waking a binding is sent alone, the optimistic view holding no derived row, and the ledger line arrives through a shape following `ledger` in the change the write's `Fennec-Seq` names; a binding's guard refusing puts the write back with its `reason`; a write whose answer was lost is sent again under its key and one ledger line arrives; and `create statement ... after` on a synced database is `NAMED`'s refusal. `make sync-scenarios-check`: all 88 held by both runners.

*The router.* `admin::schema_description` read a binding already: fennec-shard's grammar is fennec-ql's with the feature. Held now: a tenant's schema declaring a binding after the collections it binds and writes reads, one before them is refused before anything is placed, and the router's tenant whose schema binds `stamps` runs the binding at the node with the call's insert. No line of the router's request path changed, so `make shard-bench ARGS=latency` owes nothing.

*Tests.* fennec-http: `/query` keyed and `/batch` naming the binding to the server's token (`a_binding_s_refusal_is_told_the_writer_as_its_token_may_know_it`); the listings, `/_schema` in both forms read back by the plan, and a scoped token told no name, text or written collection; the held claim; the `bound` and `schema` audit lines (`tests/audit.rs`). fennec-server: the counters and the slow line (`tests/metrics.rs`), the `after` shapes (`tests/statements.rs`), the spans (`tests/tracing.rs`). fennec-abi: the native answer's `binding` and `at`. fennec-core: `condition_text`. fennec-shard: the tenant's binding through the router.

*Size.* `make size-report BASE=origin/main`: the module's code is main's to the byte (its file 16 bytes smaller, gzip and brotli within their noise), and the builds without the indexes and without any feature too. The first build carried 0.8 KB: `Refused::Error`'s fourth word moved with every refusal, the arm writing `binding` and the description's `bindings` read -- none of which the module can meet, as it runs no binding -- so `fennec_abi::Bound` is `()` on wasm32, `Declared::bound` and `fennec_ql::bound_into` native, and `describe_with`'s closure the empty one where the module describes. `fennec-server` (`BIN=fennec-server`, main's tree built beside) is 30.4 KB of code larger, 4162.4 to 4192.8 KB: fennec-http 14.9 KB -- `/_schema`'s `bindings` and `?as=fennecql`, the audit's `bound` line and schema fields, the `after <name>` shapes, the spans, the counters and the slow line's `bound`, `bound_noted` 4.6 KB of it --, fennec-core 9.2 KB (the note, `refused_by`, `condition_text`, the listings' columns, a description's `bindings` read), fennec-abi 2.9 KB (`bound_into`, inlined in `read`), the rest std's. Each is a surface the slice was asked for, on the server alone. `serve_connection` is 0.5 KB larger than main's (53.8 to 54.3 KB), the bound path out of it and only the look and the call to `bound_noted` in -- the rest the inlined routes' refusals naming a binding (`bound_by`).

*Time.* A request with no binding pays one thread-local `bool`'s look as it is counted (`Database::runs_noted`); `metrics::record`, `statements::record` and `audit::statement` are main's, a request's reset in `audit::request` one more `Cell`, and nothing of the engine's write path changed but the `bool` set beside each note. The first build took the note itself every request (a `RefCell` borrow and three looks) and passed an `Option<Runs>` -- seven words -- through the counts; that went out of the loop before the measure. `make requests-bench`'s bench against main, seven pairs in turns, each run made once the 1-minute load is under 1.5: timing pending (`/tmp/claude-scratch-f2s5/timing/results.txt` on the dev machine).

**Slice 6, the SDKs, the studio and the shell -- F2 complete.** *What it lets a user do: tell, in every SDK, which binding refused a write, and see a collection's bindings in the studio and change one there.* No Rust changed but the shell's help text: the server and the native library name the binding since slice 5, and this slice reads it.

*The SDKs.* Each reads `binding` from a refusal's body where it reads `reason`, a text or nothing: JavaScript's `FennecError.binding` (`refused` in `web/http.js`, declared in `fennec.d.ts`, which `client.d.ts` re-exports), Python's `FennecError.binding` (`_answer`), Go's `Error.Binding` (`errorOf`), .NET's `FennecException.Binding` (`FennecException.From`, the constructor's last optional argument), Swift's `FennecError.binding` and Kotlin's and Dart's `FennecException.binding` (each `FennecRemote.refused`). The three over the native library read it from fennec-abi's error too, where slice 5 put it (Swift's `Fennec.call`, Kotlin's `FennecNative.answer`, Dart's `_answer`), so an app's own database names the binding as a server does to its token. A scoped token is told none, as the server sends none. Not read: a write `FennecSync` or the native sync was refused, whose refusal says its `reason` alone -- `fennec_abi::sync`'s `Said` reads no `binding`, so both runners stay where `integrations/sync-scenarios.json` holds them, and a synced app's writer is most often a scoped token, told no name anyway -- and the page's database, whose module runs no binding. *Tests*, each failing with the field taken out: a guard refusing over HTTP, 412 with its `reason` and `binding`, a `/batch`'s beside `at`, and a refusal no binding made naming none -- JavaScript (`fennec.sync.test.js`, against the server), Python (`test_call.py`), Go (`TestABindingThatRefusesAWriteIsNamed`), .NET (`ABindingThatRefusesAWriteIsNamed`), Swift's and Kotlin's remote suites; through the native library, Swift's `EmbeddedCall`, Kotlin's `CallLocalTest` and Dart's `fennecdb_test.dart`, which holds `FennecRemote.refused` to a server's body as its `reason` test does.

*The studio.* The schema view lists a collection's bindings from `/_schema`, which names them beside it to the server's token alone (`"bindings": [{"name", "events", "text"}]`, slice 5): a table under its fields, *Statements bound to its writes*, a row each -- its name, `after insert or set` -- and a button opening its text in the query editor as `create or replace statement ...` (F1's `replacing`). The named statements' list reads `statements`' `after`: a binding is marked `bound`, listed as `after insert or set bills` where a callable statement shows its signature (a binding's is always `()`), and has no call form, since only the writes it binds run it; its definition's dialog says when it runs, `describe statement`'s `condition` with it, where a callable one says what it answers. No statement is new: the view opens texts the server gave it and sends nothing it did not send before, so `statements*.js` and `transport.test.mjs` are as they were -- the page's database describes no binding, holding none it runs. The highlighter's words gain `after`, `new` and `old` (`site/build.py`'s `KEYWORDS`; `make studio-highlight` writes the studio's and the Grafana data source's copies): `new` beside the plan's `old`, since a binding's condition reads both and one marked without the other reads as a slip. `e2e.test.mjs`'s `the schema: a collection's bindings, each opened as its replacement, none called` drives both lists, the replacement in the editor and the dialog. *Size*: `schema.js` +1 513 bytes raw, +324 gzipped (8 258 to 8 582), within the 24 000 a view and the modules it shares are held to; `highlight.js` +20 raw, +10 gzipped; the first load unchanged, the view loading apart from it.

*The shell.* `.help` gives the bound form beside the callable one, `after insert|set|del [or ...] <collection> [where ...] [comment '...'] [scans] as ...`.

*Docs.* The FennecQL reference's refusal paragraph names each SDK's field, the studio's lists and `.help`; the languages page lists the field per SDK beside `reason`, the JavaScript, mobile and studio pages theirs, and README's row on statements bound to writes says both.

### F2's risks

- *An internal writer forgotten*, running a binding twice or at a time no write chose. Most cannot: a replica, a load, a compact and the sweep never reach `run_one`. The rest are `Database::unbound`'s, each a case of the test above, and CLAUDE.md's rule names them, so a new one owes a line there.
- *A way in forgotten*, landing a write without its rows. `run_one` is the one door every statement passes; the test runs each way in.
- *`new.` trusted where its writer chooses it.* A binding is as safe as the policy over its collection: the reference and auth pages carry the debit with its policy line, and a binding's guards are where a definer checks what a row says.
- *Grants to keep.* A binding woken by a role with no `wake` line refuses that role's writes once a row passes its condition: loud, named in the audit, the price of a policy that says what a token may cause. A binding defined after the policy wakes for no scoped token until a line names it.
- *Write amplification.* A run a row: a `put` of 1 000 likes writes a post's counter 1 000 times into one record. Bounded by `MAX_WOKEN`, measured in slice 3; a binding over a whole statement is the follow-up if the record shows the cost.
- *The write lock held longer*, by a write's bindings: bounded by `MAX_WOKEN` and `--call-max-rows`, shown by the slow line and the spans.
- *Availability.* A broken binding stops its collection's writes until it is defined again or dropped, loud by choice; only an internal writer or a grammar change can break one, which the commit's check and the corpus rule keep rare.
- *An older node promoted* stops at op 3 rather than writing without the binding: the upgrade's order keeps the cluster writing.
- *The sync's optimistic view* shows a write before its derived rows, said on the sync page and held by a scenario.
- *CLAUDE.md's room*: the FennecQL rules are in [crates/fennec-ql/CLAUDE.md](../../crates/fennec-ql/CLAUDE.md), an area file under 24 000 bytes, since the root was split (#471): slice 3's rule took slice 2's place there, the root's index naming its title; slice 4's sentence goes into the access rule's file.

### What CLAUDE.md would gain from F2

Slice 3 replaced it, in `crates/fennec-ql/CLAUDE.md`, with *A write-bound statement runs in the block of the write that woke it, once a row*: the record and the mask as slice 2 had them, the asking and the run, depth one, the writers under `Database::unbound` a new internal writer owes, and a scoped token's wake refused until slice 4. Slice 2 had brought its own rule, after the named statements' one, *A write-bound statement's record binds its collection's writes*: op 3 holds the collection and events outside the text, which a binary from before refuses; every build reads them into `Collection::bound`, which every change keeps (`Database::rebind`), so a collection no binding names pays one byte's look a writing statement; until the engine runs one a definition is refused (`BOUND_NOT_BUILT`) and so is each write it binds, 409 where one is broken or unread; `@ttl` and `after del` refuse each other; a replacement keeps the collection, the events and each kind of write, a `put` making and setting rows. To keep CLAUDE.md under its 64 000 bytes the named statements' rule said its feature's sentence shorter. Slice 3 replaces the refusal's sentence with the rule below.

Under *FennecQL and schemas*, beside the named statements' rule, with slice 3:

> **[A write-bound statement runs in the block of the write that woke it, once a row.](docs/design/named-statements.md#write-bound-statements-f2)** `create statement <n> after insert|set|del <c> [where ...] as ...` is kind 13's op 3, which a binary from before refuses, and a build or a server that cannot run one refuses the writes it binds. `run_one` alone wakes it, after the statement's rows, hooks and `require` and before the block lands, row by row, its bindings in name order; it is never called, its writes wake none and it writes no bound collection; its condition is asked as each row is written, a write over an expired row is an `insert`, and `after del` and `@ttl` refuse each other; past `MAX_WOKEN` woken rows or `--call-max-rows` a write is refused, never cut, and a refusal puts the block back, a scoped writer told its status, `at` and `reason` alone. A scoped token wakes only what a `wake` line grants it (403, never a skipped binding), its runs charged to `--call-rate`, and a replacement keeps the bound collection, events and what it writes. A replica's apply, a load, a compact and the sweep never reach it; `--follow`'s mirror, `fennec import`, a declared migration and the sync's apply run `Database::unbound`; a collection with no binding pays one byte's look a statement (`Collection::bound`).

Slice 5 added to the binding rule in `crates/fennec-ql/CLAUDE.md` that every answer but a scoped token's names the binding (`binding`, `Database::refused_by`), and that what the bindings did is noted on the writing thread (`Database::take_runs`), taken once a request for the server's counts, slow line, spans and audit, and described to the server's token alone.

Slice 4 gave *scoped tokens*' "a scoped write outside `within()` goes unchecked, so only a named statement's call runs one there (`run_as`)" its second case, in `crates/fennec-http/CLAUDE.md`: "and a binding's writes, which the `Check` hook passes over (`Hook::writer_only`) once `Hook::waking` judged it" -- the `wake` line, `* wake` refused, `append-only`, the audit's `unwoken` line, the runs charged to `--call-rate` and `judge_call` holding a body to the same; and the binding rule's last sentence in `crates/fennec-ql/CLAUDE.md` became "a scoped token wakes only what a `wake` line grants it (403, never a skipped binding), told its status and `reason` alone (`told_bound`)".

Slice 6 changes no rule: the SDKs read what the server's rule already sends, and the studio opens texts as its rule has it, sending no statement of its own.

### F2's decisions for the owner

1. **The syntax.** *Recommended: a clause of `create statement`* -- one record, one text, a bound statement never callable. The alternative, `create binding` naming a callable statement, lets one statement be called and bound, at the price of a grant writing derived rows with no write behind them.
2. **A chain.** *Recommended: refused at definition, in both orders.* The alternative, depth one with chains allowed, leaves a binding asleep for every row another wrote, without a word.
3. **A broken binding.** *Recommended: its writes refused (409)* until it is defined again or dropped. The alternative lets them land without their rows, the gap in the log alone.
4. **What a scoped writer is told.** *Recommended: its status, `at` and `reason`, no name.* The alternative names the binding, which helps a client's developer and tells every token what the schema does.
5. **Expiry.** *Recommended: no binding sees a row expire* -- the sweep wakes nothing, a write over an expired row is an `insert`, and `after del` and `@ttl` refuse each other. The alternative, `after expire`, runs a binding when the sweeper gets there, at a time no write chose; F3's scheduled statements are the form for acting on time.
6. **Where a `wake` grant is asked.** *Recommended: as a binding wakes*, a row passing its condition, and for a call before its body by what it may wake. The alternative, every write of a bound event, needs a grant from every role writing the collection -- a courier moving an order's location granted the ledger -- for no binding it could wake.
7. **What `--call-rate` charges.** *Recommended: each run* a scoped writer's write wakes, refused past a full bucket. The alternative, one charge a binding a statement, lets one `put` of 10 000 rows run 10 000 bodies under the write lock for the price of one.

## Scheduled statements (F3)

**Status: built, slices 1 to 6 -- F3 complete ([below](#what-f3-has-built)): the parser and the tree, the record every build keeps, a file's server running them, a tenant node running its tenants', what watches them -- the listings, `/_schema`, the slow line, the traces, `/_stats/statements`, `/_metrics` and the Datadog monitors -- and the clients: the studio's schedules and its run now, every SDK's typed listing, the shell's `.help`.** A named statement run by the clock -- `every 1h`, or at the times a cron expression names on a zone's clock -- by the primary that may write its file, each run and the mark of the slot it ran for landing as one block: a crash, a failover or a move leaves a slot run once or not at all in the history that survives, and whoever writes the file next runs it once -- once in that history, not once to everyone who looked ([below](#where-a-run-happens-and-once)). This section decides what [F1's paragraph](#what-it-does-not-do) left open and keeps what it promised -- the primary alone, a run a block keyed by its slot, once behind its schedule, the tenant's quota and `--call-max-rows`, `now()` read once a run, a last-run record that replicates, a router that knows nothing of it -- but the form: no parentheses and no `public` (its `purge() public`), which a statement no one calls has no use for, and `slot()` beside `now()`. Its rule is in `crates/fennec-ql/CLAUDE.md`, slice 3's ([below](#what-claudemd-would-gain-from-f3)).

```fennecql
create statement lapse_holds
  every 1m
  comment 'Lapses each hold whose time is up, a thousand a minute'
as set holds {state: 'lapsed', lapsed_at: now()}
   where state = 'held' and until < now() order until limit 1000
```

```fennecql
create statement monthly_reset
  at '0 0 1 * *' in 'Europe/Berlin'
  scans
as set plans {used: 0, period: slot()} where used > 0
```

With F2's binding `after set holds where new.state = 'lapsed' and old.state = 'held'` giving the hold's stock back, the stock returns in the run's block: acting on time with a write a binding sees.

### The form of a schedule

**A clause of `create statement`, as `after` is.** `create [or replace] statement <name> (every <interval> | at '<cron>') [in '<zone>'] [comment '<text>'] [scans] as <body>`. The schedule is its statement's: one name, one record, one text the digest covers, and `drop statement` takes both away. Rejected:

- *pg_cron's job naming a command* (`cron.schedule('purge', '0 3 * * *', 'call purge()')`), here `create schedule <s> at '...' call <statement>`: two records with a dependency between them, two texts to read for what runs when, and the statement it names callable, so a token granted `purge call` runs the nightly job whenever it likes. A statement wanted on a schedule and on demand is two texts, or the server's token's call ([below](#a-runs-authority)).
- *A schedule in the server's flags or a file beside the database* (`--schedule purge=1h`): a schedule is the data's, kept by the file's images, archives and moves; a tenant moved to another node would leave it behind.
- *`after` and a schedule on one statement*: refused, naming the two statements to write. A binding runs on its writer's row, a schedule on none.

**`every <interval>` runs at the edges `bucket` cuts.** The interval is `bucket`'s (`query::Interval`, [lookup-and-aggregates.md](lookup-and-aggregates.md#time-zones)) -- a whole number of minutes, hours, days or weeks counted from the epoch, a week from a Monday, or of calendar months (`mo`, `y`) -- and its slots are the starts of the buckets `bucket(t, <interval>, <zone>)` gives: `every 1h` runs at each hour's start and `every 15m` at :00, :15, :30 and :45, never an interval after its definition, so a schedule's slots are the edges a report grouped by `bucket` shows. Anchored to the epoch rather than to its definition, a slot is the same instant on every node that computes it, which is what keys a run (below). `every 7m` is a cadence of seven minutes from the epoch; `at '*/7 * * * *'` is cron's, starting again each hour.

**`at '<cron>'` is cron's five fields** -- minute, hour, day of month, month, day of week -- each `*`, a number, a range `a-b`, a list `a,b` or a step `*/n` or `a-b/n`, Sunday 0 or 7, and the three-letter English names of months and days: the expression pg_cron, Cloud Scheduler and PocketBase take. It is a text literal, since `*` and `/` are operators where FennecQL reads an expression.

**`in '<zone>'` names the clock the schedule reads**: one of IANA's zones by its name or a fixed offset (`'+03:00'`), as `bucket` takes one (`tz.rs`); UTC without it. A cron slot is a time on that clock, run at the first instant the clock reads it or later: a time the clock skips -- 02:30 the night summer time starts -- runs at the instant it jumps past it, and a time it shows twice runs once, at the first. Every time a skipped hour holds is that one slot, the jump's -- `*/15 2 * * *` runs once that night where it ran four times the night before -- and every time the repeated hour holds runs at its first instant alone, the second pass running none. That is the rule `bucket` gives a day's start; an `every` slot is a bucket's start, so the hour a clock shows twice is two slots and one it skips none, an hour of elapsed time a run. Cron's daemons disagree on both nights, and the rule the engine holds already is one less to learn. A zone's rules are the table its binary was built with (`tools/tz/gen.py`): a country changing its clock is an upgrade, as it is for `bucket`. A zone's name is checked against that table at definition, by the parser (slice 1, [below](#what-f3-has-built)).

**The refusals the text alone answers**, each at definition, naming the form to write:

- an interval under a minute, or not a whole number of minutes (`every 30s`, `every 90s`): the floor, `named::MIN_PERIOD`, is a minute, cron's finest step and the sweep's period ([decision 3](#f3s-decisions-for-the-owner));
- a cron expression of other than five fields -- six, Quartz's seconds, refused naming `every` -- a value outside its field, a step of 0, a range running backwards; Quartz's `?`, `L`, `W` and `#`, and pg_cron's `$`; a macro, `@hourly` named as `every 1h` and `@daily` as `every 1d`, `@reboot` as no time at all;
- a day of month and a day of week both restricted. POSIX's `crontab` and Vixie cron run on either, and a reader takes it for both: `0 0 13 * 5`, Friday the 13th to its writer, runs every Friday and every 13th. Refused, naming the two statements that say either, and a guard in the body that says both. Restricted is read off the text, as cron reads it, never off the values named: POSIX's `crontab`, Vixie cron and pg_cron count a field not starting with `*` as restricted, so `0 0 1-31 * mon` runs every day there and `0 0 13 * 0-6` too, where the values ANDed would run on Mondays and on the 13th alone -- a copied expression running on fewer days without a word. A field is open as `*` alone, and an expression with both fields written otherwise is refused whatever they name: a step is no POSIX form, and its readers part on it -- Vixie cron and pg_cron count any field starting with `*` as open, `*/2` too, robfig's Go cron `*/1` and not `*/2` -- so `*/1`, open to those three, is held to the rule as well. A refusal costs its writer one edit; a reading other than the one an expression was copied from costs runs;
- an expression that never fires (`0 0 30 2 *`), its days held to the months it names, 29 February admitted;
- what a statement no one calls has no use for: parameters and their parentheses, `claims` and `$jwt`, `check`, `public`, `new.` and `old.`;
- what F2's bodies refuse for the same reason, their rows going nowhere: `returning`, and a `get` without `require`; and a body of guards alone, a run that can change nothing.

The body is otherwise F1's ([definitions](#definitions)): at most 64 statements of `get` with `require`, `insert`, and `set` or `del` with a `where`, picked or not, reading through an index or saying `scans` -- never `put`, a parameter's documents, `else set`, a schema change, `call` or a `_` collection.

**`now()` is the run's start, read once a run; `slot()` is the instant of its slot.** A run late -- after a restart, behind another tenant's run -- reads the time every read reads: `now()` and `@ttl`'s `alive` read one clock (`Database::now`), so the run leaves out a row expired before it as every read does ([indexes.md](indexes.md#ttl)) and `until < now()` finds every hold due. A run reading its slot's time there would read rows every client is told are gone. A body labelling what it did by the period it ran for reads `slot()`, a timestamp, the same whether the run was on time or hours late: `monthly_reset`'s `period` is the month's first midnight in Berlin even when a restart ran it at 00:40, as Firebase hands a scheduled function its `scheduleTime`. `slot()` outside a scheduled body is refused at definition, as `new.` outside a binding is ([decision 4](#f3s-decisions-for-the-owner)).

A database schedules at most `named::MAX_SCHEDULED` statements, 16, refused past it, never cut ([crates.md](crates.md#limits)): at the floor, sixteen runs a minute a database.

### Where a run happens, and once

**On the primary that may write its file, and nowhere else.** A run is a write, so it happens where writes land. A server's scheduler (`fennec_http::schedule`, one thread a process, as the sweep's is) looks at each database the process serves and runs nothing on one that cannot take a write of its own:

- *a following database* -- a `--replica-of` server, a tenant's standby, a read replica, a node's following tenant -- whose writes are its primary's, the runs among them (`history().following`, asked as the sweep asks it). A promoted copy runs its schedules from its promotion; a read replica is never promoted ([read-replicas.md](read-replicas.md#marked-reads)).
- *a tenant whose lease lapsed*: the engine's fence (`Database::set_fence`) refuses the run's block as it refuses every write, so a node cut off from its router lands no run past its lease.
- *a frozen tenant*: a run holds the tenant's gate as a request does (`Tenant::enter`) and passes over a frozen one, so no run lands on a move's source after the export its target was made from. The sweep calls the database directly today and could land a batch there; its deletes the target's sweep finds again, where a run's writes would be lost with the source.
- *a database whose storage failed* (`Database::failure`), until it is opened again ([durability.md](durability.md#storage-errors)).
- *a server under `--http-read-only`*: an operator who turns a server's writes off expects its file to change by nothing a statement says. The sweep goes on, changing nothing a read sees; the scheduler does not start, and the server says at the start how many scheduled statements it holds unrun.
- *the shell, `--read-only` or not, the native library and the browser module*: none holds a scheduler. The shell holds the file for a session; the native library is an app's, whose process its operating system suspends and kills at will ([decision 8](#f3s-decisions-for-the-owner)); the module has neither thread nor clock ([browser-module.md](browser-module.md#no-threads-in-wasm)).

**A run's writes and its slot's mark are one block.** A run begins a block, runs the body as the server's token's call runs it -- each statement through `run_one`, its writes waking the bindings they reach -- and appends the mark of its slot, kind 13's op 5 ([below](#a-schedules-record-replicas-and-upgrades)), before the block lands. The slot is to a run what an `Idempotency-Key` is to a write: its record lands with what it made ([http.md](http.md#idempotency-keys)). Whatever holds the run holds its mark, and whatever lost one lost both -- a crash before the fsync, a replica the stream had not reached, an archive's end, a move's export. The scheduler runs only the next slot after the last mark, landed or pending, asked again under the write lock it runs under (`Database::due`), as a call is asked under its lock whether it writes: no slot runs twice in one history -- but behind a mark a wrong clock left far ahead (below) -- and the slot a lost run was for is due again to whoever writes the file next. The run is then made durable as any write is, after the lock: its fsync and, under `--sync always`, the streams keeping up (`Feed::wait_sent`), which leaves a failover less to lose.

**A run that lands nothing writes nothing.** A slot finding nothing -- a purge of an empty range, a guard unmet, a broken statement ([below](#when-a-run-refuses)) -- must not cost an fsync, a record to an archive and a send to each standby, ten thousand times at 03:00 on a node. So a run whose block holds no write of its own lands no block: its mark is kept in memory beside the table (`Database::pending`) and lands as the last record of the next block that does -- any write's, the sweep's and another run's included, `commit` appending it -- or with the checkpoint the one shutdown writes, whose image holds the table's marks. On a tenant node the scheduler takes a closing tenant's pending marks by name, with the history and change counter its file had, and the hint keeps them ([below](#where-a-run-happens-and-once)); handed back as the tenant opens, they hold only while its file's history and counter are still those. A pending mark counts as one write where it lands (`writes_in`), and a block put back keeps it pending. Exactly once still holds, by three facts. A pending mark is lost only with nothing written after it -- the next write would have carried it -- so the slot due again in the surviving history is run over the data the lost run read, at a later `now()`. The lost run wrote nothing a reader, a stream, a consumer, a binding or a held write could see, so the run made again is the first that history shows of its slot. And a pending mark kept outside the database counts only while the counter is the one its run read, so it never stands for a run over data that changed. A run made again may find what the lost one did not, rows expired since, as a run made late by a restart does; a body that must leave a trace of a slot that found nothing writes one.

**After a failover the promoted copy holds the run and its mark, or neither.** A node lands a run's block under its lease; then:

| What happened | The promoted copy holds | It runs |
|---|---|---|
| the stream sent the block before the node was lost | the run and its mark | the next slot, when due |
| the block never left the node: a lagging stream not waited for past `SENT_WAIT`, a crash before the fsync | neither | that slot, once, at its first look as primary |
| the lease lapsed while the run held the lock | nothing: the fence refused the block, put back whole | that slot, once |
| the old node comes back | -- | nothing: it follows the promoted copy, its own block on a history leading nowhere, never applied or sent |

**Once in the history that survives, not once to everyone who looked.** The mark makes a slot's run once in each history; it cannot unsee a run a history lost, and two cases lose one. After a crash under a buffered `--sync` (`off` or `<ms>`), a run landed and not yet synced is gone from the file that reopens: a reader, or a worker whose held `claim` the run answered, may have acted on it. After a lagging standby's promotion -- a stream past `SENT_WAIT`, not waited for, the old node's fsync done -- a `/_changes` consumer of the old node may have too. In both the surviving history makes the slot's run again, at a later `now()` and the same `slot()`, the history (`Fennec-History`) telling the two apart. That is what a failover does to any write landed and lost, `outcome: unknown`'s window ([tenants.md](tenants.md#scaling-out-by-tenant), `answered_under_lease`) with no client to tell: the run's lease is asked again as its durability ends, and one lapsed meanwhile is logged and counted (`outcome="unknown"`). A schedule whose effects leave the database -- a job row a worker turns into mail, a row a consumer forwards -- runs on a server under `--sync always`: there a worker's claim, a later write, is answered only once its own fsync and streams, which cover the run's block, are done, and `/_changes` serves only what an fsync covered, so neither acts on a run a crash can take back, a stream lagging past `SENT_WAIT` the window left. Or its body makes its mark durable in its data: `slot()` in each row it hands out, so a worker or a consumer acts once per name and slot whatever history it read. A plain read may see a run before its fsync under any `--sync`, as it may any write. A move is the same table with the freeze before it: the source runs nothing after the export, the target runs the slot due by its file, and a move undone (`thaw`) leaves the source to run it.

**The clock moves the slots; a mark ahead of it is an anomaly, never a stop.** Slots are instants read off the wall clock, a lease off the boot clock (`lease::Moment`). A zone's summer time moves no instant, so it steps nothing: its skipped and repeated local times are [the form's rule](#the-form-of-a-schedule). A clock stepped forward finds slots due, which run as missed ones do. A clock stepped back that stays past the last mark runs nothing again, the next slot after the mark coming when the clock reaches it. A clock stepped back behind the last mark -- or behind `since` -- finds a mark ahead of it, which a clock right then and right now cannot leave: one of its two readings was wrong. Each is counted (`fennec_scheduled_mark_ahead_total`) and an audit line (`audit::clock`) names the statement, the mark and the clock, once a statement and mark; and then:

- *ahead by at most `named::AHEAD_MAX`, an hour* (`Database::due` holds the rule, slice 2): the clock now is taken for the wrong reading, and the slots up to the mark, which ran, wait for it to pass them -- a pause of at most an hour, no slot run twice. A promoted copy whose clock is seconds behind its old primary's is this case.
- *further ahead*: the mark is taken for the wrong reading, left by a clock that was ahead -- a host booted with its clock a year out and put right by NTP after its first runs -- and the schedule goes on from the clock, its next slot the first the clock reaches after `since`, its run's mark replacing the one ahead. The slots the wrong clock ran early run again as they come: the one way a slot runs twice in a history, counted and logged as it begins. A `since` that far ahead -- a definition made by such a clock -- anchors nothing either: with no anchor left, the latest slot runs once and its mark anchors the slots after it (slice 3's review: taken for the time itself, `since` was read again at every look and the schedule never ran).

Waiting for the clock to reach a mark a year ahead is every schedule of the database stopped for a year without a word; going on from the clock after every small step is a slot run twice each time NTP steps a clock back past a run. An hour bounds the pause and leaves the steps NTP makes in service alone. A replacement clears the mark ([below](#where-a-run-happens-and-once)), an operator's way out of either case.

**Missed slots run once, at the latest.** A server down for a day, a tenant frozen for an hour, a clock stepped forward: at its first look the scheduler finds the slots S1 < ... < Sk due after the mark and runs Sk alone, its mark counting the k - 1 it passed over (`skipped`) for the listing, the metric and the log. A look runs at most one slot a statement, so a restart after a week is one run a statement, never a week's, and no catch-up holds the write lock longer than a run does. Rejected ([decision 2](#f3s-decisions-for-the-owner)):

- *every slot missed*: a minute's cadence down for a day is 1 440 runs at the start, under the write lock every client waits on, to do what one run does for a purge, a lapse or a reset. A statement accounting for each period keeps its place in its data -- a row naming the last period done -- as a consumer keeps its place in `/_changes`.
- *none until the next slot*, pg_cron's and cron's: a monthly reset missed by a restart at midnight waits a month.

**A definition starts its schedule at its own landing.** Op 4 holds `since`, the time its block landed, and the first slot due is the first after both `since` and the last mark: a statement defined at 02:59 `every 1h` runs at 03:00, one defined at 03:01 at 04:00, none at once for a slot from before it existed. A replacement moves `since` to its own landing and clears the mark, which `since` makes needless -- no slot before it runs -- and which, kept, would hold a replaced schedule behind a mark a wrong clock left ahead: a schedule changed from `every 1h` to `every 1d` at 14:20 runs next at midnight. A replacement made after a clock stepped back past a run may run that slot again under the new text, its mark naming the new definition's version.

**The one shutdown asks nothing new.** A run holds the write lock from its due check to its landing, and the syncer of [the one shutdown](durability.md#the-one-shutdown) takes the write lock, syncs and exits holding it: a run in flight lands whole and is synced, or never begins. The scheduler starts where the sweep does, after both listeners are bound, since its first look may write a missed slot: a server failing at a port has written only what the open does. A scheduler that cannot start its thread stops the server through `stop_with`, as anything beside the database does, and so does one whose thread ends -- a panic outside a run's own catch, in a look, a log line or a sync -- a server answering while its schedules never run is the silent gap F2 refused for bindings. A run's error stops nothing; a storage error stops writes, as ever.

**A tenant node runs its tenants' schedules from one thread, a tenant at a time.** The scheduler keeps a queue of next slots -- a tenant by its name, a statement, an instant -- never a tenant's `Arc`, which would keep its database open past `--idle-close` and the registry from closing it on its last; it runs the earliest due, one run at a time, each bounded by `--call-max-rows`. Where several tenants are due at once it takes one run from each before a second from any (round robin by tenant), so ten thousand tenants due at 03:00 run in turn and no tenant's sixteen statements put the others behind them. A run is late by the runs before it, measured (below) and counted (`fennec_scheduled_lateness_seconds`). A pool of schedulers is a follow-up if the measured lateness at a node's tenant count passes a minute: each tenant's runs take its own write lock, so threads would not contend, but each holds an open tenant and its fsyncs. pg_cron runs 32 jobs at once (`cron.max_running_jobs`), and Supabase advises no more than 8.

**An idle tenant is opened when its slot comes, and a run is no use of it.** A node closes a tenant untouched for `--idle-close` (300 s) or over `--max-memory`, so most of its tenants are closed most of the time; a closed tenant is not a server down, and its daily statement must run every day. So the node keeps beside the registry a hint of its own (`schedule::Hint`) naming the tenants that hold a schedule, and those alone, each with its next slot and its pending marks ([above](#where-a-run-happens-and-once)), written whole by rename at most once a minute and a line appended, synced, as a tenant enters it -- a pending mark lost with a hint not yet written being a run made again over the data it read -- and read at the start: a start opens no tenant before its slot, and none that holds no schedule. A tenant enters the hint, synced, before every block bringing a schedule lands -- its first, and each replacing or adding one, so a crash never finds a later slot than the one defined -- and leaves it only once its last one's drop is durable, so the hint may name a tenant holding none -- opened once, at its slot, to learn so -- and never misses one that holds one. Every way a file comes onto the node -- an install, a restore, a move's target, a promotion -- enters its tenant, learned at its first open; a file copied into `--dir` behind the node's back is unknown until something opens it ([risks](#f3s-risks)). A node starting with no hint -- its first start running F3, or a hint deleted -- opens each tenant once, one at a time on the scheduler's thread before its first look, to write one, and says so as it begins: the only start that opens them all. The hint is derived and never the truth, as the HNSW graph is: the slots are computed again from the tenant's file as the scheduler opens it -- through the registry, as a request opens it, under its lock and the memory ceiling -- so a hint too early opens a tenant to find nothing due. A run's open is no use: it leaves `last_used` as it was, a tenant opened for a run counting as never used, so `make_room` evicts it before any tenant a client touched, never a hot one for it; and the scheduler closes a tenant it opened once its run is done, unless a request opened it meanwhile. Rejected ([decision 7](#f3s-decisions-for-the-owner)): *keeping a scheduled tenant open*, which takes `--idle-close` and `--max-memory` from every tenant with a nightly purge; *running a closed tenant's slots as it next opens*, before it serves -- its writes would read as if on time, but an idle month would be one run where the schedule said thirty, and the opening request would wait on it.

**Held to the tenant's bytes and rows, never its rate.** The quota is asked of the run's block as it lands (`Database::set_limit`): past its bytes or rows the run is refused (507) and its slot left due, tried again as the room may come back ([below](#when-a-run-refuses)), and a purge, only taking away, is let through as any such block is ([quotas.md](quotas.md#tenant-quotas)). A mark never makes a block growth: each replaces the mark before it, so the file's live bytes do not grow, and `grows` (`engine/limit.rs`), which counts every kind 13 record as growth today, passes over op 5 -- a client's purge carrying a pending mark is let through as it was. A run's `set` is growth, as a client's is: a reset of a tenant at its quota waits for room. A tenant's rates charge a request's own thread (`writing_here`), and the scheduler's is none: a tenant's clients are not slowed by its schedule, nor its schedule by its clients.

### A run's authority

**The definition's, by the server's token, and no token's.** A run has no caller: it runs its text as the server's token's call does, checked by no scope, with no claim to bind and no `check` -- what it does is what its text says, fixed when it was defined ([authority](#authority)). Who may define one is who may define any statement: the server's token, never a scoped token, and no grant lets one. A schedule is a write at a time no request chose, made with an authority no policy line bounds, which only the holder of the schema may make. A grant to define (`purge schedule for ops`) was weighed: a policy line would let a token write a text that then runs unscoped every minute for ever, the elevation F1's definer model keeps out.

**No one calls it by name but the server's token.** A `call` line naming a scheduled statement is logged as the server starts and grants nothing, as one naming a bound statement is; a scoped token's call of one is the 404 a missing one is; MCP lists no tool for it. The server's token may `call` it to run it now -- the studio's "run now", an operator's test -- which runs the body, answers its last statement and marks no slot: the mark is the clock's, and a run on demand the operator's. Its `slot()` is the call's own time truncated to the schedule's slot: the latest slot at or before the call's `now()`, by the function `Database::due` computes slots with, `since` aside. `call monthly_reset` on 15 November writes `period` as 1 November's midnight in Berlin, the slot the scheduler ran last or runs next: a run by hand repeats the slot that has come, under its label. A slot still due runs at the scheduler's look too, the hand's run having marked nothing; a body that must not do one slot's work twice says so in its data, a row keyed by `slot()`. Refusing a call of a body reading `slot()` was weighed: it leaves untried by hand the bodies that label their work, which most need trying ([decision 6](#f3s-decisions-for-the-owner)). Every rule of this section lands with the first slice that lets a schedule be defined ([slices](#f3-in-slices)).

**Its runs wake bindings and are held to the bounds, not to a rate.** A run's writes go through `run_one` as any statement's do and wake the bindings they reach, run with their own authority in the run's block; no `wake` line is asked, the server's token waking every binding. A run examines at most `--call-max-rows` rows, a fresh budget a run that its bindings' runs spend; `MAX_WOKEN` holds its woken rows. `--call-rate` holds a subject's calls and a run has no subject: its cadence is held by the minute's floor, sixteen statements and one run a slot.

**Audited as a definition; a run is in the file.** `create statement ... every` or `... at`, its replacement and its drop are schema changes logged by their shapes (`audit::statement`), the line naming the schedule. A run is no audit event, as a write is none: its mark is its record -- the slot, the definition's version, what came of it -- in the file, replicated, archived and restored with what it wrote, where a log line would be beside it. A refused run is a log line ([below](#watching-schedules)).

### When a run refuses

**A refusal the statement makes marks its slot; one the state of its node or its data makes leaves it due.** What tells them apart is whether the same text over the same rows would refuse again. A guard unmet now is unmet in a minute, and a broken statement stays broken until someone mends it. A tenant at its quota is not: a client's delete, a compaction (`quota_policy`) or a raised quota makes the room, and the 507 is gone -- while `grows` counts every `set` as growth, so a tenant at its quota refuses every run that sets anything. Marking that slot would lose `monthly_reset` for a month to an hour at the quota. A lapsed lease makes the slot another node's.

| Whose refusal | What stops the run | Its slot | Said |
|---|---|---|---|
| the statement's | a guard's `require` unmet (412), a broken statement (409, not run), a `@unique` value taken (409), `--call-max-rows` or `MAX_WOKEN` (400), arithmetic past 64 bits, a binding's refusal, a hook's -- a mirrored collection's guard, an `Error::ReadOnly` not the fence's | marked refused, its status and the guard's `reason` in the mark, a run that lands nothing ([above](#where-a-run-happens-and-once)); the next slot runs as due | a log line naming the statement, the slot, the status and the engine's words; `outcome="refused"`, or `"broken"`; `last` in the listing |
| the statement's | a panic in the run | caught (`catch_unwind`), the block put back, the slot marked refused (500), so the next look does not meet it again | the log line, with the panic's words |
| the state's, tried | the tenant's quota (507), the fence's refusal noted mid-run (`Database::fenced`), a storage error (`Error::Io`) | not marked, the block put back: due, and held in memory (`schedule::Held`) -- tried again at the first look after the change counter or the limit (`Database::set_limit`) moved, at most once a minute, and otherwise after a backoff doubling from a minute to `schedule::RETRY_MAX`, 15 minutes, since a compaction making room moves neither. The next slot supersedes it, counted in `skipped` | a log line as it is first held and as the next slot supersedes it, never each try; `outcome="held"`; `held` in the listing |
| the state's, not tried | a following database, a lapsed lease, a frozen tenant, a storage failure (`Database::failure`), `--http-read-only` | not run and not marked: due to whoever writes the file next | nothing a slot: a standby has every slot due and is not the one to run them |

The engine tells the fence's refusal from a hook's, though both are `Error::ReadOnly`: the fence is asked before a run begins and its refusal mid-run noted as the fence's (`Database::fenced`), so a lease lapsing during a run leaves the slot due where a mirrored collection's guard marks it. A storage error is tried once: `Database::fail` then stops the database's writes, and the scheduler passes it over until it is opened again. A held slot keeps nothing on disk: a restart, a failover or a move finds it due in the file and tries it at its first look, the backoff begun again. A refusal's mark is a run that lands nothing, kept in memory until the next write, so a guard refused every minute costs no fsync a minute. A broken statement's slots are marked as they pass; once it reads again -- redefined, which moves `since` and clears the mark, or made to read again by a schema change -- the next slot runs, never one it missed while broken, which `broken` in the listing and the metric said meanwhile. Retrying the statement's own refusals each look would hold the write lock every look for a run that fails the same way until its data changes; marking the state's would lose a slot to a minute at the quota ([decision 5](#f3s-decisions-for-the-owner)).

### A schedule's record, replicas and upgrades

**Kind 13, op 4: a scheduled definition; op 5: a run's mark. A binary from before refuses both.** Op 4 is `[13][0][length][4][history][change][epoch][digest][name][depends][text][schedule][since]`: op 1's fields and the text, then the schedule outside the text -- `every`'s unit and count, or cron's five fields as bitmasks of 60, 24, 31, 12 and 7 bits, then the zone's name, empty for UTC -- and `since`, so a build that cannot parse the text lists the schedule and computes its slots. After the text, the record's tail, so a build that runs none keeps it as the bytes it is and reads none of it (before the text, a schedule cost the module a walk over it to find the text, 0.2 KB of its code). Op 5 is `[13][0][length][5][name][slot][history][change][outcome][status][skipped][reason]`: the slot, the version of the definition that ran, `ran` or `refused` with the status and the guard's reason, and the slots passed over. F2's reader refuses an op it does not read as corrupt (`engine/stored.rs`), so an F2 binary -- a replica, an archiver, a node a tenant moves to, a standby a failover would promote -- stops at the first schedule in words, never reading op 4 as a callable statement whose schedule nobody runs ([storage.md](storage.md#file-format)). Rejected:

- *The mark as a row of a `_` collection* (`_runs`, as `_idempotency` keeps keys): a document every surface would have to hide -- the listings, `/_changes`, a shape, the studio, a `*` rule -- where kind 13 is passed over by each already, and one an older binary reads without a word.
- *The mark as a field of op 4*, written again each run: each run would write the whole text again and move the definition's version, which names the text.

**`writes_in` counts each as one write**, so a replica's counter stays its primary's and a mark is sent as any write is: a record moving no counter is never sent ([replication.md](replication.md#replication)), and a promoted copy would run again every slot its old primary ran. An image writes op 4 and each statement's last op 5, a pending one too; a compact keeps the last mark alone; a replacement's op 4 clears its name's mark; `Undo::Mark` puts back a block's mark, and the one a replacement or a drop took, beside `Undo::Statements` for the definitions; a mark passes `Database::note` as every write does, so a compact beside the database keeps it ([storage.md](storage.md#maintenance-beside-the-database)). A replica applies both into its table and runs nothing; `/_changes` passes over both and tells the run's writes in its block's change range; an archive holds them, and a restore to a point in time runs again, once, the slot the history after that point held. A drop (op 2) takes the definition and its mark; a statement defined again under the name starts from its own `since`.

**Its dependencies and its contract are F1's.** `depends` names what the body reads and writes: a schema change breaking it is refused at its commit, naming it, and an internal writer marks it broken. A replacement keeps the kind -- a callable or bound statement replaced by a scheduled one, or the other way, is refused naming the `drop` and `create` it needs, since a `call` line naming it would come to grant something else -- and changes its schedule, zone and body freely, no grant hanging on them. The canonical tree writes the schedule only where it holds one, so no stored digest moves, `GRAMMAR_EPOCH` stays at 2, and `make fennecql-corpus` holds every FennecQL text of the repository to main's tree.

**Upgrades go one way, every reader before the first schedule**: F1's order ([upgrades](#replicas-sync-the-router-and-upgrades)) -- archivers, routers, every node, and then a primary may define one. `make upgrade-check` gains a base, the last main before op 4, whose replica stops at op 4 in words and connects again, whose archiver keeps the stream's bytes and takes no image of its own, whose `restore` and server refuse the archive and the file, whose router refuses a schema declaring a schedule (400, nothing placed), and onto whose node a move fails at the install, the tenant left on its source. RELEASING.md's notes name `every` and `at` as the clauses that move the format. The router knows nothing else of a schedule: it reads a tenant's schema with the grammar as it creates one (`admin::schema_description`), and forwards and grants no run; a query across tenants reads, and runs none.

### Schedules beside bindings, the sweep and held writes

**A run is a write: it wakes bindings, and held writes wake on it.** A schedule is fennecdb's form for acting on time, which F2 left `after expire` to ([F2's decision 5](#f2s-decisions-for-the-owner)): `lapse_holds` sets a hold `lapsed`, and the binding over that `set` gives the stock back in the same block -- a write a binding sees, where `@ttl`'s sweep deletes a row no binding sees, at a time no write chose. The scheduler is no internal writer in F2's sense: it runs a definer's text as a call does, never under `Database::unbound`. A held write wakes on a run's writes as on any landing -- `Waits::wrote` is handed the collections the run's block noted -- so a worker holding `claim` on `jobs` is answered when a schedule puts a stale job back in the queue ([waits.md](waits.md#waking-held-writes)). A run is never held: it has no request. A slot finding nothing lands nothing ([above](#where-a-run-happens-and-once)) and wakes no held write: the scheduler tells the watcher `Waits::wrote(Some(&[]))`, a landing naming no collection, never `None`, which would wake every group and move `epoch` for a run that wrote nothing.

**`@ttl` and a schedule do different work, and neither waits for the other.** The sweep gives space back, every read having left its rows out at their time; a schedule writes what the data should say once a time comes. A reaper `every 1m` reading `expired()` -- which a run, scoped by nothing, may -- sees a row past its time before the sweep deletes it only when it runs on time: the sweep waits `sweep::GRACE` (a minute) past the time, and a run later than that -- behind other tenants' runs, after a restart, held at the quota, broken -- may find the row gone. The sweep is not held back for a schedule: one broken or held would keep the space of every expired row for as long, and a sweep waiting on a schedule's mark is a coupling no read needs. A body that must act on every expiry keeps the time in a field of its own, as `lapse_holds` keeps `until`, and deletes the row itself once it acted, never leaving it to `@ttl`.

### Schedules beside sync replicas, the module and the native library

**A sync replica defines none and runs none; what a run wrote reaches it through its shapes.** `NAMED` refuses `create statement ... every` on a synced database as it refuses every definition, and a run's rows come through the shapes following their collections as any server write's do. `integrations/sync-scenarios.json` gains the refusal before either runner changes ([native-and-sync.md](native-and-sync.md#sync-scenarios)); the rows' arrival is a server's write and needs no scenario.

**The module and `openFile` keep the records and run none.** The records are core, as op 1's and op 3's are: every build reads op 4 and op 5, keeps them through its images and lists the statement. The module keeps them as the bytes they are: a schedule as its record's tail, unread, and a mark as its record's body on its statement, read only for its name and version. A schedule binds no write, so where F2 refuses a bound write in the module, F3 refuses nothing: a page writing a server's file (`openFile`) loses no schedule, and the slots that pass while a page or the shell holds the file run once, at the latest, when a server next opens it. Measured in its slice (`make size-report BASE=origin/main`, all three builds), against the 824 bytes brotli op 1's record cost.

**The native library runs none in F3.** An app's process is suspended and killed by its operating system, and a thread of the library's own would run where the app neither sees nor schedules it, on a phone's battery. The due slots are a pure function of the table, the marks and a time (`Database::due`), so an app wanting them run would call an entry such as `fennec_run_due` from its own timer -- WorkManager, `BGTaskScheduler` -- in a follow-up of its own: a C ABI entry, a JNI one and `fennec.h`'s line ([decision 8](#f3s-decisions-for-the-owner)).
