The timing script's +1.5% on a tenant's write through `handle` and +1.2% on its read (seven pairs against main) are not in what the loop executes: in process, the loop alone counted (`proc_pid_rusage`'s instructions and cycles, nine runs a side in turns), main's write is 18 731 instructions and 3 927 cycles an op, the slice's first 18 569 and 3 877, this one 18 650 and 3 896; the read 14 146 and 2 680, 14 227 and 2 699, 14 065 and 2 670 -- each run's count a step of 81 instructions from the next, its hash tables' seeds, and the three sides' spreads the same. Built with every function aligned to 64 bytes on both sides, two pairs of the timing program read the slice +0.8 and +2.1% on the write at loads of 1.4 to 1.5, as unaligned: the wall-clock of a loop under others' load, the cycles it took the same.

**Slice 5, the surfaces.** *What it lets a user do:* see each scheduled statement's schedule, next slot, last run and a slot held back where statements are listed; count its runs by what came of them, read how late they began, find a slow one in the log and follow one as a trace; and be told by Datadog when one refuses, is held, finds a mark ahead of the clock or runs late. Nothing a run does changes; each surface is the server's token's or the admin's, as every other statement's is, and none is an authority or a refusal.

*The listings.* `statements` and `describe statement` gain four columns after `after`, `null` for a statement no clock runs: `schedule`, the clause as its text writes it up to its last word or string -- a comment after it is the text's -- read off the stored text by `named::schedule_text` -- `named::condition_text`'s walk over the words outside strings, comments and brackets, now one iterator the two share (`TopWords`) -- so a statement this process holds no tree for shows it too, and the audit's `schema` line reads it the same way (`audit::schedule_of` called the lexer for it; the two now cannot name a schedule apart); `next`, the slot due now where one is -- in the past, overdue, wherever the scheduler holds it back or nothing runs it yet: a risk this section named, "`next` ... show[s] a slot overdue" -- else the first after the clock; `last`, the last mark, pending or landed, `{outcome, reason, skipped, slot, status}`; and `held`, `{since, slot, status}`. `Database::schedule_shown` computes the four, reading the clock only where the table holds a schedule (`clock_for_schedules`), so a listing of statements no clock runs reads none. `held` is the scheduler's memory, not the engine's: the scheduler tells the database what it holds back (`Database::set_held_slots`, a word of the database's, `None` while nothing is held) at the end of each look that changed it (`State::changed`, set wherever its held slots change), under the write lock taken for that alone, and a node hands a tenant's back as it opens (`Node::opened`, from the entry that keeps it across a close: a look hands its state back to the entry before it closes the tenant, and a tenant opened while a look holds the state -- by the look, or a request before it -- is told as the look ends, `Entry::reopened`); the listing shows one only while its slot is the one due, so a slot run, marked or passed over since shows none, whatever was told last. A scoped token is refused both as before (403, 404).

*`GET /_schema`*, to the server's token, writes the scheduled statements after the collections, `"schedules": [{"name", "schedule", "text", "next", "last", "held"}]`, present only where the table holds one, so every other database's description is the bytes it was; `?as=fennecql` writes each definition after the bound ones. Either goes back through `/_schema/plan` as a plan of nothing: natively `declared::parse` takes the member (`Declared::scheduled`) and `fennec_ql::bound_into` reads each text as the scheduled definition of its name, a `schedule` listed other than its text's refused naming both, as a binding's events are; `next`, `last` and `held` are the server's to tell, and no plan reads them. A bound or scheduled statement the schema broke is listed with why (`"broken"`, after its text), which the plan reads back holding it to no dependency (`Declared::broken`), and `?as=fennecql` writes it as comments under a `-- broken:` line: the server's own description, in either form, plans nothing. The browser module, which reads a description as `fennecql` alone, takes no `schedules` member, so its parse is the code it was.

*The slow line.* A run whose time under the write lock is past `--slow-ms` -- never its lateness -- writes the slow line on the scheduler's thread, its `statement` the shape `schedule <name>`, `scheduled` `["tick",[0,4],"2026-11-03T03:00:00Z",1]` -- the statement, the version that ran, the slot and the rows its last statement answered, as a call's are counted -- `bound` beside it where its writes woke bindings, `failed` where refused or held, and the run's own `request_id` (`schedule::run_id`); it counts in `fennec_slow_statements_total`. A run is no request: it counts in no other statement family.

*Traces.* Each run is a trace of its own (`trace::begin_run`, `end_run`, a request's `start` and `finish` shared), sampled at `--trace-sample` as a request with no `traceparent` is: a root span `schedule <name>` of kind internal, `db.operation.name` `schedule`, `db.stored_procedure.name`, `db.query.text` its shape, `fennec.slot`, `fennec.lateness_ms`, `fennec.slots_skipped`, `fennec.request_id`, `fennec.tenant` on a node, and `fennec.outcome`, which the design did not list -- the metrics' outcome, so a trace and a count are read alike. Beneath it the lock's wait, the run (`execute`), its sync (`durability`, where it landed) and the bindings its writes woke (`bound <name>`, as a request's). A refused or held run's span is failed with its status and the reason its `else` wrote, `error.type` the status -- one whose outcome is unknown with a 500, as a client's write is answered --, never the engine's words: a unique value its body met is named in them (`a_scheduled_run_is_a_trace_of_its_own` holds one out of every span). A run that ran no slot -- one asked again under the lock and found run -- is discarded, no trace. It never waits on the collector, as a request does not.

*`/_stats/statements`* counts each run as the shape `schedule <name>`: calls the runs, each try of a held slot among them; rows its last statement's; time under the write lock; an error where refused, broken, held or of an outcome unknown. It is keyed where no client's text is (`statements::own_key`, the shape's hash taken on over `0xfe`, which no UTF-8 holds), as `after <name>` is: keyed by its shape, a client's `schedule purge`, a parse error, added to the runs' errors. A tenant node's run is its tenant's shape, read under `/t/<tenant>/_stats/statements` and, named, by the admin across tenants; the bindings a run woke are counted as `after <name>` as any write's are (`statements::bound`, `metrics::bound`, `trace::bound_runs`, taken off the scheduler's thread after each run: slice 3 left the engine's note there to grow to `RUNS_KEPT`).

*`/_metrics`*: `fennec_scheduled_runs_total{outcome}` -- `ran`, `refused`, `broken`, `held`, `unknown` (landed as its lease lapsed, or its fsync failed) --, `fennec_scheduled_skipped_total`, `fennec_scheduled_mark_ahead_total` and `fennec_scheduled_lateness_seconds`, a histogram of a run's start past its slot, from a tenth of a second to a day. No label names a tenant, a statement or a collection. Three things differ from the plan. Each run is counted once, by what came of it: slice 3 counted a run landing as its lease lapsed both `ran` and `unknown`, so the outcomes summed past the runs. The lateness counts the runs that did not hold -- a held slot's tries, a minute doubling to fifteen apart, would fill the top buckets with one slot; its run once room comes is counted, as late as it was. And the families are written once the process made a look (`LOOKED`): a server that never held a schedule scrapes the bytes it did, as `a_server_holding_no_schedule_makes_no_look` has it wake for nothing. The scheduler is one thread a process, so its counters are one set of atomics, not a shard a thread. `monitoring/datadog/conf.yaml` keeps the four, `dashboard.json` gains a group charting them, and `monitors.json` -- new -- five monitors: a run refused or broken in the last hour, a run whose outcome is unknown in one, more than three held tries in one (warning at one), a mark ahead of the clock, and the p99 of lateness over two minutes (warning at one). `tests/datadog.rs` holds each monitor's metric and every tag it matches to a real scrape, and requires the five, beside the dashboard's names.

*Tests*, each failing with its part taken out. fennec-http's `the_listings_show_a_schedule_its_next_slot_its_last_run_and_a_held_slot` (the four columns in the engine and over HTTP, `describe statement`, `/_schema` in both forms read back by the plan, a listed schedule other than the text's refused, a scoped token shown none, a held slot shown and gone once it runs); `scheduled_counts.rs`, a binary of its own, `a_run_is_counted_by_outcome_and_shaped_by_name_a_tenant_s_apart` (no family before a look; each outcome once, the slots passed over, a mark ahead and the lateness histogram, on a file's server and a tenant node alike, no label but `outcome` and `le` and no name of a tenant, a statement or a collection in either scrape; the shapes on the file's server, a tenant's under its prefix and the admin's across tenants); `what_the_schema_broke_and_a_commented_schedule_read_back_as_a_plan_of_nothing` (a broken scheduled statement and binding listed `broken` and read back in both forms, a schedule's comment left out of `schedule`); `node_scheduled.rs`' `a_held_slot_is_listed_once_a_request_opens_its_tenant_again` (the quota's hold shown after the look closed the tenant and a request opened it); `scheduled_counts.rs` holding too a run whose fsync failed counted `unknown`, the unknown runs' spans failed 500, and a client's `schedule tock` a shape apart; fennec-core's `a_schedule_is_read_from_its_text_as_written`, and `run_slot`'s rows; fennec-server's `a_scheduled_run_is_counted_and_its_slow_line_names_it` and `a_scheduled_run_is_a_trace_of_its_own`, against the binary; fennec-shard's `the_datadog_monitors_watch_what_the_servers_send`.

*Size and time.* The browser module does not move (`make size-report BASE=origin/main`: 818.2 KB raw to the byte, gzip +5 bytes and brotli -64, noise; `fennec-lite` and the build with no feature the same raw): what fennec-core gains is behind `statements` or native alone (`Declared::scheduled`), which the module leaves out. `fennec-server`'s code grows 21.2 KB, 4 325.6 KB to 4 346.8 (+0.5%, `size_report.py --bin fennec-server` on each tree): fennec-http 10.2 KB -- the run's trace, the four families and the counts beside them (`watched`), the slow line's, `/_schema`'s writer, out of line and cold --, fennec-core 5.6 KB (`schedule_shown`, `schedule_text`'s walk, the description's member), the standard library's generics 2.6 KB, fennec-ql 1.8 KB (a description's scheduled texts read back) and fennec-abi 1.0 KB (the listing's columns). A server holding no schedule runs none of it but a listing's four `null`s, a branch for `/_schema` and one load a scrape. Its time -- a `/_metrics` scrape over loopback, a `/query` page of 20, a `statements` listing and a `set` by id through `handle`, main and the branch in turns, seven pairs, by instructions retired and by the clock -- read the scrape +0.1%, the page +0.16%, the write +0.28% and the listing +26.7% (50 117 instructions an op to 63 483).

*After the review.* The listing of six statements none of which a clock runs was 13 400 instructions over main: each row's values were laid out, then extended by the four, a reallocation whose free the allocator timed (a fifth of the listing's samples), and the column names were a list made each call before their strings. The four are now laid in with the rest (one allocation a row, as on main), the names come from a static list, and `schedule_shown` returns the four `null`s before its body, which is cold and out of line: 57 573 instructions an op (57 551 to 57 721, five runs a side in turns, main's 50 111, 49 911 to 50 121), +14.9%. What remains is the answer's own: main built with the same four `null` columns and nothing else of the slice counts 57 897 to 58 282 -- the four names' strings and 24 more `"key":null` pairs written and dropped, about 330 instructions a pair. Making the four one column, or leaving them out where no schedule is held, would change what the listing answers, which this section decided. The write by id adds no instruction: its count falls on steps of about 81 instructions, a hash table's probes under the process's seeds and layout, and within each step the branch counts what main counts with nothing but the `held_slots` word added to `Database` -- 6 fewer than main (25 480, 25 560, 25 641, 25 722, 25 804 against 25 486, 25 566, 25 648, 25 729). Which step a process lands on moves with the build alone: over 35 runs a side main's mean step was 2.37, the branch's 3.03, and main with that word alone 2.48 over 25, two modes -- the +0.2% the script read; fifteen more a side of the final build read the branch's median 25 565 and main's 25 592. The page is main's (51 991 and 51 995, the medians of seven); the scrape, mostly the kernel's, varies 1% within a side, and the fixes moved it none (the branch before them 305.2 K, after 304.2 K, main 302.6 K in the same turns). `fennec-server`'s code is 1.4 KB smaller than the slice's first, 4 346.8 KB to 4 345.3 (`size_report.py --bin fennec-server` on both, by function): the trace's shared `start` and `finish` -1.4 KB (with `end` as `serve_connection` inlines it), `/_schema`'s schedules written straight from the table -1.2, `look_at` telling `held` by a flag rather than a copy compared each look -0.6, the listing's static columns and its row -0.6; against what the fixes added -- a broken statement listed and written as comments +1.4 (the standard library's line splitting and `replace` 2 KB more until a loop of its own), the description's `broken` read back +0.7, a node's hand-back +0.1. The four families' help texts are a sentence each, 0.7 KB of strings out of the binary. An `obj` helper for the listing's two objects measured 0.1 to 0.6 KB more than the objects written out, in three forms, and was not kept.

**Slice 6, the studio, the SDKs and the shell -- F3 complete.** *What it lets a user do:* see each scheduled statement's schedule, next slot, last run and held slot in fennec studio and run one now from it; read the same, typed, from every SDK; find the form in the shell's `.help`. No Rust changed but the shell's help text: the listing is slice 5's and the call of a scheduled statement slice 3's, and this slice reads one and sends the other.

*The studio.* The named statements' list (`statementsPart`, which reads `statements` already) marks a scheduled statement `scheduled` and shows its schedule as its text writes it where a callable one shows its signature -- a scheduled one's is always `()` -- and under it the clock in words (`clockText`, in `schema.js`, which alone reads it: a text, not a statement, so none of `statements*.js`): `next 2027-01-01T02:00:00Z; not run yet` -- `no next slot` where this server's build lacks the zone, whose `next` is `null` -- `ran for <slot>`, `refused (412) for <slot>: <reason>`, `, 2 before it passed over`, `; <slot> held (507) since <time>`; why the schema broke it in the comment's column, as for any statement. *Run now* is the call form in a mode of its own (`callForm(n, signature, run)`): no input, since the statement declares none, the statement it sends -- `call lapse_holds`, its name alone (`runNow`), held as every statement of the studio is to the name FennecQL writes -- and the hint that no slot is marked; it runs in the query editor under an `Idempotency-Key`, its answer in the grid, disabled for a broken statement as a call is. The definition's dialog says when it runs, `Runs at '0 3 1 1 *' in 'Europe/Berlin' by the server's clock, a slot once`, and opens it as `create or replace statement ...`. Rejected: a table of its own, *Scheduled statements*, with a column each for the schedule, the next slot, the last run and the held slot -- the first draft, the schema view 1 154 bytes gzipped larger where the row costs 383; the listing the view reads carries the four columns, and `/_schema`'s `schedules` would be a request more that the view with no collection open does not make. The highlighter's words gain `every` and `slot`, and `at` where a text follows it on its line, in either quotes as the parser takes the cron (`(?P<kw>\bat(?=[ \t]+['"]))`, `token_pattern`'s FennecQL alone): `at` is a field in 98 lines of the docs' FennecQL blocks (`bucket(at, 1h)`), which a word list would colour as a keyword; `in` was one already. `site/test_highlight.py` holds the two highlighters to the same HTML over a schedule's samples, `at` both ways and across a line, and `at` to a keyword before a cron text in either quotes and a field in `bucket(at, 1h)`, so taking the rule out fails it; `make studio-highlight` writes the studio's and the Grafana data source's copies. `e2e.test.mjs`'s `the schema: a scheduled statement with its clock, run now, opened as its replacement` drives the row, the dialog, the run in the editor -- its `slot()` the latest slot before it, and the server's listing after it still `last: null` -- and the replacement; `statements.test.mjs` holds `runNow` and `clockText`.

*The SDKs.* None typed the `statements` listing -- the design's "where an SDK types them" found no SDK that did -- so each gained it, as each gained F2's `binding` where it types an error: a method sending the text `statements` as any text is sent, and its rows read into a `NamedStatement` of every column `statements` answers (`name`, `version`, `signature`, `writes`, `comment`, `broken`, `after`, `schedule`, `next`, `last`, `held`), `last` a `LastRun` (`slot`, `outcome`, `status`, `reason`, `skipped`) and `held` a `HeldSlot` (`slot`, `status`, `since`), the times in the language's own: JavaScript's `FennecHttp.statements()` (declared in `fennec.d.ts`, `client.d.ts` re-exporting the types; the server's ISO text, a `Timestamp`, as a row's times are; the page's module has none, holding no statement it runs), Python's `Client.statements()` and `AsyncClient.statements()` (`NamedTuple`s, an aware UTC `datetime` -- `fromisoformat` takes a `Z` from 3.11 alone, the SDK from 3.10), Go's `Client.Statements(ctx)` (`QueryAs[NamedStatement]`, `*time.Time`), .NET's `StatementsAsync` (records, `DateTimeOffset`), Swift's `statements()` on `Fennec` and `FennecRemote` (`Date`, through `ISO8601DateFormatter`, fractional seconds only where the text has them), Kotlin's `statements()` and `statementsBlocking()` on both (milliseconds since the epoch, read by hand as `Json.iso` writes them, since `java.time` is on Android from API 26 only), Dart's `statements()` on both (a UTC `DateTime`; the reader, `namedStatement`, left out of the package's exports). `version` is the history and the change, as the server writes them signed (`Value::Int(s.version.0 as i64)`): a history is 0 until a database first forks or has a replica, then a fresh 64-bit id, negative where its top bit is set -- half of such ids -- so each SDK reads them signed (Go `[2]int64`, .NET `long`, Kotlin `Long`, Swift `Int64`, Python's and the Dart VM's `int`) and every scripted or read row holds `-3244637736030576075`: the review found Go's first `[2]uint64` refusing such a listing whole. JavaScript's numbers, and Dart's compiled for the web, round a history past 2^53; it is an identity, so there the declaration and the docs say to compare the change, not the history, rather than a type promising exactness. Kotlin's reader refuses a time it cannot read as `Code.QUERY`, the answer the binding cannot read, never `CORRUPT`, a damaged file. A scoped token is refused the listing (403), as the server refuses it; the native library lists what its file holds and runs none of its schedules. *Tests*, against a real server in each: a statement scheduled at Berlin's 03:00 on 1 January -- an hourly one could pass its slot while a test looks, and a test that passes on a rerun is a bug -- listed with its schedule, its next slot to the second and no run, a callable one with no clock, then called by the server's token and still listed with no run (JavaScript's `fennec.sync.test.js`, Python's `test_call.py`, Go's `TestTheStatementsAServerHoldsAreListedAScheduledOneWithItsClock`, .NET's `CallTests`, Swift's `RemoteCall`, Kotlin's `CallRemoteTest`, Dart's `sync_test.dart`), Go and .NET with a scoped token's 403; a refused run with its reason and skipped slots, a held slot to the millisecond and a binding's `after` read from a server's answer -- Python's, Go's and .NET's from a scripted one, Swift's, Kotlin's and Dart's through their readers; and the embedded database's listing in Swift, Kotlin and Dart.

*The shell.* `.help` gives the scheduled form beside the called and the bound ones -- `every 1h | at '0 3 * * *' [in 'Europe/Berlin'] ... -- scheduled: fennec-server's clock runs it, a slot once, slot() the slot's time; defined on a server, never here` -- since the shell refuses the definition (`SCHEDULED_UNRUN`); `the_help_gives_the_scheduled_form` holds it.

*Size and time.* Nothing on a server's path changed: no Rust but the shell's help text, and the studio's files are served as the repository holds them. The schema view grows 383 bytes gzipped with what the views share, 23 819 to 24 202 (`schema.js` +337, `statements-views.js` +19, `highlight.js` +27), past the 24 000 a view was held to since the studio's second part: `statements.test.mjs` holds the schema view under 25 000 and the editor, the live rows and the admin view under 24 000 still (22 422, 18 310 and 19 868), so the room goes to the view that took it. The other views grow by the shared modules' 46 bytes, the first load by 57 (72 860 to 72 917, `http.js`'s `statements`, its comment cut to a line: the declarations carry the rest, and the eight lines first written cost the first load 195). The first draft grew the schema view 549 and every view by the shared 279: `clockText` sat in `statements-views.js`, which the editor fetches too, and four comments said what the code beside them says (166 bytes); the review moved the one into `schema.js` and cut the others. `fennec-server` embeds the files as they are, 1 224 bytes more; the shell's help text 255 bytes more.

*Docs.* The FennecQL reference's scheduled section names the SDKs' listing, the studio's row and run now and `.help`; the languages page lists each SDK's method and types, the JavaScript, mobile, integrations and studio pages theirs, each SDK's README its own, and README's row and the home page say both.

### F3's risks

- *A slot run twice*, only where a run's writes and its mark parted: one block makes them one record, and the due check under the lock reads the mark the run lands after. A new path running a scheduled body owes the mark in its block, or is the server's token's call, which marks nothing. A pending mark lost runs its slot again over unchanged data, the lost run having shown nothing; a mark a wrong clock left more than `AHEAD_MAX` ahead runs its slots again as they come, counted and logged.
- *A slot run by no one*: a node that may write whose scheduler sleeps -- an idle tenant the hint missed, a scheduler thread gone, a mark ahead of the clock -- runs nothing without a word. The hint over-names, entered before a first schedule lands and by every way a file comes on; a file copied into `--dir` behind the node is the one it can miss. A scheduler that cannot start stops its server, a mark ahead pauses an hour at most, and `next` and `held` in the listing, the held runs and the lateness histogram show a slot overdue.
- *A slot lost to a passing state*: none -- a quota, a lease, a freeze or a storage error leaves the slot due, and only the next slot supersedes it, counted in `skipped`.
- *A run made twice to the eye*: a run lost to a buffered crash or a lagging standby's promotion, made again at a later `now()`, may have been acted on by a reader, a `claim` worker or a `/_changes` consumer, as any write landed and lost was. `--sync always`, or a body putting `slot()` in what it hands out, is the schedule's answer where its effects leave the database.
- *The write lock at the hour*: every tenant's `0 3 * * *`, one run at a time a process, each bounded by `--call-max-rows`, measured; a pool is the follow-up.
- *Lateness taken for a fault*: a run at 03:04 for 03:00 is none; `slot()` is how a body names the slot it ran for.
- *File growth* by marks at the floor, given back by compaction, measured; a run landing nothing adds none of its own.
- *A late reaper* may find an expired row the sweep took first; a body that must act on every expiry keeps its own time field and deletes the row itself.
- *An older node promoted* stops at op 4 rather than leaving schedules unrun: the upgrade's order keeps the cluster writing.
- *A zone's rules* are its binary's: a country changing its clock is an upgrade.
- *CLAUDE.md's room*: the rule goes into [crates/fennec-ql/CLAUDE.md](../../crates/fennec-ql/CLAUDE.md), an area file under 24 000 bytes with room for it, its title in the root's index; slice 4's node rule into [crates/fennec-shard/CLAUDE.md](../../crates/fennec-shard/CLAUDE.md), beside the registry's, at 8 545 bytes once its review moved its mechanics here.

### What CLAUDE.md would gain from F3

Slice 2 brought a short rule of its own after the binding rule in `crates/fennec-ql/CLAUDE.md`, as F2's slice 2 did, its title in the root's index: *A scheduled statement's record is kept by every build, and run by none yet* -- op 4 and op 5 counted by `writes_in` and refused by a binary from before; `Stored::schedule` and `Stored::last` kept by every image, each schedule's last mark alone, and by a compact beside the database, which writes the marks at its swap; a replica's apply and `/_changes`; a run's mark in its block, or pending in `Database::pending` until the next block lands; a replacement clearing the mark, moving `since` and keeping the kind; `grows` and a rate passing over op 5; `MAX_SCHEDULED`; `Database::due`; and every definition refused but a test's, no call running one. Slice 3 replaces it with:

> **[A scheduled statement runs once a slot, by the primary that may write its file.](docs/design/named-statements.md#scheduled-statements-f3)** `create statement <n> every <interval> | at '<cron>' [in '<zone>'] as ...` is kind 13's op 4 and a run's mark op 5, each counted as a write and refused by a binary from before. A run's writes and its mark are one block, its slot the next after the file's last mark asked again under the write lock (`Database::due`), so a crash, a failover or a move leaves a slot run once or not at all in the history that survives; missed slots run once, the latest (`skipped`); a run landing nothing marks in memory (`Database::pending`), its mark landing with the next write; a mark ahead of the clock is counted and logged and stops nothing past `AHEAD_MAX`, and a replacement clears it. Only a server's scheduler (`fennec_http::schedule`) runs one, after both listeners are bound, through a tenant's gate and the engine's fence: never a following database, `--http-read-only`, the shell, the native library or the module. A run is the server's token's: its writes wake bindings, a fresh `--call-max-rows`, the tenant's bytes and rows but not its rate; a refusal the statement makes marks its slot, one the state makes -- the quota, the fence, a freeze, `Error::Io` -- leaves it due, tried again as the counter or the limit moves, the next slot superseding it; no token but the server's calls one, its `slot()` the call's time truncated to a slot, and that call marks nothing. A database holding no schedule starts no scheduler.

Slice 3 first wrote it as above with what it built named, a dozen sentences; its review brought it to the rule's few -- what must hold (a run and its mark one block, its slot asked again under the write lock, a pending mark the latest by order), what must never be (a schedule held where nothing runs it: refused at a definition no scheduler watches and at a tenant node's install, said by a server running none), what enforces it (`Database::due`, `schedule::watch`, `SCHEDULED_UNRUN`, `schedule::unrun`, `stop_with`) and what a change owes (a new path running a body its mark in the block, a new way a file reaches a node the refusal or the word) -- the rest here: the marks beside the table and their images and compacts, the clock's rule and `AHEAD_MAX`, `now()` and `slot()`, the refusal classes and the server token's call. Slice 4 had it name a node's scheduler beside a file's (`schedule::Node`), the install's refusal kept only where a node runs none, and the word a new way a file reaches a node owes the scheduler's (`Node::note`, `Node::installed`, `Node::opened`); what the node does is a rule of its own, in [crates/fennec-shard/CLAUDE.md](../../crates/fennec-shard/CLAUDE.md) beside the registry's -- *A tenant node runs its tenants' schedules from one thread, by name, a run no use of a tenant* -- its review bringing it to five sentences: the queue by name and a run no use (`for_run`), the hint never missing a tenant (named, synced, before any scheduled definition or installed file is written, a look queued once the file is in place, left only with its last drop durable or as a frozen move's source leaves), one tenant's panic its own, and the hooks a new way a tenant opens, closes, comes on or leaves owes. The mechanics it first named are here, above: `UNUSED` and `make_room`'s run, the gate and one slot a look, `Stood` and `keep_pending`; slice 5 the surfaces it names, none an authority; slice 6 no word, its clients reading the listing and calling as the server's token does. The one shutdown's rule needs no word: the scheduler is what runs beside the database, and `stop_with` is its way down -- a file's server's for a panic outside a run's own catch, a node's for one outside any tenant's look, a tenant's look panicking holding that tenant back alone ([below](#f3s-decisions-for-the-owner)).

### F3's decisions for the owner

Taken after review, 5 and 6 changed from the first draft and the others as it recommended; each line ends with what the owner can still override.

1. **The syntax.** *Taken: a clause of `create statement`* -- `every <interval>` at `bucket`'s edges or `at '<cron>'`, `in '<zone>'` -- one record, one text, a schedule no token calls. *Still the owner's:* pg_cron's job naming a callable statement, one statement on a schedule and on demand at the price of a grant running the nightly job at will.
2. **Missed slots.** *Taken: the latest runs once*, the others counted (`skipped`). *Still the owner's:* every one, 1 440 runs under the write lock after a day down at a minute's cadence; or none until the next, pg_cron's and cron's, a monthly reset missed by a restart waiting a month.
3. **The finest period.** *Taken: a minute*, cron's step and the sweep's period; a reaper sees an expired row before the sweep only when on time, and a body acting on every expiry keeps its own time field ([above](#schedules-beside-bindings-the-sweep-and-held-writes)). *Still the owner's:* seconds (pg_cron's `[1-59] seconds`, Supabase's "every second"), a mark a second a statement and the write lock a second a tenant, where `Fennec-Wait` is fennecdb's form for acting within a second.
4. **The time a run reads.** *Taken: `now()` the run's start, `slot()` its slot's instant.* *Still the owner's:* `now()` the slot's, which reads one clock for `now()` and another for `alive`, or rows every client is told are gone.
5. **A refused run** (changed). *Taken: a refusal the statement makes marks its slot* -- `require` unmet, a broken statement, a `@unique` clash, the row bounds, a binding's or a hook's refusal, a panic -- *and one the state makes leaves it due* -- the quota's 507, a lapsed lease, a freeze, `Error::Io` -- tried again as the counter or the limit moves or the backoff ends, the next slot superseding it. *Still the owner's:* marking every refusal, the first draft's, which loses a monthly reset for a month to an hour at the quota; or retrying the statement's own too, the write lock held every look for a run that fails the same way.
6. **Running one by hand** (changed). *Taken: the server's token's `call`, marking no slot, its `slot()` the call's time truncated to the schedule's slot*; a scoped token never. *Still the owner's:* refusing a call of a body reading `slot()`, which leaves the bodies that label their work untried by hand; or no call at all, as for F2's bindings, an operator waiting for the slot or redefining it to try it.
7. **An idle tenant's slots.** *Taken: the node's hint, naming scheduled tenants alone, opens one when its slot comes*, a run no use of it and the tenant closed after. *Still the owner's:* keeping a scheduled tenant open, which takes `--idle-close` and `--max-memory` from it; or running its slots as it next opens, which makes an idle month one run.
8. **The native library.** *Taken: it runs none in F3*, `Database::due` the core of a follow-up an app drives from its own timer (`fennec_run_due`). *Still the owner's:* a thread in the library, running where the app's operating system suspends it and the app cannot see it.
9. **A tenant's panic on a node** (taken at slice 4's review). *Taken: one tenant's look panicking holds that tenant back* -- the backoff a held slot waits, a minute doubling to `RETRY_MAX` -- logged with its name and counted (`Node::panics`), *the node going on with every other*; a panic outside any tenant's look -- the loop, the hint's write, the learning's end -- stops the node through the one shutdown, and a file's server keeps slice 3's rule, its one database's look stopping it. The one shutdown exits the process: on a node a panic in one tenant's look -- its file, its statements, its open -- took every tenant's requests and schedules down with it, and a supervisor restarted the node into the same slot and the same panic, an outage of every tenant for one tenant's file. A look's state is the tenant's own (its entry's, taken by the look), so what a panic leaves half done is that tenant's: its held slots and marks told forgotten, tried again after the backoff, its database put back by the engine where the run's own catch did not reach. *Still the owner's:* stopping the node as a file's server stops, every tenant's schedule waiting on one tenant's fix.

## Webhook consumers (F4)

**Status: designed, revised after its review; slices 1 and 2 built ([below](#what-f4-has-built)) -- the parser and the tree, and the record, the keys and the taking, which tests drive: every definition refused where statements run but a test's, and no server takes an event.** A named statement taking the events another service sends -- Stripe's payments, GitHub's pushes and issues, the events of every sender that signs as Standard Webhooks does -- at `POST /_hooks/<name>`, verified by the signature its sender makes before anything of it is read, its values bound to the statement's typed parameters from the event's body, and taken once by the event's key: the statements' writes and the key's row land as one block, so a sender's retries, a duplicate delivery and a failover between a write and its answer take an event once in the history that survives ([below](#where-an-event-is-taken-and-once)). This section decides what [F1's paragraph](#what-it-does-not-do) left open and keeps what it promised -- the inbound endpoint, a signature as Stripe and GitHub sign theirs, a statement's parameters bound from the body, the event's id keying it, the secret never the database's, an authority of the statement's own -- but two of its words: no `public`, which a statement no token calls has no use for, as F3 found for its schedules, and the secret not the policy's but a hooks file's, since a server taking webhooks may verify no token at all. It changes no rule of CLAUDE.md now; the rules come with the slices that build it ([below](#what-claudemd-would-gain-from-f4)).

Its review found the model sound and four things wrong, each adopted below with its reason: a duplicate answered 200 before the first try's block was durable or sent, a gap `Idempotency-Key` has today ([B1](#a-replay-waits-for-what-its-first-try-waited-for)); a tenant's `<t>.hooks` able to outlive it and arm a later tenant of the same name ([B2](#where-the-secret-lives)); the checks before the signature telling which hooks exist and their schemes, so the "one 401 after the same work" could not hold as ordered ([B3](#verifying-an-event)); and a `github` event keyed by its body, which takes two deliveries of one body as one event ([B4](#an-events-key)). Its smaller findings -- headers sent twice, `keep` against the tolerance, the route that exports a secret, a `PUT` answered before the standbys hold it, `event.` in a body, the broken statement's reach, Stripe's own events, `audit::verified`, the 411's citation, the cost of an fsync an event -- are taken where each belongs, and [the owner's decisions](#f4s-decisions-for-the-owner) gain five.

```fennecql
create statement stripe_paid(
    $intent text from event.data.object.id,
    $order text from event.data.object.metadata.order,
    $amount int from event.data.object.amount_received,
    $currency text from event.data.object.currency)
  on hook stripe
  where event.type = 'payment_intent.succeeded' and event.livemode = true
  comment 'Marks the order an intent paid, for its whole total alone'
as set orders {status: 'paid', intent: $intent}
   where ref = $order and total = $amount and currency = $currency
     and (status = 'open' or intent = $intent)
   require 1 else 'no open order of that total'
```

```text
# hook       scheme    secret files, then options
stripe       stripe    /run/credentials/fennec-server/stripe.whsec                            tolerance 5m
stripe_test  stripe    /run/credentials/fennec-server/stripe-test.whsec
github       github    /run/credentials/fennec-server/github.secret                           key body
clerk        standard  /run/credentials/fennec-server/clerk.whsec /run/credentials/fennec-server/clerk.next.whsec
```

Stripe is told `https://db.example.com/_hooks/stripe`; a payment lands as the order's `set`, and with F2's `paid_ledger` bound to that `set` the ledger line and the debit land in the same block -- a payment the bank confirmed becomes a ledger entry with no server of the app's own between them. The guard holds what the event says to what the data says: `metadata.order` is the app's, set where the intent was made, and a statement trusting it alone would mark an order of 100 paid by an intent of 1 naming it; `total = $amount` and `currency = $currency` are the check every such handler owes, written where the write is. `intent = $intent` makes it idempotent by the intent's id as well as by the event's: Stripe may send two Event objects, two `id`s, for one change ([below](#stripes-events-as-stripe-sends-them)), and the second meets its own row, met by `require 1`, writing what is there. `event.livemode = true` keeps a test event out of production should a test secret ever be set on the live hook; the test mode's endpoint is a hook of its own, `stripe_test`, signed by its own secret.

### The form of a hook

**A clause of `create statement`, as `after` and `every` are.** `create [or replace] statement <name> [(<parameter> [, ...])] on hook <hook> [where <condition>] [comment '<text>'] [check <expr>] [scans] as <body>`, a parameter being `$name type from <path> [= <default>]`. The hook is its statement's: one name, one record, one text the digest covers, and `drop statement` takes both away. The hook's name is a word -- ASCII letters, digits and `_`, starting with a letter, at most 59 bytes, as a statement's -- a path segment with nothing to escape. Rejected:

- *A line of the policy, or a flag, naming a callable statement* (`stripe hook stripe_paid`, `--hook stripe=stripe_paid`), the event's fields mapped to its arguments there: the mapping -- which fields of the event a statement reads, the part of the API a sender's shape fixes -- would be configuration text outside the database, unchecked against its schema, left behind by a move and a restore, and the statement it names callable, so a token granted `stripe_paid call` marks orders paid with no payment. F1's paragraph put the secret beside the policy and the statement in the database, and the mapping is the statement's; F2 and F3 found the same for their bindings and schedules, a statement someone else's event runs being no statement a token calls.
- *The body reading the event's paths itself* (`set orders {...} where ref = event.data.object.metadata.order`), as F2's body reads `new.<field>`. A binding's `new.` is typed by its collection's schema; an event's paths are JSON, typed by nothing, and a text compared with an `int` field never matches: `id = event.data.object.metadata.order` over Stripe's metadata -- texts, always -- would refuse every payment at its guard (412) for the three days Stripe retries, where a parameter declared `int` refuses the first delivery naming the path and the type (400). So every value of the event a body reads goes in through a typed parameter, and `describe` shows what of the event a statement reads. In a body, `event.x` is then what it is in every statement: the path `x` into a field named `event`, checked against the collection's schema as any field is -- refused at definition where the collection has none, the refusal naming the parameter form the event reaches a body by. Rejected (the review's): *refusing `event.` and `hook.` in a body*, the first draft's, which would have kept a collection holding a `json` field called `event` -- a likely name in a collection of events -- from every hook's body, to guard a mistake its schema check already refuses.

**A parameter is F1's, read from the event rather than from a caller.** Its type is one a field's type names, read as a document's field of that type reads a body's value (`parse_documents_json`: a `json` one exact, a timestamp from its text or milliseconds) -- no cast, so a text holding digits is no `int` and a Unix time in seconds, Stripe's `created`, is no timestamp ([also left](#webhook-consumers-against-other-systems)). Its path is `event.` and a path into the body's JSON as [json paths](fennecql.md#json-fields-and-paths) read one -- keys cut by dots, at most `MAX_PATH_KEYS` (64), a key FennecQL does not write as a name quoted as a claim's is -- or `event` alone, the whole body, for a `json` parameter only (`$e json from event`: the raw event kept beside what it did, a body's whole bytes the definer chose to keep, personal data and all), or one of the three values the scheme reads beside the body: `hook.id`, the event's key ([below](#an-events-key)); `hook.at`, the timestamp its signature covers, `null` where the scheme signs none; `hook.event`, GitHub's `X-GitHub-Event`, `null` elsewhere. A path leading nowhere, or to `null`, is a missing value: refused (400, naming the parameter and the path) unless the parameter has a default, as an argument missing from a call is. At most 32, as F1's.

**The condition picks the events a statement takes.** `where` reads `event.<path>`, `hook.event` and literals alone -- no parameter, so a statement whose parameters would refuse an event never refuses one its condition passes over, and no field, the event being the one thing there is -- each path's JSON value compared as a `json` field's is. Without one a statement takes every event of its hook. A hook is consumed by at most 16 statements (`named::MAX_HOOKED`, refused past it, never cut, [crates.md](crates.md#limits)): every one whose condition an event passes takes it, in name order, in one block. Rejected ([decision 8](#f4s-decisions-for-the-owner)): *one statement a hook*. Stripe takes at most 16 event destinations an account ([Stripe](https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks)), and an app consuming `payment_intent.succeeded`, `charge.refunded`, `customer.subscription.deleted` and a dozen more through one would be out of endpoints before it was out of events; a statement a kind of event, one endpoint for them all, is the shape Stripe's own handlers have (`case event.type`).

**`check` holds the typed values**, as F1's does: `check $amount > 0` refuses (400) before any statement runs.

**The refusals the text alone answers**, each at definition, naming the form to write:

- what a statement no token calls has no use for: `claims` and `$jwt`, `public`;
- what another kind of statement's place is: `new.` and `old.`, `slot()`, `after` or a schedule beside `on hook` -- one statement, one way to run;
- what F2's and F3's bodies refuse for the same reason, their rows going nowhere: `returning`, a `get` without `require`, and a body of guards alone, which takes an event and changes nothing; a sender is no reader, and an answer naming rows would put them in another company's delivery log;
- a parameter in the condition (above).

The body is otherwise F1's ([definitions](#definitions)): at most 64 statements of `get` with `require`, `insert`, `insert ... if absent`, and `set` or `del` with a `where`, picked or not, reading through an index or saying `scans` -- never `put`, a parameter's documents, `else set`, a schema change, `call` or a `_` collection.

**`now()` is the event's taking, read once an event** for every statement it wakes and their bindings; the time the sender signed is `hook.at`, a parameter's to read.

### Verifying an event

**Before anything of an event is read, its signature is checked: the HMAC-SHA256 of what the scheme signs, under the hook's secret.** Three schemes, each a sender's own format:

| Scheme | The signature | What it signs | The event's key | A timestamp |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `stripe` | `Stripe-Signature: t=<seconds>,v1=<hex>[,v1=<hex>...]`, exactly one `t`, other schemes than `v1` ignored, "to prevent downgrade attacks" ([Stripe](https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks)) | `<t>.<body>` | the body's `id` (`evt_...`), signed with it | `t`, held to the tolerance |
| `github` | `X-Hub-Signature-256: sha256=<hex>`, "the HMAC hex digest of the request body" ([GitHub](https://docs.github.com/en/webhooks/webhook-events-and-payloads)); the SHA-1 `X-Hub-Signature` never read | the body | the body's SHA-256 (`key body`, the default) or `X-GitHub-Delivery` (`key delivery`), [below](#an-events-key) | none |
| `standard` | `webhook-signature: v1,<base64>[ v1,<base64>...]`, `v1a` (Ed25519) and others ignored ([Standard Webhooks](https://github.com/standard-webhooks/standard-webhooks/blob/main/spec/standard-webhooks.md)) | `<webhook-id>.<webhook-timestamp>.<body>` | `webhook-id`, holding no `.`, signed with it | `webhook-timestamp`, held to the tolerance |

Standard Webhooks is what Svix sends, and the services sending through it; a `standard` secret is the `whsec_`-prefixed base64 the sender shows, decoded, where Stripe's `whsec_` text and GitHub's chosen secret are their bytes as written.

**fennec-http has what it needs.** `crypto.rs` holds SHA-256, HMAC under a key whose padded blocks are hashed once (`HmacKey`, what a router checks the global entry's mark with), a constant-time comparison (`ct_eq`) and base64 in both alphabets, each held to RFC vectors, for SCRAM, HS256 JWTs and the entry's mark. A hook adds a hex reader and nothing else: no dependency, and none in fennec-core, which never sees a signature ([crates.md](crates.md#zero-dependencies)). A hook's `HmacKey`s are made once, as its secrets are read.

**Every refusal before a verified signature is one 401, after the same work.** The first draft asked the hook for its scheme, its tolerance and its ceiling before its secret and answered each failure in words of its own, so the answers listed a server's hooks to anyone: `POST /_hooks/github` carrying a `Stripe-Signature` alone was told the `X-Hub-Signature-256` it lacked where `/_hooks/nosuch` could not be, a `t` seven minutes old was told "the tolerance" by a hook of the default and "the signature" by one of ten minutes, and a body of 2 MiB was 413 from an unknown hook and 401, after the HMACs, from a GitHub hook whose ceiling was raised. Now the checks are these, and any failure is held to the end:

1. *The method*: `POST`, or 405 with `Allow: POST` -- every path under `/_hooks/` alike, so it says nothing of a name.
2. *The scheme, from the headers present*, never from the hook: `Stripe-Signature` is `stripe`, `X-Hub-Signature-256` `github`, `webhook-signature` with `webhook-id` and `webhook-timestamp` `standard`. Well formed: one scheme's headers, each sent once -- a header sent twice is a failure, since a reader signing with one `t` and holding another to the tolerance takes an old capture, with only `keep` between it and a second taking; `Stripe-Signature` holding exactly one `t`; a `webhook-id` holding no `.` and a `webhook-timestamp` of digits, as Standard Webhooks asks of the signed content's parts, so no two splits of it sign alike; at least one `v1` signature and at most eight, each in its encoding. Else a failure (`header`).
3. *The hook*, looked up in the server's memory -- the hooks file's on a file's server, the tenant's on a node ([below](#where-the-secret-lives)), never opening a tenant -- for its scheme, tolerance, ceiling and secrets. Where there is none -- no such hook, no such tenant, no secret -- the defaults stand in and a key of the process's own, made at its start, for each secret, and the work goes on all the same; a scheme other than the hook's is a failure (`scheme`) known here and answered at the end.
4. *The timestamp*, where the scheme signs one, within the tolerance of the server's clock either way, else a failure (`stale`). The tolerance is the hook's, five minutes unless its line says otherwise, Stripe's libraries' default, from a second to an hour: none is refused, as Stripe warns ("Don't use a tolerance value of `0`"), and past an hour a captured event verifies for longer than any sender's clock needs, its key kept the longer for it ([below](#an-events-key)).
5. *The body*, read up to the hook's ceiling -- a mebibyte unless its line says otherwise, at most `max_body` -- the read stopped there, a failure (`size`), the HMACs run over what was read. Standard Webhooks recommends events "usually smaller than 20kb"; GitHub's are "capped at 25 MB", which a hook of large pushes raises its ceiling for -- its one tell the time a body past the default takes to read, which its sender spends sending it.
6. *Two HMACs, always*, over what the detected scheme signs: under each of the hook's secrets, the process's key in place of a second it lacks, each compared with every `v1` signature by `ct_eq`, 32 bytes each. One matching, no failure before it, verifies the event.

Every failure is that one answer -- 401, one body ("the event does not verify"), no header naming a check -- after the same work, as F1 makes an ungranted statement and a missing one one 404 in one time ([authority](#authority)): a stale timestamp, a hook missing or a scheme not the hook's still reads the body to its ceiling and computes both HMACs. The check that failed is named only where an operator reads it: the log line, the audit's `refused` line (its `reason`) and `fennec_hook_unverified_total{check}` -- `header`, `scheme`, `stale`, `size`, `hook`, `signature` ([watching hooks](#watching-hooks)). A router in front tells a tenant's name already -- it answers 404 "no tenant `x` in the directory" before any check (`crates/fennec-shard/src/region.rs`), as for every route below `/t/<t>/` -- so behind one the single 401 keeps whether a known tenant has a hook, and of what scheme; on a node or a file's server reached directly it keeps the hooks' names and schemes as well. A server with no hooks file has no such route: its `/_hooks/` asks for a token as every unknown path does, which says it takes no hook at all and names none. Rejected (the review's (b), [decision 11](#f4s-decisions-for-the-owner)): *a hook's name and scheme not secret, its secret alone*, the first draft's answers kept and its claim of one 401 dropped -- each answer plainer in the sender's delivery log, but a server's hooks and their senders listed to anyone, where F1 tells a token no name it was not granted; the operator's words are in the log, under the request's id.

**A refused event waits by a count of the hooks' own, and a verified one clears no token's.** `audit::verified` is called where a token checks out ([http.md](http.md#audit)), and a signature is no token. A verified event from an address many share -- a cloud's egress, a sender's own ranges, and Svix lets an endpoint's owner add headers of its own, `Authorization` among them -- would start the token-guessing count again for every client there; and a hook's own holder, a tenant's GitHub admin who chose its secret, could send a signed event between guesses at another tenant's tokens. So a hook's 401 is logged as every 401 is (the audit's `refused` line) and waits by a count of its own, the token count's shape -- 100 ms, doubled for each more within a minute, 5 s at most, under the 10 s GitHub gives an answer ([GitHub](https://docs.github.com/en/webhooks/using-webhooks/best-practices-for-using-webhooks)) -- kept by the sender's address and the hook's path, behind the router by `Fennec-Client`, the router waiting out a node's 401 to a `/t/<t>/_hooks/` path by that count, read from the path it takes the tenant from already. A verified event of that hook from that address starts it again, and nothing else does: no token's verification touches it, and it touches no token's. Rejected ([decision 14](#f4s-decisions-for-the-owner)): *`audit::verified` on a verified event*, the first draft's, for the reasons above; *the token count itself*, which `audit::http` keeps for every 401 -- a hostile Stripe account pointing its endpoint at `/_hooks/stripe`, signing under its own secret, would raise the wait of everything refused from Stripe's addresses, a count no verified event may clear; *no wait at all*, since no one guesses a 256-bit HMAC online -- but GitHub's secret is its user's choice and may be a word, and one try every five seconds an address is what the token count gives a token.

Only once verified is the content type asked -- JSON's (`application/json`, its parameters aside), or 415 naming the type to set at the sender, GitHub's form-encoded deliveries (`payload=`) told so, a sender holding the secret alone -- and the body parsed, as an HTTP body is, exact (`parse_documents_json`), an object at most `MAX_JSON_DEPTH` deep, or 400; then the event's key read, the tenant opened and a lock taken. A forged event costs the server the body's read up to the ceiling and two HMACs, opens no tenant and takes no lock.

**Rotation is two secrets at once.** A hook's line names one or two secret files, each checked: Stripe "generates one signature for each secret" for up to 24 hours as one is rolled, Standard Webhooks sends a list, and GitHub signs with one -- the old until its settings change, the new from then -- so a server holding both while a sender changes over takes every event of the change. The second file is taken away once the sender's old secret has expired, by a restart: a server reads its hooks file once, at the start, as it reads its policy ([risks](#f4s-risks)). A second slot is for a rotation alone: Stripe's test and live modes sign with secrets of their own, and both in one hook's slots would leave none to rotate with and write test events where live ones go -- a mode is a hook of its own ([below](#stripes-events-as-stripe-sends-them)).

### An event's key

**An event's key is a value its signature covers, or the one header its hook was told to trust.** Stripe signs the body and its `id` with it; Standard Webhooks signs `webhook-id`, which "remains the same no matter how many times a webhook that has failed is retried". GitHub signs the body alone, and gives each delivery a GUID, `X-GitHub-Delivery`, which a redelivery keeps ("the same as in the original delivery") and which GitHub's guidance names as the defence against a replay ("you can use the `X-GitHub-Delivery` header to ensure that each delivery is unique per event", [best practices](https://docs.github.com/en/webhooks/using-webhooks/best-practices-for-using-webhooks)) -- but which its signature does not cover. The first draft keyed every `github` event by its body; its review found that it drops events GitHub sends twice alike, so a `github` hook's line chooses, by `key` ([decision 10](#f4s-decisions-for-the-owner)):

- *`key body`, the default*: the key is the SHA-256 of the body, which a redelivery repeats and no one changes without the secret, so a captured delivery sent again under another GUID -- and another `X-GitHub-Event` -- is answered from its key for as long as the hook's `keep`. Its cost: two deliveries whose bodies are the same byte for byte are one event, the second answered 200 from the first's key and never run. Most of GitHub's events differ -- a push names its commits, an issue's event its `updated_at` -- but two `repository_dispatch` or `workflow_dispatch` calls with one payload, the repository unchanged between them, carry no id of their own, and the second is dropped for the whole `keep`.
- *`key delivery`*: the key is `X-GitHub-Delivery`, GitHub's guidance, for a hook whose events repeat byte for byte. Its cost: whoever holds one signed delivery -- TLS broken, a proxy's or a log's copy -- sends it again under another GUID and has it taken again, at any time, the scheme signing no timestamp; a hook keyed so has statements that do no harm taken twice.

The reference page names both costs where the hook's line is written; either way the GUID is logged beside the key, for the reader of GitHub's delivery log, and a `key` on another scheme's line is refused at the start. Rejected: *one key for every `github` hook*, either one -- `body` drops the dispatches a workflow repeats, `delivery` takes a captured body again on every hook, and only a hook's own events say which costs less. A key is 1 to 255 bytes with no control character, as an `Idempotency-Key` is (400 otherwise), and is kept as `<hook>`, `U+001F` and the key, so one event sent to two hooks is taken once by each.

**A key outlives the window its signature verifies in.** A signed timestamp verifies within the tolerance either side of the server's clock, so an event signed at `t` may be taken as early as `t - tolerance` and a copy of it verifies until `t + tolerance`: a key kept no longer than twice the tolerance after its taking lets a copy inside the window be taken again. So a hook's `keep` must be more than twice its tolerance plus `SKEW`, a minute for the clocks of the nodes a failover moves it between, refused at the start and at a node's `PUT` otherwise -- the first draft refused only a `keep` shorter than the tolerance, which left the window's second half open. With the tolerance at most an hour, the floor of a day holds it for every tolerance allowed; the rule is stated for whatever the floor becomes. A `github` hook, whose scheme signs no time, is bounded by its `keep` alone. The defaults ([decision 6](#f4s-decisions-for-the-owner)): thirty days for `stripe`, the window in which Stripe's CLI resends an event, and seven for the others; a resend after `keep` is taken again, its statements' guards deciding what it writes, as the reference page says in those words.

### Where the secret lives

**Never in the database: a hooks file's on a server, a tenant's file beside its database on a node.** A database is replicated, archived, copied into a bucket that is never pruned (`rclone copy`, never `sync`, [integrations.md](integrations.md#deploy)), opened in a page and moved; a credential in it would be in every copy for as long as the copies last, and one leaked from any of them is a sender's identity to every database holding it. The secret is the deployment's, as a token is.

- *A file's server* reads `--hooks <path>`, a line a hook -- its name, its scheme, one or two secret files, and optionally `keep`, `tolerance` and, for `github`, `key` (above) -- at the start, as it reads `--policy`; each secret is a file, as `--key-file` and `--jwt-secret-file` are, so no secret is on a command line `ps` shows, and `deploy/`'s units hand it over by `LoadCredential=`. Refused at the start: a secret file empty ([access.md](access.md#empty-tokens): an empty secret would make every signature anyone can compute verify) or holding a scheme's secret malformed (a `standard` one not `whsec_` and base64), an unknown scheme, a hook named twice, a tolerance of none or past an hour, a `keep` shorter than a day or not more than twice the tolerance and `SKEW`, a `key` on a hook not `github`'s. A hooks file needs no `--policy` and no token: a server whose clients hold its token, or that answers only the shell, may still take Stripe's events. A hook no statement consumes, and a statement consuming a hook the file does not name, are logged at the start and as each is defined, as a `call` line naming no statement is.
- *A tenant node* keeps each tenant's hooks in `<t>.hooks` beside its file: the same lines with the secrets in them, written beside and renamed, readable by the node's user alone, set by `PUT /_admin/tenants/<t>/hooks/<name>` (`{"scheme", "secrets", "keep", "tolerance", "key"}`) and taken away by `DELETE`, each with the node's admin token. The `PUT` refuses (400) what a hooks file is refused at the start -- an empty secret above all, which would arm a hook every forger signs for, and a malformed one -- naming the field, never its value. `GET /_admin/tenants/<t>/hooks` lists names, schemes, `keep`, `tolerance`, `key` and when each secret was set, never a secret nor anything computed from one -- a fingerprint of a secret GitHub lets its user choose is a test of every guess. The node reads them as it starts and as each is set, kept in its memory beside the registry, never in a tenant's `Database`: verifying opens no tenant.

**A tenant's `<t>.hooks` is bound to its file, as `.instance` is.** The first draft kept it "as `<t>.quota` is kept", and `.quota` goes best-effort, after the file, its error passed over (`tenants.rs`); nothing took `<t>.hooks` away at a delete, a move's source's deletion or a reclaim, or looked for one left as a create or an install made a file beside it. Its review's case: tenant `acme` is deleted or moved off a node, its `acme.hooks` left by a failed remove, a crash between the two removals or a path that never removed it, its owner's Stripe endpoint still naming `/t/acme/_hooks/stripe`; another customer later makes `acme` there and defines `on hook stripe`, the node reads the file left as it starts, and the first owner's payments verify and run in the second's database. So `<t>.hooks` takes `.instance`'s binding ([crates/fennec-http/src/instance.rs](../../crates/fennec-http/src/instance.rs)): its first line names the file it was written for -- its device, inode and birth time -- and every way of fennec's that makes, removes or replaces a tenant's file puts it away first, synced: a create, an install, a follow, a delete, a reclaim, `fennec restore` and `backup --force` (`put_away_beside`). One naming another file is never adopted -- not read as the tenant's, logged and put away -- and the node's own renames (a `compact`, a checkpoint, a follower taking its primary's image) carry the binding to the file they made, as `.instance`'s close does. A promotion keeps it: the copy promoted holds the set its router sent it, bound to the file that is now the primary. A follow drops it -- put away before `.follows` is written -- and the router, whose directory holds the set's version, sends the current one to the follower once it follows (below), so a copy never serves a set it was not sent for the file it holds. Left, as for `.instance`: a file replaced by hand, or by a binary from before, the sidecar kept, on a file system keeping no birth time -- the operator removes `<t>.hooks` with the file. Rejected: *`<t>.hooks` removed before the file at every delete*, which closes a delete's window and no other -- a binary from before, a hand, a path not yet written making a file beside one left -- where the binding refuses a leftover however it was left.

**One route reads a tenant's secrets, and one token asks it.** `GET /_admin/tenants/<t>/hook-secrets` answers the tenant's set whole with its version, for the router to copy onto a copy that lacks it; `PUT` on the same path installs a set whole, refused as a hook's `PUT` is. The node's admin token alone asks either -- a data, a scoped or a replication token never -- and whoever holds it reads every tenant's secrets on that node, as it may already delete every tenant; the router holds that token, and the deployment keeps it as it keeps the router's own. A path of its own rather than a flag of the listing, so no listing answers a secret by a parameter left on; answered `Cache-Control: no-store`; its body is in no log line, span, slow line or audit field -- the audit line it writes names the tenant and the router's request id alone -- and the router holds it in memory only until the copy's `PUT` is answered.

**The router carries a tenant's secrets and keeps none** ([below](#hooks-on-tenant-nodes-the-router-and-the-global-entry)). Its directory records each tenant's hook names and a version, so a standby, a moved copy and a node a failed attach is tried again on are known to lack the current set; the secrets themselves go node to node through its admin channel and are in its memory only while they pass. Since it keeps none, a standby promoted without the current set cannot be given it once its primary is lost: so a `PUT` is answered only once the primary and every standby hold its version, a copy that does not take it named in the answer and counted (`fennec_router_tenants_hooks_behind`, as `fennec_router_tenants_unreplicated` counts a copy missing) until the router's repair copies it, and a `PUT` is ordered against a running move by the tenant's claim ([below](#hooks-on-tenant-nodes-the-router-and-the-global-entry)).

**Never in a log, a trace, an answer or a description.** No line, span, metric, audit event, `describe`, `/_schema`, listing or studio view holds a secret, a signature or an event's body, the export's answer above being the one answer that holds a secret; the admin's `PUT` is an audit event naming the tenant and the hook alone. A restore from an archive or a backup brings the statements back and no secret, putting away a `<t>.hooks` left beside the file it replaces: each of the tenant's hooks answers 401 until its secret is set again, which the node logs as the tenant opens ("hook `stripe`: consumed by 2 statements, no secret").

Rejected ([decision 2](#f4s-decisions-for-the-owner)): *the secret sealed in the database*, under a key every node of a cluster holds (`seal.rs`'s ChaCha20-Poly1305, [replication.md](replication.md#sealed-archives)). A move, a failover and a restore would carry the hooks with the data, the one thing the files beside it make harder; but every archive ever written would hold every tenant's secrets under one key, and archives are never pruned: a cluster key leaked in a year opens every secret of every tenant back to the first archive, and rotating it reaches no archive already written. *The router's directory*: a fennecdb file too, replicated to the standby router and archived. *A FennecQL statement setting it* (`alter hook stripe secret '...'`): the text would reach the slow log, the audit's schema line and `/_stats/statements`' shapes before it reached the file.

### Where an event is taken, and once

**On the server that may write the file, under the write lock, its key and its writes one block.** Once verified and parsed:

1. *Before the write lock*, under a short read lock: the key looked up in `_hook_events` -- a duplicate, a retry whose first try's answer was lost among them, is answered from it once its block is as durable and sent as the first try's answer was to be ([below](#a-replay-waits-for-what-its-first-try-waited-for)), taking no write lock -- and the hook's statements read from the table (`Arc`, as a call's definition is, [calls](#calls)), each condition asked and the parameters of each statement taking the event typed, its `check` asked. A hook no statement consumes is 409 ("no statement takes hook `stripe`"); one with a statement unread, 409 naming the hook, and an event taken by a statement broken, 409 naming the hook (below); an event no condition passes, 200 `{"taken": 0}`, nothing written.
2. *Under the write lock*, the key looked up again -- a first try still running when a retry overlapped it, Stripe's after a slow answer, has landed by now, and is answered as step 1 answers it, after the lock -- and the table held to be the same `Arc` (`Arc::ptr_eq`), read again otherwise; then a block: each taking statement's body in name order through `run_one`, as a call's body runs (`Database::call`), each seeing what the ones before it wrote, and the key's row kept last (`Database::take_event`), so the block lands with every write the event made and its key, or with none.
3. *After the lock*, durable whatever `--sync` says, and held by a standby where [decision 4](#f4s-decisions-for-the-owner)'s recommendation is taken ([below](#what-a-sender-is-told)), then the lease asked again (`answered_under_lease`), then the answer.

**The key is the `Idempotency-Key`'s machinery, in a collection of its own.** `idempotent.rs` keeps a write's key and answer in the write's block under the write lock, answers a second request from it and keeps none for a failed write ([http.md](http.md#idempotency-keys)); a hook's key is that, with three differences. *Its collection*, `_hook_events`, whose rows hold their own expiry (`until timestamp @ttl(1ms)`): `_idempotency`'s rows expire `--idempotency-ttl` after they were kept, a day by default, one duration a collection, and a hook's keys must outlive its sender's retries -- Stripe "attempts to deliver events to your destination for up to three days", Svix over about 28 hours before it stops ([Svix](https://docs.svix.com/retries)) -- each hook by its own `keep`, thirty days for `stripe` and seven for the others unless its line says otherwise ([above](#an-events-key), [decision 6](#f4s-decisions-for-the-owner)). It is a server's own collection (`declared::own`), held out of every surface `_idempotency` is -- listings, `/_schema`, the studio, a `*` rule, a statement's body, MCP, the tenant's row quota (`Database::app_writes`) -- most by its `_`, `/_changes` by its name beside `idempotent::KEYS` (`cdc.rs`). *Its owner*, the hook rather than a token's subject. *A second body under a held key*, which `_idempotency` answers 422: an event's key is the sender's own name for it, signed, so a second body under it comes from the holder of the secret, and may be the same event lawfully -- Stripe renders an event in the API version of the destination it goes to, so an endpoint upgraded between a delivery and a resend gets the same `id` with another body, the one payment it was -- or a sender's bug; answered 422 it would be retried until the key expired and then taken, a payment taken twice days later. It is answered as the first was, logged and counted (`outcome="conflict"`), and never run ([decision 7](#f4s-decisions-for-the-owner)).

Rejected: *the key as a kind 13 record*, as F3's marks are. A mark replaces the one before it, one live a statement; a key a hook would be one an event, kept for days and then let go, which only a row's `@ttl` does.

**A statement that cannot run refuses the events it would have taken, and an unread one every event of its hook.** A statement that cannot run is not run, and not passed over either: an event it would have taken answered `taken: 0` is a 2xx a sender never sends again. How far its refusal reaches is how far its condition can be trusted. *Broken by its body* -- a schema change by an internal writer breaking what the body reads or writes (`depends`) -- its condition still reads: a condition reads `event.`, `hook.event` and literals alone, which no schema change touches, so it is asked as ever, and only the events it takes are 409, naming the hook ("statement `stripe_paid`, which takes hook `stripe`, is broken: define it again or drop it"), the hook's other events taken by its other statements as before. *Unread* -- a text a later grammar reads otherwise, one a process with no reader holds -- its condition is unknown, so every event of its hook is 409. Either way as F2's broken binding refuses the writes it binds ([when a binding refuses](#when-a-binding-refuses)), and the sender's retries keep the events until it is mended. The first draft refused every event of the hook for a statement broken either way; its review narrowed the first case, a refusal reaching no event the condition would have passed over. The hook's name is outside the text in the record ([below](#a-hooks-record-replicas-and-upgrades)) for the second: a server that cannot read a statement still knows which hook to refuse.

**After a failover the promoted copy holds an event's writes and its key, or neither.** A node lands an event's block under its lease; then:

| What happened | The sender was told | The promoted copy holds | A resend |
|---|---|---|---|
| the stream sent the block before the node was lost | 200 | the writes and the key | answered from the key |
| the lease lapsed while the event held the lock | 503, the fence refusing the block | nothing | taken, once |
| the block landed, the lease lapsed before the answer | 500 `outcome: unknown` ([tenants.md](tenants.md#scaling-out-by-tenant)) | the block or not, by the stream | answered from the key, or taken once |
| a crash before the fsync | nothing: the answer waits for it | nothing | taken, once |
| a duplicate came while the first try's block was in its fsync or the streams' wait, and the node was lost | nothing: the replay waits as the first try does ([below](#a-replay-waits-for-what-its-first-try-waited-for)) | nothing | taken, once |
| the block landed, synced, and its stream lagged past `SENT_WAIT`, not waited for | 200; 503 with `Retry-After` under [decision 4](#f4s-decisions-for-the-owner)'s recommendation | nothing | none where told 200; taken, once, where told 503 |

The last row is the one way a told event is lost as the first draft had it, the window every durable write has with a lagging standby ([replication.md](replication.md#replication)); `outcome="unknown"` counts its neighbour, and a sender's dashboard resends it (Stripe for 15 days from its Dashboard, 30 from its CLI), which its key then lets run once. Decision 4's recommendation closes it at a cost in latency alone: where the tenant has a standby, a hook's 2xx waits for one to hold the event ([below](#what-a-sender-is-told)), and a 503 the sender retries replaces the 200 that a failover would make a lie. A move is the freeze before the export: a hook to a frozen tenant is 503 with `Retry-After`, its sender comes back after the flip, and the target holds every key the source kept.

### A replay waits for what its first try waited for

**A duplicate is answered only once the record holding its key is as durable and sent as the first try's answer was to be.** The first draft answered it as soon as the key's row was there to read, and the row is there before the first try's answer may be sent: the first try lands its block under the write lock and makes it durable after letting the lock go -- its fsync, the streams' `Feed::wait_sent` up to `SENT_WAIT`. Its review's case: Stripe sends `evt_1`, and a duplicate delivery, or the router replaying a cut connection's request as keyed, reaches the node while the first try is in its fsync or waiting on a lagging standby; the duplicate finds the key, under either lock, and is answered 200 `Idempotent-Replayed`; the machine loses power, or the node is failed over to the lagging standby, and Stripe holds a 200 for a payment no copy has, which it never sends again. So a replay waits for the key's block as the first try does: durable, whatever `--sync` says, and sent to the streams the first try waits for -- and to a standby holding it, where [decision 4](#f4s-decisions-for-the-owner)'s recommendation is taken -- before its answer goes out, no new fsync made where one under way or done covers it.

**The same gap is `Idempotency-Key`'s today, and its fix comes first.** `rest_write`, `keyed_query` and a call's path answer `idempotent::answered` with no wait for durability or the streams, and the router replays a keyed request on the word that a replay is safe. Its fix -- a durable position the read side can wait on, the branch `idempotent-replay-durable`, since `Database::flush` takes `&mut` and clears its dirty mark before the fsync runs, so no flag read beside it says what the disk holds -- is a pull request of its own, before F4; slice 3 waits on it and builds on it, a hook's replay the second caller of that wait ([F4 in slices](#f4-in-slices)). Rejected: *a write lock taken by a replay to flush*, which a burst of Stripe's retries would turn into one writer's queue, when a position read beside the readers is enough.

### What a sender is told

**A sender is told 2xx once its event is durable, or once resending it could change nothing; everything else is the refusal's status, so the sender's retries are the queue.** Stripe retries any status but a 2xx for three days in live mode, Svix on its schedule and then disables the endpoint after five days of failures, Standard Webhooks counts "a `2xx` status code" alone as delivered; GitHub "does not automatically redeliver failed deliveries", and its delivery log is where an operator redelivers one. So a sender holds every event fennecdb has not taken, and redelivers it on its own schedule: a guard unmet now -- `payment_intent.succeeded` before the order's row, since "Stripe doesn't guarantee the delivery of events in the order that they're generated" -- is met at a retry, and a statement mended within the sender's window takes every event refused meanwhile. A guard says *not yet*; a filter says *not this one*: `set orders ... where status = 'open'` with no `require` takes an event for an order already paid as a write of no row, 200, where `require 1` is told back until it finds one.

| Answer | Status |
|---|---|
| not a `POST` | 405, `Allow: POST`, on every path alike |
| before a verified signature: the scheme's headers missing, malformed or sent twice; a scheme not the hook's; the timestamp outside the tolerance; the body past the hook's ceiling; no such tenant or hook, no secret; a signature that does not verify | 401 "the event does not verify", one answer after the same work, its check in the log alone ([above](#verifying-an-event)) |
| verified, not JSON's content type | 415, naming the type to set |
| a tenant frozen for a move, its lease out; under [decision 4](#f4s-decisions-for-the-owner)'s recommendation, no standby holding the event in time | 503, `Retry-After` |
| a read-only server, a replica, a marked request | 403 |
| the body not a JSON object; a key missing, past 255 bytes or holding a control character | 400 |
| the key held: a retry, a duplicate | as the first was answered, once its block is as durable and sent as the first's answer was to be, `Idempotent-Replayed: true` |
