The router's `PUT` takes the tenant's claim -- a running move refuses it -- and is answered once the primary and every standby hold its version, a copy behind named and counted (`fennec_router_tenants_hooks_behind`); its directory holds names and versions, never a secret.

And *The router looks at a pooled connection first* gains that `replayable` and the entry's keyed rule take a `POST` to `/t/<t>/_hooks/` as keyed.

### F4's decisions for the owner

Taken as recommended, after review, under the owner's word to go on with the recommendations (2026-11-03): 2, 4, 6, 7 and 8 gained what the review found, marked *revised*, and 10 to 14 are the decisions it found missing, marked *new*; each line's alternative is what the owner can still choose instead, before the slice it bites in.

1. **The form.** *Taken: a clause of `create statement`* -- `on hook <name>`, typed parameters `from event.<path>`, a condition choosing the events -- one record, one text, a statement no token calls. The alternative, a line of the policy or a flag naming a callable statement with the mapping beside it, keeps the mapping out of the database and lets a token granted the statement make the events it stands for.
2. **Where the secret lives** (revised). *Taken: a hooks file of secret files on a server, `<t>.hooks` beside a tenant's file on a node, relayed by the router, which keeps none* -- an archive holds no credential, and a restore asks for the secret again -- *with `<t>.hooks` bound to its file as `.instance` is, one route exporting a set to the node's admin token alone, and the router's `PUT` answered once every standby holds it*, so no leftover arms a later tenant of the name and no promoted copy lacks a set unseen. The alternative, the secret sealed in the database under a cluster key, lets a move, a failover and a restore carry it, at the price of every archive ever written holding every tenant's secrets under one key.
3. **What a refused event is answered.** *Taken: its status*, so the sender's retries are the queue -- a guard waits for its row, a statement mended takes what was refused meanwhile, and nothing of an untaken event is kept. The alternative, 2xx for every verified event and the refused ones kept in a collection to drain, keeps bodies -- personal data, payment details -- the definer never chose to keep, in a second queue an operator must watch.
4. **The 2xx's durability** (revised). *Taken: durable whatever `--sync` says*, its fsync and streams before the answer, *a replay answered no sooner than its first try* (B1, on `Idempotency-Key`'s fix), *and, where the tenant has a standby, the 2xx waiting for one to hold the event*, bounded by `SENT_WAIT`, else 503 with `Retry-After`: the sender's retries are the queue, so the wait closes the failover table's one "told and lost" row at a cost in latency alone, and with every standby gone in 503s the sender retries until one is attached. The alternatives: `--sync`'s own, which answers sooner and loses, in a crash, events their senders will not send again; or durable without the standby's wait, the first draft's, which a failover to a lagging standby makes a 2xx for a lost event.
5. **The schemes.** *Taken: `stripe`, `github` and `standard`*, the three most senders use, Standard Webhooks covering Svix's; a `stripe` hook takes Snapshot events (13). The alternative adds a generic one naming a header and an encoding now, each sender's quirks -- an unsigned id, a prefix, a timestamp of its own -- configuration rather than code.
6. **How long a key is kept** (revised). *Taken: thirty days for `stripe`, the window its CLI resends an event in and its volumes low, seven for the others, past Svix's last retry 28 hours after its first; every `keep` more than twice the tolerance and `SKEW`, the tolerance at most an hour*, and the reference page saying plainly that a resend after `keep` -- Stripe's Dashboard's up to 15 days, its CLI's up to 30, Svix's manual retry "at any time" -- is taken again, its guards deciding. The alternatives: seven days for every hook, the first draft's, under which a Stripe resend of a week-old event takes it twice; thirty for every hook, four times the keys of senders that resend nothing so late.
7. **A second body under a key taken** (revised). *Taken: answered as the first, never run, logged and counted* -- Stripe renders an event in the API version of the destination it goes to, so the same `id` can come with another body lawfully, the same payment, and running it would take it twice. The alternative, `_idempotency`'s 422, has the sender retry it until the key expires, and then takes it -- a second body under an event's name, days later.
8. **Several statements a hook** (revised). *Taken: up to 16, each its condition, in name order, one block* -- coupled: one statement's guard, typing error or `@unique` clash refuses the event for every statement taking it, and an unread statement holds every event of its hook (a statement broken by its body, only those its condition takes), the price of an event taken once and whole. The alternatives: one a hook, which makes an endpoint a kind of event and caps a Stripe account's app at its 16 destinations; or a block and a key each statement, which uncouples them and lets an event be half taken, a payment marked and its ledger line refused.
9. **A hook no statement consumes.** *Taken: 409*, which a sender retries and shows -- the endpoint configured and the statement forgotten is loud. The alternative, 200 `{"taken": 0}` as for an event no condition takes, answers a hook whose statement was dropped as if all were well.
10. **GitHub's key** (new, B4). *Taken: per hook, `key body | delivery`, default `body`*: the signature covers the body, so a captured delivery cannot be sent again under a new GUID, at the cost of two byte-identical deliveries -- a `repository_dispatch` or `workflow_dispatch` repeated -- taken once; `delivery`, GitHub's own guidance, for a hook whose events repeat byte for byte, at the cost of a captured delivery taken again under another GUID, at any time. Each cost named here and on the reference page. The alternatives: `body` for every hook, the first draft's; `delivery` for every hook.
11. **The answers before a verified signature** (new, B3). *Taken: (a), one 401 for every refusal*, the scheme read from the headers present, the hook's tolerance and ceiling applied, two HMACs always, the check named in the log, the audit line and `fennec_hook_unverified_total` alone, as F1 makes a missing statement and an ungranted one one 404 -- keeping a hook's existence and scheme behind a router, and its name on a node reached directly; a router's 404 tells a tenant's name already. The alternative, (b): the first draft's answers kept and their claim dropped, a hook's name and scheme not secret, its secret alone, each refusal plainer in a sender's delivery log.
12. **Who sets a tenant's secrets in the hosted product** (new). *Taken: the router's admin alone, for now*, through `/_shard/tenants/<t>/hooks`, a tenant's own admin's route left for a proposal of its own. The alternative, a route a tenant's token sets its own hooks by -- likely the hosted product's main path -- is a new authority, a tenant writing a credential that runs statements in its database, which owes its own token, rate and audit before it is built.
13. **Stripe's thin events** (new). *Taken: left*: a thin event carries no object, and reading one is a fetch from Stripe's API under a key of the app's, a call out a hook never makes, under the write lock or before it. The alternative, a fetch before the lock, holds a second credential and makes Stripe's API's outages a hook's refusals.
14. **Whether a hook's verification clears the token-refusal count** (new). *Taken: no*: a hook's refusals wait by a count of their own, by the sender's address and the hook's path, which its verified events clear and no token's verification touches -- a verified event from an address many share must not reset the brake on guessing tokens there, nor a hook's holder clear its own guesses with signed events. The alternatives: `audit::verified` on a verified event, the first draft's; or no wait for a hook's refusals, which leaves a GitHub secret its user chose open to online guessing at the round trip's pace.
